NACOR Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (explain that a current prescription is required and refer for an examination): opticianry is a dispensing profession, so an optician acts on a prescription written by an optometrist or an ophthalmologist and never creates or alters one. Raising the sphere power because the patient reports blur is prescribing, and that sits outside entry level registration in every participating province. Neutralizing the existing lenses tells you only what she is wearing now, which is useful for a service record but is not a prescription. Supplying the old power unchanged also leaves a four year old refraction and an unassessed visual complaint in place.
  2. The correct answer is B (report what you measured and recommend an examination without naming a cause): an optician may report neutral, factual observations and must refer, but may never attach a diagnosis to a patient's symptoms. Telling him that haze at his age is an early cataract is a diagnosis and is outside scope, even when the guess is statistically likely. Reassuring him that his lenses are correct treats a lens check as an eye health check and delays care he may need. Selling a coating in response to a reported change in vision substitutes a product for the referral the presentation calls for.
  3. The correct answer is D (a separate provincial authorization with added education and assessment is required): sight testing or automated refraction by an optician exists in several provinces, but only under a distinct authorization with its own education, assessment, and practice conditions. The national eyeglasses examination assesses entry to practice dispensing competence and confers no refracting authority anywhere. Health professions in Canada are regulated provincially, so no federal grant of refracting authority exists. Having an optometrist review a result afterward does not create authority the optician never held in the first place.
  4. The correct answer is A (optometrists and ophthalmologists both write prescriptions; opticians interpret and dispense them): an optometrist examines, refracts, diagnoses ocular disease, and prescribes, while an ophthalmologist is a physician who manages medical and surgical eye disease and also writes spectacle prescriptions. The optician interprets that prescription and turns it into eyewear through frame selection, measurement, ordering, verification, dispensing, and service. Optometrists do far more than screening, so limiting prescribing to ophthalmologists is wrong. Opticians never write prescriptions and never modify a prescribed power in response to a patient's report of blur.
  5. The correct answer is B (NACOR develops and scores the examination; the provincial regulator grants registration): the National Alliance of Canadian Optician Regulators is an alliance of provincial regulators that builds and administers the national examinations so one entry standard applies across participating jurisdictions. It does not issue licences, hold registrations, or conduct discipline, so any statement that it licenses or registers opticians is wrong. A passing result is one requirement among several, alongside education, practical hours, a jurisprudence component, and good character declarations. Continuing education is also a regulator requirement rather than something separated away from licensing.
  6. The correct answer is D (supervised practical hours, a jurisprudence component, and character declarations): provincial regulators use the national examination result as one requirement and normally add practical experience, a jurisprudence assessment on their own legislation and standards, and declarations about character and conduct. There is no second national sitting to clear, and criminal record screening where used is a regulator requirement rather than a federal registration step. NACOR issues no practice permit of any kind, since it is not a licensing body. Liability coverage may be an employment or regulator condition, but it is not the substantive gap after an examination pass.
  7. The correct answer is A (Quebec sits outside the NACOR system and runs its own examination): Quebec opticians are regulated by the Ordre des opticiens d'ordonnances du Quebec, which sets its own examination and its own entry requirements. A national examination pass does not by itself grant entry to practice there, so treating it as transferable with a language add on is wrong. Quebec certainly has a regulator, and an Ontario registration has no legal force inside another province's jurisdiction. Adding a fixed supervised practice period to a NACOR pass also misdescribes a system that Quebec does not participate in at all.
  8. The correct answer is C (it supports registration in the new province on the regulator's own terms): labour mobility means a licensed optician in good standing in one participating province can generally be registered in another without repeating the full entry pathway, but the receiving regulator still sets and applies its own conditions. A licence does not travel automatically, so it is not valid in another province before that province registers her. There is no short placement exemption that removes the need to register with the regulator whose jurisdiction she is practising in. NACOR assesses no applications, because registration decisions belong to the provincial regulator.
  9. The correct answer is A (the same reasonable skill and attention owed to any paying patient): duty of care attaches to the professional relationship, not to the size of the sale, so a free screw tightening is owed the same competence as a premium progressive package. Making the repair conditional on a frame consultation converts a professional obligation into a sales lever. Sending her back to the competing dispensary for a two minute service is a refusal of care dressed as a referral. Framing service as discretionary because no contract of sale exists confuses commercial obligation with professional obligation, and only the second one governs here.
  10. The correct answer is D (whether a reasonable person could believe financial interest drove the choice): the standard test for conflict of interest is a perception test applied by a reasonable observer, and if the answer is yes the arrangement must be disclosed or declined. Whether patients later complained is irrelevant, since a conflict exists at the moment of the recommendation, not when harm surfaces. Comparing the commission with the dispensary's usual margin measures profitability rather than the influence on clinical judgement. Employer approval of a commission structure does not cure a conflict, because the duty runs to the patient rather than to the employer.
  11. The correct answer is B (decline it, since accepting an inducement to steer patients invites discipline): accepting payment to direct patients toward a particular practitioner or supplier is treated as a discipline matter in most jurisdictions, and the request to keep it secret confirms it cannot survive disclosure. Proximity of the clinic does not launder the payment, because the money still drives the referral rather than the patient's need. Telling the employer while hiding it from patients discloses to the wrong party entirely. Donating the money afterward changes nothing about the incentive that shaped the referral at the time it was made.
  12. The correct answer is C (the adaptation promise, because a guarantee of outcome misleads patients): advertising must be truthful and verifiable, and a promise that a lens will eliminate any adaptation problem guarantees a clinical result that no dispenser can deliver. Publishing hours of business is ordinary factual information and creates no professional problem. Stating staff credentials is permitted and is a proper use of a protected title by those entitled to hold it. Naming frame brands the dispensary actually stocks is factual product information, and it is the undisclosed steering of patients rather than the naming that creates a supplier conflict.
  13. The correct answer is D (it lets the public infer that the opticians perform eye examinations): allowing the public to believe an optician examines eyes misrepresents scope and is a serious violation, particularly where the store staff are opticians only. Clinical service advertising is not banned outright across Canada, so a blanket prohibition misstates the rule. There is no general requirement that the individual optometrist be named on signage. Prior written approval of every sign is not the mechanism regulators use, since the obligation is on the advertiser to keep claims truthful and free of misleading inference at all times.
  14. The correct answer is A (servicing family without records and taking gifts blur professional boundaries): professional boundaries cover treating family without proper documentation, accepting significant gifts, and social contact that erodes the professional line, and this scenario contains several of those at once. The discount itself is not the core issue, since fees may vary; the absent record and the gift are what create the risk. Records are not optional for family members, because the same standard of documentation applies to every patient. A gift routed through the patient's employer is still an inducement connected to the professional relationship.
  15. The correct answer is B (the adaptation period, the soft peripheral zones, and the narrower corridor): informed consent means disclosing what the patient will actually experience, and a first time progressive wearer in a short fitting height frame will meet all three of those effects. Warranty terms, coating choices, and delivery dates are commercial details rather than the clinical limitations that shape the decision. Base curve, index, and edge thickness are lens construction values the optician resolves, and they do not describe how the lens will perform for her. Frame material, hinge type, and cleaning advice belong to handover rather than to the consent conversation.
  16. The correct answer is C (increased chromatic aberration and higher reflectance than a standard material): high index materials have lower Abbe values, so colour fringing at the periphery is more noticeable, and their higher refractive index increases surface reflection, which is why an anti reflective treatment is normally discussed alongside them. High index materials do not reduce ultraviolet protection, and they have no effect on the working life of the frame. There is no requirement to replace the lenses annually, and impact performance depends on the specific material rather than being lost outright. Field of view and fitting height are governed by the lens design and the frame, not by the index chosen.
  17. The correct answer is A (a substitute decision maker consents for a minor, subject to mature minor rules): consent for a minor normally comes from a parent or guardian, with provincial mature minor rules deciding when a young person may consent for themselves. Holding a valid prescription says nothing about who is legally able to agree to the service, since capacity and authority are separate from the prescription. The prescriber consents to nothing on the patient's behalf, because consent is obtained by the practitioner performing the service. Payment is a commercial act and never substitutes for valid consent from the right decision maker.
  18. The correct answer is D (contact the patient, explain the change, and obtain consent before proceeding): consent is a process tied to a specific plan, so a change of material with a different appearance and price is a new decision that requires fresh agreement and a note in the record. Authorizing the substitution and explaining it at collection presents the patient with a completed job they never agreed to. Cancelling the order outright removes a choice the patient may well want to make. Absorbing the price difference addresses only the money and still delivers a product the patient did not select.
  19. The correct answer is C (arrange a competent interpreter before discussing the cost and the risks): valid consent requires that the patient actually understand the options, the limitations, and the cost, which cannot happen through a relative guessing at optical terminology. Relying on the son because he knows her well confuses familiarity with accurate interpretation of technical and financial information. A signature on a written list proves only that a form was signed, not that its content was understood. Choosing the simpler lens for her removes her decision entirely and still leaves cost and limitations undiscussed.
  20. The correct answer is B (manual equipment may be brought only if authorized; automated equipment is barred): candidates may bring their own manual instruments where the moderator has authorized them in advance, while automated equipment is not permitted at all. Equipment is randomly assigned rather than selected, so candidates should practise on whatever instrument is put in front of them. A blanket prohibition on all personal equipment overstates the rule, and simply declaring an instrument on arrival is not the same as prior authorization. Familiarization time is provided before each section's timer starts so the assigned instrument can be checked.
  21. The correct answer is D (consent for the new purpose, since use is limited to the original purpose): privacy law limits use and disclosure to the purpose for which the information was collected, and marketing is a new purpose that requires fresh consent. Lawful collection at the outset does not authorize a later disclosure that patients never agreed to. Stripping names from a file that still contains prescriptions and dates of birth leaves identifiable health information, so it is not a safe workaround. Provincial regulators oversee professional conduct and do not issue permissions for the sharing of patient lists.
  22. The correct answer is A (provide access to her own information, since no reason needs to be given): a core privacy principle is that individuals may access their own personal health information on request, and they are not required to justify why they want it. Withholding internal notes treats the record as the dispensary's property when the information in it is the patient's. Demanding a written reason plus regulator approval invents a barrier that no privacy statute imposes. Routing her own file to her optometrist rather than to her denies her the direct access the legislation exists to protect.
  23. The correct answer is C (draw one line through it, add the correction, the date, and initials): a defensible record keeps the original entry readable while showing clearly what was changed, when, and by whom. Erasing the figure destroys the original entry and makes the record look altered rather than corrected. Correction fluid does the same thing more obviously, and rewriting a whole page replaces contemporaneous entries with a later reconstruction. Leaving the wrong height in place and putting the correct one on a separate sheet leaves two conflicting figures with nothing linking them, which is exactly the ambiguity a complaint will exploit.
  24. The correct answer is B (retention is set provincially, commonly ten years from the last service): health professions are regulated provincially, so the retention period comes from the regulator and the legislation of each province, commonly ten years from the last service for adults and ten years from the age of majority for minors. There is no single federal retention number, and PIPEDA does not set a seven year rule for health records. Destroying a record when a prescription expires confuses the validity of a prescription with the life of the record. NACOR administers the national examination and sets no record retention rules.
  25. The correct answer is A (the measurements taken, the method used, and who performed the service): when a complaint turns on how a monocular pupillary distance was obtained, the defence depends on a contemporaneous record of the values, the technique used, and the optician responsible for the service. Frame colour, temple length, and a supplier order number describe the product rather than the clinical work performed. Lens brand, coating, and warranty details are commercial specifications that do not evidence how a measurement was taken. Appointment time, payment method, and collection date are transaction data and answer none of the clinical questions raised.
  26. The correct answer is D (the prescription belongs to the patient and refusing to release is indefensible): a patient is entitled to a copy of their own prescription, and withholding it to keep the sale in house cannot be justified professionally. Paying for or holding a record does not create ownership of the information contained in it. Requiring the prescriber's written permission adds a gatekeeper to information the patient already owns. Making release conditional on a purchase turns a professional obligation into a commercial bargaining chip, which is precisely the practice the entitlement exists to prevent.
  27. The correct answer is B (treat it as needing a current prescription, since a neutralization is not one): neutralizing the current lenses records what the patient is wearing, which is legitimate for a repair or a service note, but it is not a prescription and cannot support the supply of new eyewear in most jurisdictions. Supplying the same power and labelling it a repair service simply renames the same scope breach. A signed waiver does not transfer responsibility for practising outside scope back to the patient. The fact that he already wears the power does not make an eighteen month expired prescription current.
  28. The correct answer is A (a safety data sheet with sixteen sections in a fixed standard order): under WHMIS 2015 every hazardous product has a safety data sheet with sixteen numbered sections in a set order, including first aid, handling and storage, and exposure controls, and it must be accessible to workers. Supplier labels carry pictograms, a signal word, and hazard and precautionary statements, not a sixteen part document. A product inventory identifies what is held on site but contains none of the handling or first aid detail. The nine section material safety data sheet belongs to the pre 2015 system that WHMIS 2015 replaced.
  29. The correct answer is C (hazard pictograms, a signal word, and the hazard and precautionary statements): a compliant supplier label under WHMIS 2015 carries the product identifier, supplier identifier, standardized pictograms, a signal word such as Danger or Warning, and hazard and precautionary statements. Batch numbers, expiry dates, and training records are inventory or administrative details rather than required label elements. Employee initials, storage location, and the date opened describe local workplace housekeeping and do not appear on a supplier label. Colour coded classes and numeric ratings belong to other hazard systems that WHMIS 2015 does not use.
  30. The correct answer is D (worker education and training on the products actually present in the workplace): WHMIS 2015 rests on three elements, which are labels, safety data sheets, and worker education and training, and this workplace has the first two while omitting the third. Supplier labelling was already correct, and decanted containers require a workplace label rather than a reapplied supplier label. A written spill plan is good practice under other health and safety requirements but is not one of the three WHMIS elements. An annual third party audit of hazardous products is not a WHMIS obligation at all.
  31. The correct answer is B (dress eyewear is not impact rated protection, which must be worn instead): impact rated protection is tested and certified as a complete assembly, so a dress frame and dress lenses do not qualify no matter what the lens material is. Polycarbonate does not lose its impact resistance simply because it has been surfaced and edged into an ordinary spectacle lens. Metal frames do not conduct meaningful heat from an edger onto the wearer's skin. Ultraviolet output is not the hazard at the edging bench, since the risk being controlled there is flying fragments and dust.
  32. The correct answer is A (control at source using the machine's extraction or wet cutting system): lens dust from polycarbonate, Trivex, and high index materials must be captured where it is generated, which is why edgers are built with extraction or wet cutting systems. A ceiling mounted purifier treats room air after the dust has already reached the operator's breathing zone. Dust masks are personal protective equipment, which sits at the bottom of the control hierarchy and does not replace capture at source. Sweeping addresses settled debris after the fact and puts fine particles back into the air.
  33. The correct answer is C (never acceptable, since bypassing a machine guard removes a required control): an interlock is an engineering control designed to stop the cycle when the chamber is open, and defeating it exposes the operator to a rotating wheel and flying fragments. Wearing eye protection does not license the removal of a higher level control, because personal protection is the last line rather than a substitute. Telling a supervisor afterward does not make the exposure retroactively safe. Restoring the machine at the end of the run leaves every job in between performed with the guard defeated.
  34. The correct answer is B (missing workplace labelling, uncontrolled vapour, and no ventilation at source): a hazardous product decanted into another container needs a workplace label, an open solvent container releases vapour continuously, and solvent cleaning of blocking pads requires local ventilation. Calibration of a dispensing container is not a hazard control and misses every real failure present. Storage temperature and the choice of cleaning agent are not what makes this scene unsafe, since acetone is a normal deblocking solvent when it is handled correctly. Recording the quantity consumed is a stock control matter with no bearing on exposure.
  35. The correct answer is D (wash with soap and water, since alcohol rub does not replace it): alcohol based hand rub is effective on hands that are not visibly soiled, but visible soil requires soap and water because the rub cannot lift organic material from the skin. Repeating the rub twice does not overcome that limitation, since the problem is the soil rather than the dose. Deferring soap and water until the end of the day leaves contaminated hands in contact with the next patient's face and frame. Pulling gloves over soiled hands hides the problem and still contaminates whatever is touched when they come off.
  36. The correct answer is A (the surface must stay wet for the full time stated on the label): a disinfectant is only validated for the contact time printed on its label, so wiping the surface dry immediately stops the process before it has done anything useful. Spraying onto the surface or onto a cloth is a manufacturer specific instruction and is not what fails here. The forehead rest does not need a solvent, and solvents risk damaging plastic and coated surfaces. Applying the product twice is meaningless if each application is wiped away before the stated contact time has elapsed.
  37. The correct answer is C (placed in a soiled tray, then cleaned and disinfected before returning): a frame a patient has worn goes into a designated soiled tray or bin and returns to the display only after cleaning and disinfection, with attention to the temple tips, nose pads, and bridge. Wiping the demonstration lens leaves untouched exactly the surfaces that contacted the patient's skin. Holding a frame aside for a day is not a disinfection method and simply delays the same contamination. Restricting cleaning to purchased frames reverses the logic, since it is the try on frames that pass between faces.
  38. The correct answer is B (it removes soil well but is not by itself a disinfection step): ultrasonic cleaning is excellent at lifting soil from hinges, screws, and pad arms, but it is not on its own a disinfection process for the surfaces that touch a patient's skin, so a separate disinfection step is still required. Changing the solution each shift improves cleaning quality without turning the process into disinfection. Frame material does not decide the question, since metal and plastic frames both need the disinfection step. Nothing about the cycle sterilizes the surfaces, and sterilization is not the standard expected for try on frames.
  39. The correct answer is A (do not proceed, clean what he touched, wash your hands, and refer): an obviously red, weeping, and crusted eye is a presentation that stops contact based fitting, and the correct sequence is to decline the procedure, clean the contact surfaces, perform hand hygiene, and refer for assessment. Gloving up and disinfecting afterward still exposes the practice and other patients during the appointment itself. Substituting a penlight and ruler does not remove close facial contact and still delays the referral he needs. Suggesting an appointment within a month treats an acute presentation as routine.
  40. The correct answer is C (with the optician, who carries the burden of proof at audit): registrants are expected to keep their own evidence of completed continuing education, and at audit it is the optician who must produce it. Course providers may issue certificates, but the registrant cannot rely on a provider to file attendance with a regulator on her behalf. NACOR accredits courses so they are recognized across participating regulators; it does not hold a personal completion record for each optician. An employer may keep training files as a convenience, yet the professional obligation stays with the individual registrant.
  41. The correct answer is D (NACOR accredits courses so approved training is recognized across regulators): national accreditation of continuing education is what allows one approved course to count in more than one participating jurisdiction, which reduces duplication for opticians licensed in several provinces. The credit requirement itself is set by each provincial regulator, along with any mandatory technical or jurisprudence components. NACOR does not deliver the courses or maintain individual completion records for provincial regulators. Auditing registrants is a regulator function, and no audit result would be reported to an optician's employer.
  42. The correct answer is B (say so and get help, since working beyond competence is a breach): competence includes knowing the limits of your own experience, and attempting a mounting system you have never used is practising outside competence even though the task itself is within the profession's scope. Relying on the recognized scope of practice confuses what opticians may do with what this optician is currently able to do safely. Expecting the lab to correct mounting errors transfers a clinical responsibility that remains with the dispenser. An optometrist is not the right source of help for a drill mount technique question.
  43. The correct answer is D (treat it as urgent and direct her for immediate assessment today): a sudden shower of new floaters with flashes of light and a dark area in the field is the classic pattern of a retinal problem and requires urgent assessment rather than a routine appointment. Completing the dispense and suggesting an examination in the coming weeks accepts a delay that can cost vision. Reassuring her that floaters are normal with new lenses attaches a benign explanation to a red flag and is also a diagnosis the optician cannot make. Lens powers and layout errors do not produce flashes or a field defect.
  44. The correct answer is C (say it needs urgent assessment now, without naming a cause, and document): severe pain with a red eye, nausea, and coloured haloes is an emergency presentation, and the optician's duty is to describe the urgency, direct the patient for immediate care, and record the advice given. Naming acute angle closure is a diagnosis, which remains outside scope even when the presentation strongly suggests it. Booking a dispensing appointment once the pain settles treats an emergency as a scheduling problem. Neutralizing his current lenses looks for a refractive explanation that these symptoms plainly do not have.
  45. The correct answer is A (concerns raised after leaving cannot be verified, so they cannot be addressed): candidates must report faulty equipment, a wrongly set station, or any disruption to the moderator at the time so it can be checked and recorded while the evidence still exists. Once the candidate has left, nothing can be confirmed, and this single rule accounts for more failed appeals than any other. Equipment problems can support an appeal, but only when they are raised and documented during the session. Appeals are brought by the candidate, and provincial regulators play no part in filing them, while familiarization time is an opportunity to check instruments rather than a waiver of later concerns.
  46. The correct answer is A (contact the prescriber and have the eye designations confirmed before ordering): the right eye is written first on the top line by convention, but an unlabelled form is an incomplete prescription and an optician has no authority to resolve it by assumption. Leaning on the convention when the labels are absent can put a full dioptre of sphere on the wrong eye. Choosing by recorded vision or averaging the two lines both invent powers that no prescriber wrote. A short call to the source removes the risk completely.
  47. The correct answer is B (axis runs from 001 to 180 degrees with no sign or dioptre value): axis names the meridian along which the cylinder has no power, so it is a direction and not a power. It is never signed and never carries a dioptre value, which is what makes the trainee entry wrong. The 001 to 180 range covers every possible meridian, since 200 degrees describes the same line as 020. Attaching the sign of the sphere or of the cylinder to an axis is an entry error the lab will query.
  48. The correct answer is D (-0.75 -1.00 x 020, with the cylinder and axis left unchanged): the near prescription is the distance prescription with the add applied to the sphere only, so -3.25 plus +2.50 gives -0.75. The cylinder and axis correct astigmatism at every distance and are carried through untouched. Applying the add to the cylinder, or transposing the lens for near, both alter the astigmatic correction the prescriber ordered. Subtracting the add instead of adding it lands on -5.75, five dioptres away from the correct near sphere.
  49. The correct answer is B (prism is a separate entry, ordered and verified on its own): prism is written in prism dioptres with a base direction and sits apart from sphere, cylinder, and axis on both the order and the verification. Combining it with sphere power would corrupt both figures, since the two describe different optical effects and use different units. Prism is not convertible into cylinder power at any meridian. It is verified on every job where it is prescribed, whether or not the patient reports symptoms.
  50. The correct answer is C (apply it to both eyes, since OU designates both eyes together): OD is the right eye, OS is the left, and OU covers both, which is why a single line can carry an instruction that applies to the pair. Treating OU as a right or left designation would leave one lens untinted and unpolarized, and the mismatch would be obvious at dispensing. Nothing on the order allows the patient to nominate one eye at the visit, because tint and polarization are lab operations already completed. Reading the abbreviation correctly is the whole of the task.
  51. The correct answer is A (+3.25 -2.00 x 155, the sphere and axis both adjusted): transposition adds the cylinder algebraically to the sphere, so +1.25 plus +2.00 gives +3.25, then reverses the cylinder sign to -2.00. The axis of 065 is ninety or less, so ninety is added to give 155. Carrying the original axis of 065 across leaves the cylinder in the wrong meridian entirely. Leaving the sphere at +1.25, or taking the cylinder off it to reach -0.75, both produce a lens well away from the one prescribed.
  52. The correct answer is D (-5.75 +1.50 x 020, with ninety degrees taken off the axis): the cylinder is added algebraically to the sphere, so -4.25 plus -1.50 gives -5.75, and the cylinder sign flips to plus. The original axis of 110 is above ninety, so ninety is subtracted to give 020. Adding ninety instead produces 200, which falls outside the 001 to 180 range and flags the error immediately. Taking the cylinder off the sphere to reach -2.75, or holding the axis at 110, each describes a different lens.
  53. The correct answer is C (+2.50 -3.50 x 135, the sphere and the axis both changed): -1.00 plus +3.50 gives a new sphere of +2.50, the cylinder reverses to -3.50, and the axis of 045 is ninety or less so ninety is added to give 135. The sphere crossing from minus to plus surprises candidates but follows directly from the algebra. Subtracting the cylinder from the sphere gives -4.50, seven dioptres away from the correct sphere. Keeping the original axis, or the original sphere, leaves the transposition half finished.
  54. The correct answer is A (+1.50 +2.25 x 085, ninety degrees taken off the axis): +3.75 plus -2.25 gives a new sphere of +1.50, the cylinder reverses to +2.25, and the axis of 175 is above ninety so ninety is subtracted to give 085. Adding ninety would give 265, a value no axis can take, since the scale stops at 180. Adding the cylinder magnitude to the sphere gives +6.00 and overplusses the lens badly. Holding the axis at 175 leaves the cylinder ninety degrees from where it belongs.
  55. The correct answer is B (-2.00 +2.00 x 180, the sphere now carrying the cylinder power): a plano sphere behaves like any other value in the algebra, so zero plus -2.00 gives a new sphere of -2.00, the cylinder reverses to +2.00, and 090 is ninety or less so ninety is added to give 180. Both written forms describe the same lens with the same power in each principal meridian. Keeping the sphere at plano, or reversing its sign to +2.00, produces a lens that is not equivalent. Leaving the axis at 090 puts the cylinder in the wrong meridian.
  56. The correct answer is D (add ninety when the axis is ninety or less, otherwise subtract ninety): that single rule keeps every result inside the 001 to 180 range, so 010 becomes 100, 090 becomes 180, 095 becomes 005, and 180 becomes 090. Always subtracting and then dropping the minus sign turns 010 into 080 rather than 100, an error of twenty degrees. Replacing an axis with its supplement is a different operation and transposes nothing. Cylinder power has no bearing on the axis step, which is applied at every cylinder power.
  57. The correct answer is A (-2.50 -2.00 x 020, returning exactly to the original written form): transposing an answer a second time must land back on the original, so -4.50 plus +2.00 gives -2.50, the cylinder reverses to -2.00, and 110 minus ninety gives 020. Landing anywhere else means a step was dropped on the first pass. Adding the cylinder to the sphere again gives -6.50 and proves nothing about the original work. Axis results of 110 or 200 show the ninety degree step was skipped or applied in the wrong direction.
  58. The correct answer is C (they are the same lens, with identical power in each principal meridian): transposing +2.25 -0.75 x 180 gives a sphere of +1.50, a cylinder of +0.75, and an axis of 090, which is exactly the second written form. Neither notation makes a lens stronger, because both describe the same two principal meridian powers. The idea that the plus cylinder version adds power comes from reading its two numbers as separate lenses stacked together. Nothing needs flagging to the lab beyond ordering in the minus cylinder form Canadian labs use.
  59. The correct answer is B (-5.50 D, the sphere plus one half of the cylinder power): spherical equivalent is sphere plus half the cylinder, so -4.50 plus half of -2.00 gives -4.50 plus -1.00, which is -5.50 D. Adding the whole cylinder gives -6.50 and overminuses the goggle by a full dioptre. Dropping the cylinder entirely leaves -4.50 and ignores half the astigmatic power. Subtracting rather than adding moves the answer to -3.50, the wrong direction for a minus cylinder.
  60. The correct answer is D (+0.50 D, the sphere plus one half of the cylinder power): half of -3.50 is -1.75, and +2.25 plus -1.75 gives +0.50 D. Adding that half cylinder in the plus direction instead gives +4.00 and reverses the sign of the value being averaged in. Taking the whole cylinder from the sphere gives -1.25, a result that crosses from plus to minus and is a large error. Keeping the sphere alone at +2.25 ignores the cylinder the calculation exists to average.
  61. The correct answer is C (+2.25 D, and the written form makes no difference to it): sphere plus half the cylinder gives +1.00 plus +1.25, which is +2.25 D. Transposing first gives +3.50 -2.50 x 180, and +3.50 plus -1.25 is again +2.25 D, so both routes agree exactly. Reporting +3.50 D quotes the transposed sphere rather than the spherical equivalent. Dropping the cylinder leaves +1.00 and averages nothing at all.
  62. The correct answer is A (decline, since an optician does not drop a prescribed cylinder on request): spherical equivalent belongs to temporary lenses, sun and swim wear where a cylinder is unavailable, and comparisons between prescriptions, not to reducing a patient regular spectacles. Supplying -3.75 D spheres would leave a prescribed 1.50 D cylinder uncorrected in each eye. A signed waiver does not create authority to alter a prescription, which rests with the prescriber. A 1.50 D cylinder is clinically significant, and a patient who wants the prescription changed should be referred back.
  63. The correct answer is D (-2.00 D, matching the newer card in overall average power): half of -0.50 is -0.25, and -1.75 plus -0.25 gives -2.00 D, so the two records carry the same average power even though one has a cylinder. Adding the whole cylinder gives -2.25 and overstates the change. Adding the half cylinder in the plus direction gives -1.50 and understates it. Taking the sphere alone at -1.75 skips the averaging step the comparison depends on.
  64. The correct answer is B (+5.00 D, from one divided by 0.20 metres exactly): power in dioptres is the reciprocal of the focal length in metres, and 20 centimetres is 0.20 m, so one divided by 0.20 gives +5.00 D. Feeding 20 centimetres straight into the reciprocal gives +0.05 D, the single most common error in this calculation. Reading the focal distance number as though it were the power gives +20.00 D, which is not a reciprocal at all. The +4.00 D value comes from a remembered 0.25 m example rather than the figure in front of the candidate.
  65. The correct answer is A (500 mm, from one divided by 2.00, then metres to millimetres): the reciprocal of 2.00 is 0.50, so the focal length is 0.50 m, and 0.50 m converts to 500 mm. Treating that 0.50 as though it were already millimetres gives 50 mm, out by a factor of ten. Taking the power figure of 2.00 straight across as 200 mm skips the reciprocal entirely. Working from 0.50 as if it were the power gives 2 m, or 2000 mm, which inverts the two quantities.
  66. The correct answer is C (+8.00 D, after converting 125 millimetres to 0.125 metres): the reciprocal must be taken in metres, and one divided by 0.125 gives +8.00 D. Converting only as far as 12.5 centimetres and stopping there gives +0.08 D, which is the step candidates skip most often. Reading the measurement as centimetres of power gives +12.50 D and abandons the reciprocal. Shifting the decimal in the measured number to reach +1.25 D is arithmetic with no formula behind it.
  67. The correct answer is B (about 40 centimetres, from one divided by 2.50 in metres): the add places the near focus at the reciprocal of its own power, so one divided by 2.50 is 0.40 m, which is 40 centimetres and a normal reading distance. Treating the add figure of 2.50 as the distance gives about 25 centimetres, far closer than the lens actually focuses. Reading it as metres gives 2.5 metres, a distance viewing position rather than a near one. Quoting 50 centimetres for every add ignores that working distance changes with add power.
  68. The correct answer is D (80 mm, from one divided by 12.50, then metres to millimetres): one divided by 12.50 gives 0.08 m, and 0.08 m converts to 80 mm. Treating that 0.08 as centimetres instead gives 800 mm, an answer ten times too long. Taking the power figure across as 12.5 mm skips the reciprocal completely. The 125 mm value is the reciprocal of 8.00 rather than of 12.50, and shows the two numbers being confused with each other.
  69. The correct answer is A (a plus lens adds convergence and a minus lens adds divergence): vergence describes whether light is converging, diverging, or parallel, and light from a distant object arrives with essentially zero vergence. A plus lens brings that light together toward a real focus, while a minus lens spreads it apart. Reversing the two descriptions gets the sign convention backwards and would place every focal length on the wrong side of the lens. Saying both converge light, or that a lens changes nothing, removes the distinction lens power is built on.
  70. The correct answer is C (+4.75 D, the front and back surface powers added algebraically): approximate lens power is the front surface power plus the back surface power taken with their signs, so +8.00 plus -3.25 gives +4.75 D. The result is an approximation because it ignores centre thickness, which matters most in higher plus powers. Adding the magnitudes without regard to sign gives +11.25 D and describes no real lens. Averaging the two surfaces gives +2.38 D, and reversing the sign gives -4.75 D, both of which abandon the addition the method rests on.
  71. The correct answer is D (-10.25 D, since +4.00 added to it gives the prescribed power): the two surfaces add to the finished power, so the back surface must be the prescribed power less the front curve, which is -6.25 minus +4.00, or -10.25 D. Adding the base curve to the prescription instead gives -2.25 D, and that lens would finish at +1.75 D rather than -6.25 D. Reversing the sign to +10.25 D would produce a strong plus lens instead of a minus one. Grinding -6.25 D on the back ignores the +4.00 D of front curve already present.
  72. The correct answer is B (a meniscus is convex in front and concave behind for better off axis vision): ophthalmic lenses are made in meniscus form so the wearer looks through a curved surface at every angle of gaze, which controls aberrations away from the optical centre. The front surface power is the base curve and the back is ground to whatever value brings the pair to the prescribed power. Curving both surfaces does not halve anything, since the two surface powers add. A lens clock reads any surface, and the frame groove seats the edge rather than the surfaces.
  73. The correct answer is C (+7.58 D, the reading multiplied by 0.67 divided by 0.53): the lens clock scale is calibrated for an index of 1.53, so a reading taken on any other material is corrected by multiplying by the actual index minus one, then dividing by 0.53. That gives 6.00 times 0.67 divided by 0.53, which is +7.58 D. True power always exceeds the dial reading on a higher index, because more bending happens for the same curve, so +4.75 D shows the correction run in the reducing direction. Using the full index values, or accepting the raw +6.00 D, both skip the correction the scale requires.
  74. The correct answer is A (-3.49 D, the reading multiplied by 0.74 divided by 0.53): the Geneva lens measure is calibrated for 1.53, so on a 1.74 lens the dial figure is multiplied by 0.74 and divided by 0.53, giving -3.49 D with the minus sign kept. Accepting the raw -2.50 D treats the instrument as index independent, which it is not. Running the ratio the other way round gives -1.79 D and makes the surface look flatter than it really is. Multiplying by the full index of 1.74 rather than the index minus one gives -4.35 D.
  75. The correct answer is D (steeper curves suit plus powers and wraps, and a specified curve is verifiable): best form principles put steeper base curves on plus lenses and on wrapped frames, and flatter curves on minus lenses. Once a base curve is written on the work order it becomes a checkable item, read with a lens clock and held to three quarters of a dioptre under the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR. Frame colour has no bearing on base curve, and add power does not drive it either. A specified curve is never advisory, because a job can be returned to the lab for failing it.
  76. The correct answer is B (the higher index bends light more, so flatter curves give the same power): refractive index describes how strongly a material bends light, and a stronger bending material reaches the prescribed power with flatter surfaces, leaving less material at the edge of a minus lens. Abbe value moves against index, so a 1.67 material has a lower Abbe than a 1.50 one rather than a higher one. Nothing is compressed at the edger, which cuts the shape rather than densifying the material. Blank size follows the shape and the decentration, not the index.
  77. The correct answer is A (a low Abbe value gives chromatic dispersion, worst in strong powers peripherally): Abbe value measures dispersion and runs against index, so a 1.74 material sits near the bottom of the range and splits light noticeably away from the optical centre. A strong power in a large frame puts the wearer well off centre in normal gaze, which is exactly where fringing shows. A high Abbe value means low dispersion, so describing it as increasing dispersion inverts the relationship. Anti-reflective coating cuts reflections rather than creating dispersion, and base curve does not produce colour fringing.
  78. The correct answer is C (Trivex, with an Abbe in the forties and safe drilling behaviour): Trivex matches polycarbonate for impact resistance but carries an Abbe value in the low to middle forties, so it drills safely for rimless work while fringing far less than polycarbonate. Polycarbonate sits near 30, the lowest Abbe in common use, so calling it the highest reverses the scale. CR-39 has an excellent Abbe near 58 but chips when drilled, which rules it out of a rimless mount. High index materials near 1.67 carry low Abbe values and are not impact rated as a class.
  79. The correct answer is D (polycarbonate or Trivex should be fitted, since both are impact resistant): a child everyday eyewear should be made in an impact resistant material, and those two qualify by a wide margin. CR-39 tints and polishes well but is not impact rated for safety wear, so cost alone is not a reason to fit it to a child. Glass is heavy and obsolete for children eyewear whatever its scratch resistance. High index plastics are chosen for thinness in strong prescriptions, are not impact rated as a class, and a +1.75 D lens has no thickness problem to solve.
  80. The correct answer is B (polycarbonate lenses to CSA Z94.3, the Canadian industrial eye protection standard): CSA Z94.3 is the Canadian standard for industrial eye and face protection, and polycarbonate is the default lens material for occupational eyewear built to it. A grinding bay is a high impact environment where glass is unsuitable, whatever its scratch resistance. CR-39 is not impact rated, so listing it against the standard would not produce a compliant job. High index materials are chosen for thickness reasons and are not impact rated as a class.
  81. The correct answer is C (glass is heavy and offers poor impact resistance compared to modern plastics): at -5.50 D a glass lens carries real weight, and glass is obsolete for safety and children eyewear because it fails on impact rather than resisting it. A professional driver spends long hours in the frame and is exposed to road debris, so both points bear on the conversation. Glass tints readily and edges to a bevel like any other material, so neither of those objections holds. Glass has an Abbe near 59, among the best available, so fringing is not the concern.
  82. The correct answer is A (high index gives thinness but is not impact rated as a class): high index plastics from 1.60 to 1.74 solve thickness in a strong prescription but are not impact rated, which matters in a three piece mount where the lens itself carries the hardware. Polycarbonate and Trivex are the materials that drill and mount safely, with Trivex favoured where weight and Abbe value matter. CR-39 chips at the drill hole, which is exactly why it is avoided in rimless work. Glass chips as well and is the poorest choice for a drilled mount.
  83. The correct answer is B (Trivex has the lower specific gravity, so the finished lens weighs less): Trivex has the lowest specific gravity of the common lens materials, so at equal thickness it produces a lighter lens than polycarbonate and suits a patient who finds eyewear heavy on the nose. Its index is about 1.53 against roughly 1.586 for polycarbonate, so it finishes slightly thicker rather than thinner. Its Abbe value sits in the low to middle forties against about 30, so it is the higher of the two and unrelated to weight. Tinting behaviour affects colour options rather than edge thickness.
  84. The correct answer is D (control the room lighting, since the coating changes reflections not power): anti-reflective coating cuts surface reflections and raises transmission, which can wash out the lensometer target under a bright overhead light. Managing the room light restores a readable target without touching the lens. No coating alters prescribed power, so adding a quarter of a dioptre to the reading would introduce an error rather than correct one. Stripping a coating with solvent destroys the job, and a dim target under bright light is a lighting problem rather than a lens fault.
  85. The correct answer is C (let the lenses clear away from ultraviolet light, then read the power): photochromic lenses darken in ultraviolet light, so a pair taken from a sunlit window sits at or near full density and dims the lensometer target. Moving them away from ultraviolet and letting them clear restores a readable target and costs only a few minutes. The darkening does not change prescribed power, so returning the job to the lab on that basis would be wrong. Recording a density measured while the lens is temporarily dark misdescribes the product, and no drum setting compensates for lost light.
  86. The correct answer is B (the polarization axis, confirmed against a second polarized lens or known source): a polarized lens blocks one plane of light and only works if that plane is correctly oriented, so its axis is checked against a second polarized lens or a known polarized source. A lens mounted with its polarization rotated will not cut glare as intended, even with perfect power. Tint density and base curve are checked on their own terms and say nothing about polarization orientation. Rubbing a surface with a cloth is not a test of scratch coating and risks damaging the lens.
  87. The correct answer is A (each is a separate line item and is confirmed as ordered): hard coat, tint, and anti-reflective coating are each written on the work order and each is confirmed during verification, alongside material, size, and edge finish. Verification covers the whole order rather than the optical parameters alone, so skipping them leaves part of the job unchecked. Waiting for the patient to raise a concern moves the check to the dispensing table, where a return costs far more. An invoice records what was billed rather than what was made, so it is no substitute for inspecting the lenses.
  88. The correct answer is D (no treatment changes prescribed power, so no compensation should be ordered): coatings and tints change how light is transmitted and how easily a reading is taken, but they add no dioptric power and remove none. Ordering a compensated power would put a genuine error into a job that would otherwise be correct. Anti-reflective coating raises transmission rather than shifting power, at every prescription strength. Tints cut transmission and change colour, and neither effect shows on the lensometer as a power difference.
  89. The correct answer is C (colour and density, both of which are confirmed visually against the order): a tint is specified by its colour and by its density as a percentage, and both are confirmed visually against the order at verification. An entry naming only the colour leaves the lab guessing at how dark to run the job. Base curve and lens index are separate order items with their own purposes and do not define a tint. Abbe value describes dispersion within the material and has no part in how a tint is specified.
  90. The correct answer is B (an approximate power of -2.50 D, agreeing with the work order): approximate power is the algebraic sum of the two surface powers, so +6.50 plus -9.00 gives -2.50 D, matching the sphere on the order. Adding the magnitudes without their signs gives -15.50 D and describes a lens that could not have been made to this order. Averaging the two surfaces gives -1.25 D and abandons the addition entirely. Reversing the sign to +2.50 D would turn a minus lens into a plus one, which would be obvious on the lensometer.
  91. The correct answer is C (Eye size 52 mm, bridge or DBL 18 mm, temple length 140 mm): the three stamped numbers are always the A measurement, the DBL, and the temple length, in that order. The A measurement is the horizontal width of the boxing rectangle, the DBL is the distance between the two lens boxes where they come closest, and 140 mm is the temple. Effective diameter is never stamped on a frame because it belongs to the lens shape and comes from the frame data sheet or from measurement. Boxing centre distance and the B measurement are also absent from the stamp.
  92. The correct answer is A (2 mm inward for each lens, 4 mm total for the pair): frame PD is the A measurement plus the DBL, so 54 plus 16 gives 70 mm, and half of that is 35 mm. Subtracting the 33 mm monocular PD leaves 2 mm of inward decentration per lens and 4 mm across the pair. Quoting 4 mm per lens confuses the total for the pair with the amount for one lens. Reversing the subtraction gives an outward answer, which would apply only if the monocular PD exceeded half the frame PD, and halving a second time down to 1 mm applies the division twice.
  93. The correct answer is D (Measure the shortest distance between the two boxing rectangles, the closest gap): the DBL is defined as the distance between the two lens boxes where they come closest, and that value added to the A measurement gives frame PD. Measuring the widest part of the bridge overstates the gap and pushes every later decentration figure the wrong way. A boxing centre to boxing centre reading is frame PD itself rather than the DBL. Reading the bridge along the datum line without reference to the boxes belongs to the older datum system and gives a different number on many shapes.
  94. The correct answer is B (Right lens 2.5 mm inward, left lens 4.0 mm inward, unequal amounts): the A measurement plus the DBL is 70 mm and half of that is 35 mm, the same reference for both eyes. The right lens needs 35 minus 32.5, which is 2.5 mm, and the left needs 35 minus 31.0, which is 4.0 mm. Reversing those two figures assigns the larger movement to the wrong eye and leaves both centres misplaced. Averaging the two readings into a 63.5 mm binocular PD produces 3.25 mm on both sides and puts neither optical centre in front of its own pupil.
  95. The correct answer is A (Twice the longest radius from the boxing centre to the lens edge): the ED is a diameter, so the longest radius is measured from the boxing centre out to the most distant point of the shape and then doubled. On most shapes that longest radius runs toward a corner, which is why the ED exceeds both the horizontal and the vertical dimension of the box. Taking the single longest radius without doubling it gives roughly half the true figure and would badly undersize the blank. Datum line width is the older datum measurement, and averaging the two box dimensions has no place in the boxing system.
  96. The correct answer is C (No decentration is required, the boxing centres already match the pupils): the A measurement plus the DBL is 64 mm and half of that is 32 mm, which is exactly the monocular PD for each eye. The subtraction gives zero, so the optical centres are ground at the boxing centres and no lateral movement is ordered. Answers of 2 mm in either direction come from mis-halving 64 or from reversing the subtraction. When decentration is zero the minimum blank size collapses to the effective diameter plus the edging allowance, the smallest blank this job can ever need.
  97. The correct answer is B (64 mm, the effective diameter plus twice the decentration per lens): minimum blank size is the effective diameter plus two times the decentration, so 58 plus 6 gives 64 mm. The decentration is doubled because pulling the optical centre to one side leaves that much uncovered shape on the opposite side of the blank. Adding the decentration only once gives 61 mm and orders a blank that will cut short at the far edge. Leaving the decentration out altogether, or subtracting it, both ignore that the blank must still cover the full shape after the centre moves.
  98. The correct answer is D (63 mm, from 4 mm of decentration on each lens then doubled): the A measurement plus the DBL is 70 mm, half is 35 mm, and 35 minus 31 gives 4 mm of inward decentration per lens. Minimum blank size is then 55 plus twice 4, which is 63 mm. Counting the decentration once gives 59 mm and undersizes the blank. Using 8 mm, which is the total for the pair, as though it applied to one lens oversizes the order to 71 mm and wastes material, and the bare effective diameter ignores the movement completely.
  99. The correct answer is A (Datum length is read along the midline, box width is the widest extent): the older datum system measured lens size along the horizontal midline of the shape, so on a shape widest above that line the datum length reads smaller than the boxing width. The boxing system draws the smallest rectangle containing the shape, so the horizontal box dimension is always the widest extent no matter where that width occurs. Datum length is therefore never the larger of the two. Both systems put the reference centre on the datum line, which runs halfway up the vertical dimension of the box, not at its top.
  100. The correct answer is B (A calculated 71 mm, so the next stock blank at 75 mm): the A measurement plus the DBL is 70 mm, half is 35 mm, and 35 minus 30.5 gives 4.5 mm of decentration per lens. Minimum blank size is 60 plus twice 4.5, which is 69 mm, and the 2 mm allowance takes the requirement to 71 mm. Stock blanks run in 5 mm steps, so 71 rounds up to 75 and never down to 70. Stopping at 69 mm drops the allowance, adding the decentration once gives 66.5 mm, and omitting it altogether gives 62 mm, all of which order a blank too small to cut the shape.
  101. The correct answer is D (The B of 34 mm, the vertical height of the rectangle drawn around the shape): in the boxing system the smallest rectangle that fully contains the lens shape is drawn first, the A measurement is its horizontal width and the B measurement is its vertical height. The datum line runs horizontally halfway up the vertical dimension, and the boxing centre sits on that line at the midpoint of the horizontal dimension. The A measurement is horizontal rather than vertical, and the DBL is the gap between the two boxes rather than a dimension inside one box. Effective diameter is twice the longest radius from the boxing centre and is normally the largest of the four figures.
  102. The correct answer is C (The left lens drives it, needing a 60 mm minimum blank size): half of the A measurement plus the DBL is 34 mm, so the right lens decentres 34 minus 32, which is 2 mm, and the left decentres 34 minus 30, which is 4 mm. Minimum blank size is 52 plus twice the decentration, giving 56 mm on the right and 60 mm on the left. When one blank size is ordered for the pair, the larger figure governs, so 60 mm is the number and 56 mm would cut the left lens short. Averaging the two PDs to 31 mm produces a matched 58 mm on both sides and still leaves the left lens undersized.
  103. The correct answer is A (65 mm, using a 5 mm resultant from the right triangle): when the optical centre moves both across and down, the two amounts combine as the hypotenuse of a right triangle, and 3 with 4 gives a resultant of 5 mm. Minimum blank size is then 55 plus twice 5, which is 65 mm. Adding the two figures arithmetically to 7 mm treats perpendicular movements as though they acted along the same line and oversizes the blank to 69 mm. Using either single figure on its own ignores the other direction and leaves the shape uncovered on one side of the blank.
  104. The correct answer is D (It is the temple length and plays no part in the blank arithmetic): the third number in a frame stamp is temple length in millimetres, which belongs to the fit and to the order but never to layout. Blank size runs on the A measurement, the DBL, the effective diameter, and the monocular PD only. Adding 145 to the other two stamped figures would give an absurd frame PD and a meaningless decentration. Effective diameter comes from the lens shape and the edging allowance is set by the lab, so the temple figure supplies neither of them.
  105. The correct answer is B (Total is 9 mm, but 4.5 mm each assumes a symmetric face): total decentration is frame PD minus binocular PD, so 72 minus 63 gives 9 mm for the pair. Splitting that evenly gives 4.5 mm per lens, and that split holds only when the two monocular PDs are equal, which most faces are not. Treating 9 mm as the per lens figure doubles the movement and destroys the fit. Halving the total again down to 4.5 for the pair and 2.25 per lens applies the division twice, so the correct habit is to obtain monocular PDs and work each eye on its own.
  106. The correct answer is A (5 mm inward on each lens, 10 mm total for the pair): frame PD is 58 plus 20, which is 78 mm, and half of that is 39 mm.   107. The correct answer is C (Each corridor must sit in front of its own pupil, not an average): halving a binocular PD assumes the two eyes sit equal distances from the nasal midline, and on an asymmetric face they do not. A progressive has a narrow corridor and a fitting cross that must line up with one specific pupil, so an averaged figure puts at least one eye off the corridor and produces swim and blur. The binocular figure is already a valid millimetre measurement, it is simply the wrong one for this lens design.   108. The correct answer is B (32.5 mm each eye, the half frame PD less the stated decentration): frame PD is 54 plus 18, which is 72 mm, so half of it is 36 mm.   109.   110. The correct answer is A (Blocking, where the axis reference is aligned before the block is fixed): the block fixes the lens to the edger in one orientation, so any rotation present when the block is attached is carried straight through the cut. The marked optical centre and axis line must be lined up to the layout before the block is pressed on, and a slipped or misaligned block rotates the whole finished shape. Chamfering the corners, buffing the edge, and cutting a groove all happen after the shape is already cut and none of them can rotate the axis. A lens correct as an uncut and wrong as a finished lens points straight back to the blocker.

  112. The correct answer is D (A calculated 73 mm, so a 75 mm blank goes on the order): the A measurement plus the DBL is 72 mm, half is 36 mm, and 36 minus 29 gives 7 mm of decentration per lens. The binocular reading is not invalid and the total decentration for the pair is unchanged, since the two eyes still sum to 62 mm.   115. The correct answer is C (A groove cut into the lens edge to seat the nylon cord): a semi-rimless mount holds the lens with a cord that must sit in a channel cut around the edge, and the groove depth and position are set at the edger. A raised V bevel is what a full rim frame takes, because there the frame carries the groove and the lens carries the V. A drilled flat edge belongs to a true rimless mount where hardware passes through the lens itself.   118. The correct answer is C (It removes sharp corners, protecting fingers and reducing chipping at the edge): a safety bevel is a small chamfer taken off the sharp front and back corners left by the edger. It protects the patient and the technician handling the lens, it stops small chips starting at a sharp corner, and it helps the lens seat cleanly in the frame. It is not a bevel repositioning step, since apex placement is set when the shape is cut. It removes only a fraction of a millimetre and does nothing for the cosmetic thickness of a strong minus lens, and buffing the edge to a clear finish is a separate operation.
