  120. The correct answer is D (Right lens 61 mm and left lens 67 mm minimum blank size): half of the A measurement plus the DBL is 36 mm, so the right lens decentres 2.5 mm and the left decentres 5.5 mm. Minimum blank size is the ED plus twice the decentration, giving 56 plus 5, which is 61 mm on the right, and 56 plus 11, which is 67 mm on the left. Swapping those two figures assigns the larger blank to the wrong eye and the lab cuts the other lens short. Adding the decentration once gives 58.5 and 61.5, and averaging the two PDs to 32 mm produces a matched 64 mm that suits neither eye.
  121. The correct answer is B (Trivex or polycarbonate, because both resist chipping around a drilled hole): a drilled mount concentrates stress at the hole, so the material has to tolerate the drill and the mounting hardware without star cracks. Trivex and polycarbonate both carry the impact resistance that rimless work needs, and Trivex adds the lowest specific gravity of the group and a better Abbe value than polycarbonate. CR-39 has good optics but is brittle at a drilled hole, and glass is worse still and obsolete for this mount. Chasing the thinnest high index material ignores that this class is not impact rated and cracks readily at drilled hardware.
  122. The correct answer is C (3.5 mm of decentration and a 60 mm minimum blank size): the A measurement plus the DBL is 70 mm, half is 35 mm, and 35 minus 31.5 gives 3.5 mm of inward decentration for each lens. Minimum blank size is 53 plus twice 3.5, which is 60 mm. Using the 7 mm pair total as the per lens figure inflates the answer to 67 mm. Adding the decentration once gives 56.5 mm, and quoting the bare effective diameter ignores that the shape must still be covered once the optical centre moves nasally.
  123. The correct answer is A (62 mm, taken from the right lens with the larger decentration): half of the A measurement plus the DBL is 35 mm, so the right lens decentres 3 mm and the left decentres 2 mm. Minimum blank size is 56 plus twice the decentration, giving 62 mm on the right and 60 mm on the left. A single blank size for the pair must satisfy the harder eye, so the larger figure governs and the smaller one would cut the right lens short. Averaging the two figures to 61 mm still falls below what the right lens needs, and the bare effective diameter ignores the decentration entirely.
  124. The correct answer is B (Polishing matters most on the semi-rimless job, where the edge is visible): a grooved or rimless lens leaves most of its edge exposed to view, and a ground matte edge looks grey and catches light, so polishing is close to mandatory on that work. On a full rim acetate frame the bevel is buried inside the rim and the edge is never seen, so polishing there is cosmetic and is often skipped. Polishing is not a prerequisite for insertion on either job. It is an edge finish only and changes nothing about the light passing through the optical zone of the lens.
  125. The correct answer is D (9 mm of decentration and an 83 mm requirement, beyond most stock): the A measurement plus the DBL is 78 mm, half is 39 mm, and 39 minus 30 gives 9 mm of inward decentration per lens. Minimum blank size is 63 plus twice 9, which is 81 mm, and the allowance brings the requirement to 83 mm. That sits past most stock ranges and past many surfaced high index blanks, which is the signal to move the patient to a narrower frame rather than fight the lens. Counting the decentration once gives 74 mm and omitting it gives 65 mm, while treating the 18 mm pair total as a per lens figure overstates it to 101 mm.
  126. The correct answer is C (More decentration, a larger blank, and thicker temporal edges all result): a wider eye size raises frame PD, so half of it moves further from a 29 mm monocular PD and the decentration per lens climbs. That larger decentration feeds straight into minimum blank size, and a minus lens cut from a bigger blank with its optical centre pulled well nasal carries noticeably more edge thickness on the temporal side. A bigger blank never thins a minus lens, because thickness in minus power grows with distance from the optical centre. Boxing centres moving outward is exactly what increases decentration rather than reducing it, so frame size drives both the blank order and the cosmetic result.
  127. The correct answer is A (4 mm inward per lens, assuming the two monocular PDs are equal): frame PD is 72 mm and half of that is 36 mm, while the binocular figure has to be halved to 32 mm before it can be used, which is where the assumption of symmetry enters. Subtracting 32 from 36 gives 4 mm of inward decentration for each lens and 8 mm across the pair. Using the 8 mm pair total per lens doubles the movement, and halving once more to 2 mm applies the division twice. The direction is inward because half the frame PD exceeds the assumed monocular PD, and the safer course is to bring the patient back for monocular readings.
  128. The correct answer is D (59 mm, the effective diameter plus twice the decentration plus allowance): the A measurement plus the DBL is 70 mm, half is 35 mm, and 35 minus 33 gives 2 mm of decentration per lens. Minimum blank size is 53 plus twice 2, which is 57 mm, and the stated 2 mm lab allowance brings the requirement to 59 mm. Stopping at 57 mm leaves out the allowance the job specifies, and quoting the bare effective diameter leaves out both terms. Treating the 4 mm pair total as the per lens figure pushes the requirement to 63 mm and orders more material than the job needs.
  129. The correct answer is B (1 mm inward per lens, giving 2 mm total across the pair): frame PD is the A measurement plus the DBL, so 44 plus 24 gives 68 mm even though the eye size is small, and half of that is 34 mm. Subtracting 33 leaves 1 mm of inward decentration for each lens and 2 mm for the pair. Quoting 2 mm per lens uses the pair total in the wrong place, and reversing the subtraction sends the movement outward when the frame is still the wider of the two. Leaving the 24 mm bridge out of frame PD gives a half value of 22 mm and a false outward answer of 11 mm.
  130. The correct answer is C (Frame two, by 4 mm, needing 60 mm against 64 mm): frame one has a frame PD of 70 mm, so half is 35 mm, the decentration is 4 mm, and the minimum blank size is 56 plus 8, which is 64 mm. Frame two has a frame PD of 68 mm, so half is 34 mm, the decentration is 3 mm, and the minimum blank size is 54 plus 6, which is 60 mm. The gap is 4 mm rather than the 2 mm difference in effective diameter, because the smaller frame also saves a millimetre of decentration on each lens and that saving is doubled in the formula. A larger eye size raises decentration on a narrow PD instead of cutting it, and the two frame PDs are 70 and 68, not identical.
  131. The correct answer is A (The eyepiece, which is focused by each operator before any reading): the ocular threads in and out on a helical mount to focus the fixed reticle for whoever is looking through it, and its small dioptric scale shows how far it has been set from zero. The power drum moves the target carriage and reads back vertex power, so it changes target sharpness rather than reticle sharpness. The axis wheel only rotates the target assembly and reads cylinder axis. The prism compensator shifts a displaced target back into the reticle rings and plays no part in focusing the reticle.
  132. The correct answer is C (It provides the fixed reference for centring the lens under test): the reticle is etched into the eyepiece and never moves with the drum, so it gives a stable crosshair and set of rings against which the target image is centred and the prism scale is read. Back vertex power is read from the drum scale, not from the reticle. The axis scale sits on the axis wheel, which does rotate with the target assembly. Compensating for a displaced target is the job of the prism compensator.
  133. The correct answer is B (It moves the target carriage and reads back vertex power): the drum drives the illuminated target along the internal optical bench, and the position at which a set of lines becomes sharp corresponds to the back vertex power in that meridian. Focusing the reticle belongs to the eyepiece and is done once per operator with no lens in place. Raising the frame so the eyewires sit level is the lens table's job. Stamping the three inked dots is done by the marking device after the reading is finished.
  134. The correct answer is D (A horizontal reference line drawn through the instrument's optical axis): the marking device stamps a centre dot where the optical axis passed through the lens, with the two outer dots 180 degrees apart on either side, giving a horizontal line used for checking tilt and for measuring heights. Near reference points relate to a multifocal segment and are not what these three pins produce. Blank size is calculated from the frame and the decentration, not stamped by the instrument. Lens clock legs are placed anywhere on the surface being measured and need no marks at all.
  135. The correct answer is A (The lens table, raised or lowered until the frame sits square): the table supports the lower eyewires so the frame front sits perpendicular to the instrument axis, and a tilted lens is read obliquely, which adds false cylinder and smears the target. Turning the eyepiece would only change reticle sharpness and would corrupt every later sphere reading. Rotating the axis wheel changes the orientation of the target and does not level the frame. The prism compensator moves a displaced target image and does nothing about a frame that is physically tipped.
  136. The correct answer is B (The back surface, so back vertex power is read correctly): lensometers are calibrated to measure from the back surface of the lens to its back focal point, which is the quantity a prescription specifies. Seating the front surface against the stop gives front vertex power, a different value on any lens of real power or thickness. The lens edge rests on the lens table rather than on the small central aperture. The temple hinge plays no part in seating the lens against the stop.
  137. The correct answer is D (The axis wheel, which rotates the target and reads cylinder axis): rotating the target assembly aligns the lines with the meridian being measured, and once they are continuous and evenly spaced the 0 to 180 degree scale gives the axis directly. Levelling the frame is done with the lens table and affects seating rather than line continuity. The eyepiece sets reticle sharpness once per operator before any lens is read. The marking device only stamps dots once the reading is complete.
  138. The correct answer is C (The prism compensator, which brings the displaced target back on scale): a rotating prism device is fitted to most manual lensometers so that a target driven off the reticle by lens prism can be returned toward centre and quantified. The auxiliary high power lens addresses power beyond the drum range, not lateral displacement of the target. The holding arm only keeps the lens pressed to the stop. The lens table sets the vertical position of the frame and cannot recover a target that has run outside the rings.
  139. The correct answer is C (The target, moved along the bench by the power drum): the illuminated crossed line pattern is the target, also called the mire, and it comes to sharp focus when the drum position matches the power of the meridian being measured. The reticle is the etched crosshair inside the eyepiece and stays still when the drum turns. Graticule is another name for that same fixed etching, not for the illuminated pattern. The aperture the back of the lens presses against is the lens stop.
  140. The correct answer is A (The target is focused by the drum, the reticle by the eyepiece): the illuminated target sits on a carriage driven by the power drum, so drum position sets target sharpness and supplies the power reading, while the etched reticle is brought to focus by threading the eyepiece to suit the operator's eye. Reversing those two roles would make the power reading depend on the operator's accommodation. Saying the eyepiece focuses both leaves no control for power. Saying the drum focuses both leaves nothing adjustable for different operators.
  141. The correct answer is D (The sphere power, because every reading shifts in the same direction): an eyepiece left out of adjustment introduces a systematic offset, so each sphere reading is displaced by roughly the same amount on every lens measured. Cylinder usually survives because it is the difference between two drum readings taken with the same offset, which cancels. Axis is read from the wheel and is untouched by the offset. Segment height and PD come from the marked dots and a ruler, so neither is shifted by how the ocular is set.
  142. The correct answer is B (The eye accommodates and pulls a blurred reticle into apparent focus): coming from the minus side lets the operator's own accommodation compensate for a reticle that is not truly in focus, so the eyepiece stops short of the correct setting and every sphere afterward carries that error. Turning fully to plus first blurs the reticle in a direction accommodation cannot rescue, and stopping at the first sharp point relaxes it. The etching itself is equally sharp from either approach, since only the eye changes. Neither drum drift nor thread backlash is the mechanism at work.
  143. The correct answer is A (No lens in place, drum well into plus, eyepiece fully plus): the aperture must be clear so nothing but the reticle is being judged, the drum is driven into plus so the target is thoroughly blurred and cannot be mistaken for the reticle, and the eyepiece starts fully toward plus so the operator turns back and stops at the first point of clarity. Seating a lens during this step puts an unknown power into the light path and defeats the purpose. Starting with the ocular already at zero simply adopts the previous operator's eye. Starting from the minus end invites accommodation to hide the true focus point.
  144. The correct answer is D (The setting suits the first operator's eye, not the second one's): the eyepiece compensates for the refractive state and accommodative habit of whoever looks through it, so it is personal and must be reset by each operator on each instrument. Nothing about removing a lens moves the ocular thread, so it does not drift on its own. Zeroing the drum is a separate calibration check and says nothing about whether the ocular suits a given eye. The need to reset applies equally to spherical and astigmatic lenses.
  145. The correct answer is C (That the target focuses sharply with the drum set at zero): with nothing in the aperture the instrument should read plano, so a sharp target at a drum setting of zero confirms both the ocular setting and the instrument's calibration before any lens is measured. Setting the axis wheel to 180 proves nothing about focus or calibration. Testing the marking pins is useful housekeeping but is not a check on the reading itself. Raising the lens table to its highest position would only mis-seat the first frame.
  146. The correct answer is B (Refocus the eyepiece before any further power reading is taken): the ocular setting depends on the operator's accommodative state, which changes after time away from the instrument, so a quick reset costs seconds and protects every remaining sphere. Repeating pairs already recorded burns time that a 75 minute section does not allow without a reason to doubt them. The axis wheel is a mechanical scale and does not need recalibrating partway through. Cleaning the marking pins is housekeeping rather than a focus safeguard.
  147. The correct answer is B (The setting simply suits that operator's eye and is not a fault): the ocular scale records how far the eyepiece has been threaded for the individual, and a value away from zero is entirely normal for anyone whose eye is not relaxed and emmetropic at that point. A displaced ocular scale is not evidence of miscalibration, which is checked instead by confirming a sharp target at a drum setting of zero with an empty aperture. Because the ocular has been set correctly, sphere readings carry no offset. No manual correction to the drum is required or wanted.
  148. The correct answer is A (The eyepiece was never focused for this candidate's own eye): an unfocused ocular produces a constant offset in the same direction on every drum reading, which corrupts sphere while leaving cylinder intact because cylinder is a difference between two readings sharing that offset. Seating lenses front surface down gives front vertex power, an error that varies with the power and thickness of each lens rather than staying constant across ten of them. A low lens table tilts the lens and tends to add spurious cylinder instead. Reading the left lens first only risks recording values against the wrong eye.
  149. The correct answer is C (Accommodation can hold the image sharp past the true focus point): once the eye has locked onto a sharp reticle it can maintain apparent clarity while the ocular is turned further, so the operator settles on a setting more minus than the true one and carries that error into every sphere. Stopping at the first point of clarity when approaching from the plus side avoids that entirely. The ocular thread is not damaged by extra turning. Neither the visibility of the target nor the drum zero depends on how far the ocular has been turned.
  150. The correct answer is D (They are front vertex powers, not the back vertex powers specified): the instrument measures from whichever surface touches the stop, so a lens mounted the wrong way round yields front vertex power, which differs from the prescribed back vertex power on any lens of real power or thickness. The error is largest on strong plus lenses in thick blanks and can hide inside tolerance on weak ones. Reversing the lens mirrors an oblique axis, so it reads as 180 minus the true value rather than being displaced by 90 degrees, and the cylinder sign is genuinely unchanged. The target still focuses cleanly, which is exactly why the mistake goes unnoticed.
  151. The correct answer is B (Temples opened flat and away, back surface against the lens stop): opening the temples flat and directing them away from the operator lays the frame naturally on the table with the back surface presented to the stop, which is what the instrument is calibrated to measure from. Any arrangement that puts the front surface against the stop produces front vertex power instead. Folding the temples stops the frame front sitting square and lets it tip on the table. Resting the back surface on the table rather than against the stop leaves the lens outside the light path.
  152. The correct answer is C (Powers being written against the wrong eye on the recording sheet): working right lens then left on every pair, every time, removes the chance of transcribing an accurate reading into the wrong row, which loses marks even though the measurement itself was correct. Sphere drift across a set of pairs points to an ocular problem rather than to reading order. A 90 degree axis error comes from misreading the wheel against the orientation of the focused lines. Front vertex power comes from mounting the lens the wrong way round on the stop.
  153. The correct answer is A (A tilted lens is measured obliquely, which adds cylindrical effect): light passing obliquely through a lens behaves as though the lens carried extra cylinder, so a frame that is not square to the instrument axis produces a spurious cylinder and a target that never sharpens fully. Raising the table until the lower eyewires rest evenly restores a square presentation and removes the false reading. A low table does shift the target vertically, but vertical displacement on its own does not create cylinder. The lens stop does not warp lenses, and the ocular setting is untouched by table height.
  154. The correct answer is A (Clean it first, because scattered light makes sharp focus impossible to judge): a greasy or sprayed surface scatters the illuminated target, so the operator cannot identify the exact drum position at which the lines turn black and continuous, and judging that point is the whole basis of the reading. Surface film does not change the power of the lens, but it destroys the ability to measure that power, so reading through it is not safe. Table height has nothing to do with surface contamination. The reticle sits inside the eyepiece and is not blurred by dirt on the lens.
  155. The correct answer is D (Concave surface on the stop, held steady by the holding arm): the back surface of a conventional meniscus spectacle lens is the concave side, and it must contact the stop so that back vertex power is measured, with the spring loaded holding arm pressing the lens flat against the aperture. The lens table supports the lower eyewires of a glazed frame and gives an uncut lens nothing useful to rest on. Presenting the convex front surface to the stop would return front vertex power, a different quantity from the one a prescription specifies.
  156. The correct answer is B (The weak lens, because the vertex difference may stay within tolerance): the gap between front and back vertex power grows with both power and thickness, so on a low power lens the difference can fall inside the sphere tolerance while on a strong plus lens in a thick blank it will not. That is what makes reversed seating dangerous, because the error is silent on exactly the lenses least likely to reveal it. Thickness does nothing to improve target sharpness. Vertex difference depends on power and thickness rather than diameter, and a reversed lens still focuses cleanly.
  157. The correct answer is C (Not permitted, since automated equipment is excluded from the examination): candidates may bring their own equipment only when the moderator authorizes it in advance, and automated instruments fall outside what can be authorized, so every power reading in the neutralization section is taken on a manual instrument. No arrangement involving printed output makes an automated unit acceptable. Adding a manual cross check does not change the exclusion either. A faulty assigned instrument is reported to the moderator and replaced rather than substituted with an automated one.
  158. The correct answer is D (Book bench time on a manual instrument to build physical fluency): the skill being tested is physical rather than intellectual, so hand movements, focusing judgement and drum reading need to become automatic before exam day, leaving thinking capacity for the recording sheet and the clock. Which target set a manufacturer designates as the sphere line varies between models, so memorizing one convention is unreliable and is settled instead on the assigned instrument. Transposition practice is worth having but does not build instrument handling. Reading manuals is no substitute for time at the bench.
  159. The correct answer is B (It removes operator focusing by reading the target on a sensor): an automated unit projects the target onto a sensor array and calculates sphere, cylinder, axis, add and prism electronically, so no human judgement of sharpness enters the reading and no ocular has to be set for the operator. It still reports back vertex power and still requires the lens to be seated correctly against a stop. Displayed results appear in whichever cylinder form the unit is configured for, so transposition can still be needed before recording.
  160. The correct answer is A (Every power reading in the practical section is taken manually): automated equipment is not permitted at the examination, so however a candidate's workplace is equipped, all lens powers must be read on a manual instrument assigned on the day. Manual instruments are not a fallback used only when automation fails, they are the only option available in that room. No power threshold exists below or above which manual reading becomes optional. Theory is assessed in the written section, while the manual reading itself is assessed at the bench.
  161. The correct answer is D (Manual equipment may be brought if the moderator authorizes it first): the allowance covers manual equipment only and depends on advance authorization, so nothing may simply be carried in and used at the bench. Declaring an item on arrival is not the same as having it authorized beforehand. Personal equipment is not banned outright, since authorized manual items are accepted. The allowance is not limited to one section's tools either, but automated instruments are excluded whatever the section.
  162. The correct answer is C (The instrument does not read zero and should be reported): an empty aperture should give a sharp target at a drum setting of zero once the ocular is set for the operator, so a consistent offset points to the instrument rather than the eye, and the moderator should be told before readings are taken and marks are lost. The ocular has already been set correctly here, which rules out an accommodation error. A contaminated stop would degrade sharpness rather than move the focus point cleanly to a new drum position. Lens table height cannot shift where the target focuses.
  163. The correct answer is A (The instrument's own optics, including the eyepiece and stop aperture): a haze present on every lens that survives cleaning the lenses themselves is coming from the instrument, so the ocular glass and the aperture surfaces should be inspected and cleaned, and the moderator told if the unit remains unusable. A slipped axis scale would give a wrong axis rather than a hazy image. Marking pins affect only where the dots land on the lens. A sticking drum detent would make the drum feel rough without clouding the target.
  164. The correct answer is B (Lift the lens clear, clean the next lens, reseat it squarely): lifting each lens off the stop rather than dragging it across protects both the coating and the aperture, cleaning the next lens removes the film that makes focus impossible to judge, and reseating squarely prevents the oblique presentation that adds false cylinder. Leaving the drum in plus and wiping the stop does nothing about the lens about to be measured. Leaving the marking pins down risks marking a lens that is only being positioned. Setting the axis wheel to a fixed value between pairs wastes time and protects nothing.
  165. The correct answer is D (Read a lens of known power and compare against its marked value): only a known power tests the drum scale away from zero, which is where reading errors actually occur, and the same check reveals which set of target lines focuses first on that particular model. Reading one unknown lens twice proves repeatability rather than accuracy, since a scale offset repeats faithfully. Smooth drum motion is a mechanical check that says nothing about calibration. Confirming a sharp target at zero with an empty aperture is worth doing but verifies the instrument at a single point only.
  166. The correct answer is D (Clean the pins, recharge the ink, and remark the lens properly): the dots carry real measurements, since the centre dot fixes the reference point used for optical centre height and centre separation, so smeared marks must be cleaned off and replaced rather than worked around. Estimating a centre position from smeared marks builds an error into every measurement taken from them afterward. Table height sets the vertical position of the lens and does not control pin pressure. Dot quality is a mechanical and inking matter, unrelated to how the ocular is set.
  167. The correct answer is C (Reset the eyepiece, drum and table before any lens is seated): every one of those settings was made for a different operator and a different frame, so the ocular must be refocused for this candidate, the drum returned to zero and checked for a sharp target with the aperture empty, and the table set to suit the first frame. Restoring the previous candidate's settings afterward serves no purpose at all. Leaving the ocular as found carries another person's focus error into every sphere. Settings left by a previous user are normal and are not grounds for calling the instrument faulty.
  168. The correct answer is A (Fit the auxiliary high power lens and apply its stated conversion): the drum on many manual instruments covers roughly plus and minus 20 dioptres, and powers beyond that range are read with the auxiliary lens supplied with the instrument, converting the result as the manufacturer directs. Turning the lens over would put the front surface on the stop, giving front vertex power and still leaving the power outside the range. Recording the end of the drum travel as an approximation gives a value that is simply wrong. Table height does not extend the measuring range.
  169. The correct answer is B (The crazed coating scatters light, so the focus point stays unclear): a cracked or crazed coating breaks up the transmitted target in a way cleaning cannot fix, so the sensible response is to take the best reading available and note the condition of the lens rather than chase a sharpness the surface cannot deliver. The instrument cannot be at fault when the fellow lens read cleanly on it moments earlier with the same ocular setting. Reversed seating gives a wrong value but still a sharp target. Prism displaces the target sideways without destroying its sharpness.
  170. The correct answer is C (A reversed lens focuses sharply but reports the wrong vertex power): mounting the front surface against the stop produces a clean sharp target at a drum position corresponding to front vertex power, so the fault appears as a wrong number and belongs on a list of accuracy errors rather than focusing errors. A reversed lens seats and holds perfectly well against the aperture. Nothing about reversing a lens doubles the target image. Sideways displacement of the target comes from prism in the lens, not from which surface faces the stop.
  171. The correct answer is A (Clean the lens, check seating, refocus the eyepiece, consider high power): the cheapest and commonest causes come first, so surface film, an unsquare or reversed lens, and an ocular set for another eye are eliminated before reaching for the auxiliary high power lens or concluding the lens cannot be read. Marking and measuring the dots is a later step that assumes a usable reading already exists. Reporting the instrument and restarting the section is a last resort that costs far more time than it saves. Rotating the axis wheel and reversing the lens changes readings without addressing why the focus is poor.
  172. The correct answer is D (Remove the lens and check the reticle and target at zero): with an empty aperture a sharp reticle and a sharp target at a drum setting of zero prove that the ocular suits this operator and that the instrument itself is sound, which leaves the lens as the cause. Trying the fellow lens is a reasonable second step but an unfocused ocular would affect both lenses equally and hide the real problem. Marking the lens tells the candidate nothing about focus quality. Rotating the axis wheel tests line continuity, which already assumes a target that can be focused.
  173. The correct answer is C (The lens is not held firmly and square against the lens stop): a rimless front gives the table little to rest on, so the lens rocks against the aperture, its tilt changes between attempts, the target smears and the readings will not repeat. Using the holding arm and supporting the front so it sits square resolves it. An ocular setting does not wander on its own once it has been threaded into place. A slipping drum would move the focus point without smearing the image. A loose reticle is rare and would blur the crosshair rather than the target.
  174. The correct answer is B (It is familiarization time given before the section timer begins running): the examination allows a period with the assigned equipment before each section's clock starts, so handling an unfamiliar instrument does not eat into the working time for the five pairs. Nothing done in that period is scored, so it is used for preparation rather than performance. Checks such as setting the ocular and confirming a sharp target at zero still have to be made, whether in that period or as the section opens. The period is given to every candidate, not only to those who ask for it.
  175. The correct answer is B (Which set of target lines corresponds to the first meridian focused): manufacturers differ in whether the bold single line or the group of closely spaced lines is designated the sphere line, so that association is confirmed on the assigned model with a lens of known power rather than carried across from another instrument. Every lensometer is calibrated to read back vertex power from the surface presented to the stop. The axis wheel is engraved the same way on every manual instrument, so that is not the feature that varies between models. Cylinder is always the difference between the two drum readings, whatever the target pattern looks like.
  176. The correct answer is D (Learn the routine generally, then verify model specific details on arrival): the procedure for reading a lens is common to manual instruments, but drum step size, target appearance and control placement vary, so the sound approach is a routine that does not depend on one machine plus a short check of the one actually assigned. Practising on a single model without ever confirming what transfers leaves a candidate exposed on the day. A familiar model cannot be requested, because assignment is random. The moderator supervises the section rather than teaching the controls.
  177. The correct answer is A (Set the eyepiece, confirm zero, and read one lens of known power): those three actions establish that the ocular suits this operator, that the instrument reads plano with an empty aperture, and that the drum scale and target behave as expected on a power that is already known. Marking a lens and measuring the dots comes later and assumes a trustworthy power reading already exists. Handling the frames themselves does nothing for instrument accuracy. Attempting to read pairs before the timer starts wastes the one chance to check the instrument.
  178. The correct answer is C (Report the fault to the moderator before the section timer starts): familiarization exists so that equipment problems surface before scored time begins, and the moderator is the person who resolves them, whether by repair or by reassigning an instrument. Moving to another instrument without permission ignores the fact that equipment is assigned rather than chosen. Holding a slipping drum steady by hand cannot give reliable readings across ten lenses. Noting the fault on the recording sheet does nothing to repair the values, which are marked on what they say.
  179. The correct answer is B (turn the drum toward minus and stop where the first set sharpens): starting at the plus end and travelling toward minus brings the more plus meridian into focus first, and that meridian is recorded as the sphere. The second set then focuses at a more minus setting, so the difference between the two readings is negative and the cylinder comes out in minus form without any conversion. Travelling toward plus focuses the more minus meridian first and produces a plus cylinder reading that then has to be transposed. Setting the axis wheel to a fixed number or returning the drum to zero does not begin the power reading at all.
  180. The correct answer is D (the more plus or less minus meridian, read as the sphere value): minus cylinder means the sphere is the more plus meridian and the cylinder is the negative difference to the other meridian. Because the drum is turned from the plus end toward minus, the more plus meridian focuses first and is recorded as the sphere, so the routine delivers minus cylinder automatically. Focusing the greater minus meridian first gives a plus cylinder reading that must then be transposed. The boldness of a line set is a manufacturer's choice that varies between instruments, and the axis wheel position does not decide which meridian is read as sphere.
  181. The correct answer is A (OD -2.75 -1.25 x 090, taking the first focus as sphere): the first meridian to focus is the sphere, so the sphere is -2.75, and the cylinder is the signed difference, -4.00 minus -2.75, which is -1.25. The axis is read straight off the wheel at the sphere focus, giving 090 written as three digits. Treating the second reading as the sphere gives -4.00 and misstates the lens by 1.25 dioptres. Writing the axis as 180 is the classic 90 degree misread, and adding the two drum readings together to make -6.75 of cylinder has no basis in the procedure.
  182. The correct answer is C (OD -1.50 DS, since a spherical lens focuses both sets together): when both sets of target lines reach sharp focus at one drum position and the axis wheel makes no difference, the lens has the same power in every meridian and is spherical. The result is recorded as the sphere with DS or the word sphere, and no cylinder or axis is written. Adding a small cylinder because one is usually present invents power the lens does not have. Leaving the axis blank without stating DS reads as an unfinished entry, and doubling the reading across both meridians describes a lens that was never measured.
  183. The correct answer is C (rotate the axis wheel until the lines are unbroken and evenly spaced): broken or unevenly spaced lines at best drum focus mean the target assembly is not aligned with the meridian being measured. Turning the axis wheel while adjusting the drum brings the lines to a single sharp, continuous, evenly spaced image, and the wheel then reads the axis. Accepting a further 0.25 of minus records a power the lens does not have and leaves the axis wrong as well. Marking the lens at this stage fixes an unconfirmed reading, and moving the lens table changes the vertical position of the target rather than the alignment of the lines.
  184. The correct answer is A (OS +0.75 -2.00 x 180, the cylinder being the signed difference): the first focus is the sphere at +0.75, and the cylinder is -1.25 minus +0.75, which is -2.00. The axis wheel reading at the sphere focus is 180, so the entry is OS +0.75 -2.00 x 180. Subtracting the smaller magnitude from the larger to obtain -0.50 ignores the sign change as the drum crosses zero, a frequent slip on lenses that straddle plano. Using -1.25 as the sphere records the wrong meridian first, and moving the axis to 090 is the standard 90 degree misread.
  185. The correct answer is D (the reading arrives in plus cylinder form and must be transposed): approaching from the minus side focuses the more minus meridian first, so that meridian becomes the sphere and the difference to the second meridian is positive. The reading is not wrong, it is simply in the other form, and it has to be transposed before it goes on a sheet that expects minus cylinder. Back vertex power is still being measured, because that depends on the lens sitting back surface against the stop rather than on drum direction. The axis is read from the wheel in the usual way and the cylinder magnitude is unchanged.
  186. The correct answer is B (check the wheel reading against the orientation of the focused lines): a 90 degree axis error is instantly visible if the recorded number is compared with the direction of the lines that were actually sharp, and that relationship is confirmed on the assigned instrument during familiarization. Doing this before writing the figure catches the error while the lens is still seated and the target still visible. Averaging two wheel readings simply averages the same mistake twice. The axis is read at the sphere focus rather than after marking, and counting degrees from a fixed starting point adds arithmetic without checking anything.
  187. The correct answer is A (OD +0.50 -2.25 x 020, subtracting 90 from an axis above 90): transposition adds the sphere and cylinder algebraically, so -1.75 plus +2.25 gives a new sphere of +0.50, then the cylinder sign flips to -2.25. The axis changes by 90, subtracting when the original axis is above 90, so 110 becomes 020. Adding 90 to give 200 falls outside the 1 to 180 scale and shows the direction rule was not applied. Leaving the sphere at -1.75 skips the algebraic step entirely, and keeping the cylinder positive fails to change the form at all.
  188. The correct answer is C (the optical centre of the lens, also called the major reference point): with the target centred on the reticle crosshairs, the middle pin marks where the optical axis of the instrument passed through the lens, which on a single vision lens is the optical centre. That point is the reference for centre distance, centre height and any check for unwanted prism. The geometric centre of the uncut blank and the boxing centre of the eyewire are frame and blank references that only coincide with the optical centre by chance. The top of a segment is a multifocal landmark and has nothing to do with the middle dot on a single vision lens.
  189. The correct answer is B (from the centre dot on one lens to the centre dot opposite): the separation of the optical centres is the distance between the two middle dots, and comparing it with the specified distance PD shows whether horizontal prism has been induced. Measuring between lens edges gives a frame dimension, not an optical one, and changes with the shape and size of the eye. The outer dots lie on the horizontal reference line rather than on the optical axis, so measuring between them gives a figure offset from the true centre separation. Measuring from the bridge centre to one dot gives a monocular value, which is half the answer being asked for.
  190. The correct answer is D (the centres are 4 mm wide, which exceeds the 2.5 mm allowance): 68 mm measured against 64 mm specified is a separation 4 mm wider than ordered, and 4 mm is well past the 2.5 mm allowed between the reference points and the specified distance PD. The pair therefore fails on centre position and unwanted horizontal prism must be suspected. Calling the centres narrow reverses the direction, since a larger measured separation means the centres sit further apart than the patient's PD. Recording a 2 mm difference misreads the arithmetic, and describing 4 mm as covered by a 2.5 mm allowance ignores the limit given.
  191. The correct answer is D (write every axis as three digits, so 5 becomes 005): padding the axis to three digits makes 005 impossible to confuse with 050 and removes the whole class of misread axis figures on a handwritten sheet. The convention costs nothing and applies to every axis value, so 90 is written 090 and 5 is written 005. Adding a degree symbol does not stop 5 being read as 50 once the digit smudges. Placing the axis before the sphere breaks the sphere, cylinder, axis order another optician expects, and rounding to the nearest ten degrees throws away real accuracy in a section where axis carries marks.
  192. The correct answer is A (+0.75 +1.75 x 155, adding 90 to an axis below 90): transposing +2.50 -1.75 x 065 gives a new sphere of +2.50 plus -1.75, which is +0.75, and the cylinder sign flips to +1.75. The axis moves by 90, adding when the original is 90 or less, so 065 becomes 155. Leaving the axis at 065 describes a different lens even though the powers were converted correctly. Adding the cylinder to the sphere without regard to sign gives +4.25 and shows the algebraic step reversed, and keeping the cylinder negative produces no change of form at all.
  193. The correct answer is C (from the lowest point of the lens in the frame upward): optical centre height is measured from the lowest point of the lens as glazed in the frame up to the centre dot, which is the same reference used for segment and fitting cross heights. Using one consistent lower reference lets the two lenses be compared with each other and with the patient's pupil position. Measuring down from the top of the eyewire changes with the depth of the shape and is not the recorded convention. The bridge centre gives a horizontal measurement rather than a height, and the datum line is a horizontal frame reference, not the lower edge of the lens.
  194. The correct answer is B (the 2 mm difference is twice the allowance, so vertical imbalance exists): 24 mm against 22 mm is a 2 mm difference in the height of the two reference points, and the mounted standard allows no more than 1 mm between them. The wearer therefore looks through the two lenses at different vertical positions relative to their optical centres, which is what vertical imbalance means. Dotting each lens separately is normal practice and does not excuse the difference. Averaging the two heights hides the very thing being tested, and the height limit applies to the reference points of any mounted pair, not only to pairs carrying segments.
  195. The correct answer is B (front vertex power, which differs from the prescribed back vertex power): lensometers are calibrated to read from the surface pressed against the lens stop, so a lens turned around gives front vertex power. Prescriptions and work orders specify back vertex power, and on a strong plus lens the two figures differ by enough to move the reading outside tolerance even though the target looks perfectly sharp. Back vertex power cannot be what was measured when the front surface is against the stop, and reversing the lens does not rotate the axis by 90. Equivalent power is a different quantity again and is not what any lensometer displays.
  196. The correct answer is C (OD -2.00 -0.75 x 005, sphere first and axis in three digits): the conventions are eye label, sphere with its sign and two decimal places, then cylinder with its sign, then the axis last as three digits after an x. That entry can be ordered from without a single query. Writing -2 instead of -2.00 and 5 instead of 005 invites both a smudge misread and doubt about a missing digit. Placing the axis between the sphere and cylinder breaks the expected order, and leaving the minus signs to be understood makes the strongest possible ambiguity out of a value that must never be guessed.
  197. The correct answer is A (-1.12 -0.75 x 075, the difference between the two drum readings): the first focus is the sphere at -1.12, and the cylinder is -1.87 minus -1.12, which is -0.75. The axis is read from the wheel at that first focus and recorded as 075. Using -1.87 as the sphere records the second meridian in the sphere position and shifts the whole lens by three quarters of a dioptre. Writing -0.87 borrows the fractional part of the second reading rather than taking the difference, and moving the axis to 165 is a 90 degree misread of a wheel that was sitting at 075.
  198. The correct answer is D (OS -1.50 DS, because the lens actually measures as a sphere): neutralization records what the lens is, not what was ordered, and both sets of lines focusing together through a full sweep of the axis wheel is the definition of a spherical lens. The entry is the sphere with DS or the word sphere and nothing else. Copying the cylinder from the work order records a power the instrument did not find and defeats the purpose of the reading. Adding a token -0.12 to match a tolerance figure invents data, and leaving the lens unrecorded while the work order is queried surrenders marks for a reading already complete.
  199. The correct answer is C (OS -4.50 -1.50 x 135, adding 90 to an axis below 90): adding the sphere and cylinder algebraically gives -6.00 plus +1.50, which is -4.50, the cylinder sign flips to -1.50, and the axis moves from 045 to 135 because an axis of 90 or less has 90 added. Subtracting the cylinder from the sphere to reach -7.50 reverses the algebra and overstates the lens by three dioptres. Leaving the axis at 045 converts the powers correctly while describing a different lens on the sheet. Keeping the cylinder positive means no transposition has taken place at all.
  200. The correct answer is B (the tilted lens is adding apparent cylinder and smearing the target): a lens that is not square to the instrument axis presents an oblique section of its own surfaces, which introduces cylinder that is not in the lens and prevents a clean line focus. Resting the lens table properly under both eyewires and seating the back surface flat against the stop restores a sharp target. Crazed coating does scatter light, but the tilt described is the stated cause here. Low power lenses sit nowhere near the limit of the drum range, and an engaged marking device stamps the lens rather than blurring the target.
  201. The correct answer is D (the patient looks above the optical centre and picks up unwanted prism): if the dotted centre sits below the pupil, the visual axis in straight ahead gaze passes through the lens above the optical centre, and any point away from the optical centre carries prism whose base direction depends on the sign of the lens. That is why centre height is recorded and compared to the pupil, not just written in a box. Saying the patient looks below the centre reverses the geometry described. No prism at all would require the centre and pupil to coincide, and near PD is a horizontal measurement that has no bearing on this vertical comparison.
  202. The correct answer is A (the optical centre sits 3 mm below the pupil centre on that lens): both figures use the same reference, the lowest point of the lens, so the comparison is a simple subtraction of 26 mm minus 23 mm, giving 3 mm. The smaller figure belongs to the dot, so the optical centre is the lower of the two and the patient looks above it. Reversing the direction and calling the centre 3 mm high misreads which value is larger. There is no boxing allowance to subtract from two heights taken from the same reference, and adding the two figures to get 49 mm has no physical meaning here.
  203. The correct answer is A (label the sheet first, then read right lens before left every time): fixing the eye labels on the sheet before any reading is taken, and always working right lens then left, removes the decision that creates transposed entries in the first place. The habit is mechanical, which is exactly what is wanted under time pressure on five pairs. Deciding afterwards which power belongs to which eye is guesswork whenever the two lenses are similar. Confirming against the work order does not apply when a pair is being neutralized without one, and starting on the left simply relabels the same risk.
  204. The correct answer is D (+4.00 -0.75 x 085, subtracting 90 from an axis above 90): adding the sphere and cylinder gives +3.25 plus +0.75, which is +4.00, the cylinder becomes -0.75, and 175 is above 90 so 90 is subtracted to give 085.   205. The correct answer is B (because two decimal places remove any doubt about a smudged digit): writing -2.00 rather than -2 means a marker never has to decide whether a faint mark after the number was a digit, a decimal point or a pen slip. Every power value on the sheet carries two decimals for the same reason, and the habit costs no time. Drums graduated in 0.12 steps display whole numbers perfectly well, so that is not the reason. Whole numbers do not imply a sphere, since DS or the word sphere is what states that, and laboratories will process an order written without decimals while still being exposed to the same misreading risk.
  206.   207. The correct answer is D (distance PD from the centre dots, centre height, and base curve): Section 2 records power plus the physical aspects normally taken with a pair, which for a single vision pair means the separation of the dotted optical centres, the height of each centre, and the base curve read with a lens clock. Each of those carries marks and each takes seconds once the lenses are dotted. Segment height and near addition belong to a pair that actually has a segment. Refractive index, tint density and the patient's acuity are not measurements this section asks a candidate to record.
  208. The correct answer is A (OD +1.00 -3.00 x 180, adding 90 because the axis is 90): the sphere becomes -2.00 plus +3.00, which is +1.00, the cylinder flips to -3.00, and the rule is to add 90 when the original axis is 90 or less, so 090 becomes 180. An axis of exactly 90 is the edge case the rule is written to settle. Subtracting to reach 000 gives a value that is not used, since the scale runs from 1 to 180 and the horizontal meridian is written 180. Combining the sphere and cylinder as minus to reach -5.00 reverses the algebra, and an axis at 90 does move on transposition like any other.
  209. The correct answer is C (a horizontal reference line through the centre, 180 degrees apart): the two outer pins stamp dots either side of the middle dot on the horizontal meridian of the instrument, so the three dots together define a horizontal reference line through the optical centre. That line shows whether the lens is sitting level and gives a horizontal datum for checking centre positions and tilt. Segment height is measured from the segment itself, not from these dots. The lens clock is placed anywhere on the surface being measured rather than on the outer dots, and the dots say nothing about the extent of any optical zone.
  210. Coming back means finding the pair, reseating it, recentring the target and rebuilding your mental place, and that is where candidates lose the section. Reading all powers first still requires every frame to be handled again for dots and heights. Measuring all the PDs in one pass has the same backtracking cost, and reordering the pairs by difficulty does nothing about the time lost to repeated handling.

  212. The correct answer is C (-4.25, the sphere with half the cylinder power added to it): spherical equivalent is the sphere plus half the cylinder, so -3.50 plus half of -1.50 gives -3.50 plus -0.75, which is -4.25. The sign of the cylinder is carried into the halving, which is why a minus cylinder always moves the equivalent in the minus direction. Adding the whole cylinder to reach -5.00 doubles the intended correction. Moving the sphere in the plus direction to -2.75 subtracts instead of adds, and quoting -3.50 ignores the cylinder entirely, which is not what a spherical equivalent means.
  213. The correct answer is A (the procedure is defined by the order the lines focus, not appearance): the routine says to turn from plus toward minus, take the first meridian that focuses as the sphere, take the second as the other meridian, and subtract. Nothing in that sequence depends on whether the first set to focus is a single bold line or a group of three, so both candidates get the same correct result on different instruments. Assuming the bold line always carries the sphere ties the method to one manufacturer's convention and will eventually reverse a reading. Calibration bodies do not standardize target appearance, and the axis wheel aligns the target rather than correcting anything.
  214. That takes seconds and removes any doubt before the timer starts. Asking the moderator for a make and model still leaves the candidate guessing at the convention. Setting the drum to zero checks the instrument's zero rather than the target behaviour, and practising the marking device is useful but tells you nothing about which lines focus first.

That distinction is exactly what a marker or a second optician needs from the sheet. Leaving the cylinder and axis boxes blank looks like an unfinished reading and invites a query. The power reading already taken is unaffected, but the dots are used for measurement rather than for power, so saying there is no effect is wrong. The axis does not shift with a lateral nudge, and the dots have no relationship to the frame centre.

Any difference means one eye picks up vertical prism the other does not, which is the imbalance the mounted standard limits to 1 mm between reference points. Unequal pantoscopic tilt can contribute to a height difference but is not what the two measurements themselves report. Base curve is read with a lens clock, and near PD is a horizontal measurement that this vertical comparison cannot address.

This is why the axis is cross checked against the orientation of the focused lines before anything is written. Shifting the axis by 90 degrees does not cancel itself out, since the two meridians of the lens carry different powers. The sphere is not made stronger by the error, and a two dioptre cylinder allows only a couple of degrees of axis variation, so 90 degrees is far outside any tolerance.

The test is simple, since a sphere brings both sets of lines up together at one drum setting when the reading is taken slowly. An axis wheel left between positions makes the lines ragged but does not create a second focus point on a sphere. The lens table height affects target position rather than focus quality, and an instrument that reads a known sphere correctly on a careful recheck does not need recalibration.
  224. Whichever system is chosen, it is used from the first pair to the last. R and L are perfectly acceptable in Canadian practice as long as they are used consistently. The reading order of the lenses is a separate habit that the labelling system does not change, and the presence or absence of a cylinder has no bearing on which eye labels are used.

  227. Progressive engravings are cut in at manufacture and cannot wear off, so a lens with a visible add boundary and no engravings is not a progressive.   228. The correct answer is B (across the straight top edge of the segment, side to side): flat top segments are named by the width of that straight top edge, so FT-28 means 28 mm across the top. Measuring across the widest part of the curve gives a larger figure that will not match the ordered size. Measuring from the top edge down to the bottom of the curve gives segment depth, a different quantity. A diagonal measurement corresponds to nothing the lab ever specifies.
  229. The correct answer is A (an executive, also called a Franklin or one piece bifocal): only the executive has a dividing line that runs the entire width of the lens with a ridge that can be felt, and the design is thick and heavy because both halves carry full aperture surfaces. A flat top segment stops well short of the lens edges on both sides. A round segment shows a circular boundary with no straight edge at all. A trifocal would show two lines, and polishing a segment line away is not something a lab does.
  230.   231. The correct answer is C (round 22 segment, a style still seen on repairs but rarely ordered): a boundary that is a complete circle with no straight edge anywhere is a round segment, and round segments are commonly 22 mm or 24 mm across. The FT prefix belongs only to flat top segments, which by definition have a straight upper edge. A blended segment shows a smudged blur band rather than a clean visible circle. An executive line crosses the whole lens and is never circular.
  232.   233. The correct answer is D (the added surface sits at the reference plane, so the back contribution cancels): with the front against the stop, the segment surface lies at the measuring plane and the common back surface contributes equally to both readings, so it drops out of the subtraction. Read from the back, the front segment sits a lens thickness away and is magnified differently in the segment than in the distance portion, which is why the difference of two back readings is not the true add. Target sharpness and how the stop grips the lens have nothing to do with it, and grinding order does not determine measurement order.
  234. Both the distance and segment readings for the add are then taken from that side. The setup with the temples still pointing away is the ordinary back vertex position and simply repeats the reading already taken. Raising the lens table changes where the target sits, not which surface is measured.
  235. Subtracting in the reverse direction gives a negative figure, and no add is ever negative. Adding the two readings has no optical meaning and produces a number far larger than any bifocal add. Taking the segment reading alone and flipping its sign ignores the distance power the segment is built upon.

  239. The correct answer is A (the 0.30 D error exceeds the allowance, so a good lens looks faulty): the two methods differ by 0.30 D, which is well beyond the 0.12 D permitted for an add up to +4.00 D, so back vertex subtraction can condemn a correctly made lens or produce a recorded figure the examiner marks wrong. Claiming the error sits inside the allowance ignores the arithmetic, since 0.30 is more than double 0.12. The allowance does not double because a lens is plus powered. Add power is explicitly covered by the standard, so it is not untested.
  240. The correct answer is C (from the back, because the add is read from the surface carrying it): the governing rule is to measure the add from the surface that carries the addition, so a back surface add is read with the back against the stop. Insisting on the front for every add applies the rule mechanically rather than understanding it, and would reintroduce the thickness error the method exists to remove. Thickness effects do not vanish simply because the add is on the back. Sign of the distance power has no bearing on which surface carries the segment.
  241. The correct answer is B (distance power is specified as a back vertex reading and read from the back): prescriptions and the standard both define distance power at the back vertex, so the distance figure must come from a back surface reading even when the add was taken from the front. Cylinder convention on the drum does not decide which side of the lens is measured. Reading order between distance and segment is a matter of workflow, not of validity. Halving a front vertex figure has no optical basis at all.
  242. The correct answer is A (the two agree only in thin low minus lenses, so flip every lens): in a thin low minus lens the thickness effect is smaller than the drum can resolve, so the two methods happen to agree, but that is a property of this one lens and not proof of the method. Agreement is not guaranteed whenever the segment is on the front, since the error grows with plus power and substance. The size of the add does not control the error, the thickness and front curve do. Instrument resolution is not the reason the readings matched.
  243. The correct answer is D (+2.75 D, which is 0.25 D above the order and outside tolerance): -3.75 minus -6.50 equals +2.75, and that sits 0.25 D above the ordered +2.50, well beyond the 0.12 D allowed for an add up to +4.00 D. Calling the same 0.25 D difference acceptable misreads the allowance. Summing the readings to give +10.25 is a sign and method error rolled together. Rounding the measured figure back to the ordered add hides the fault the verification exists to find.
  244. The correct answer is C (right +2.25 D and left +2.50 D, so both must be recorded): the right add is +1.00 minus -1.25, which is +2.25, and the left is +0.75 minus -1.75, which is +2.50, so the two eyes genuinely differ. Assuming a single shared add loses a real difference the examiner is checking for. Recording the raw segment readings of +1.00 and +0.75 ignores the distance power those segments sit on. Swapping the figures between the eyes puts the higher add on the wrong side.
  245. The correct answer is A (+1.25 D, half the near add exactly as the ratio states): a 50 percent intermediate means half the near add, and half of +2.50 is +1.25, which is the figure the band should give when read by the front vertex method. Reducing the near add by a fixed half dioptre step gives +2.00 and treats the ratio as a subtraction rather than a proportion. Taking a fifth off the near add applies a ratio that no stock trifocal uses. Treating the 50 percent figure as a flat half dioptre ignores the near add entirely.
  246. The correct answer is B (+1.20 D, forty percent of the measured near add value): 0.40 multiplied by +3.00 gives +1.20, the intermediate add expected on a 40 percent design. Assuming every intermediate is half the near add would predict +1.50 and would flag a correct lens as wrong. Subtracting a fixed 1.25 dioptres treats the ratio as a constant deduction rather than a proportion of the near add. Reading the ratio as a flat 0.40 dioptres ignores the near add value the fraction is applied to.
  247. The correct answer is D (+1.40 D, seventy percent of the near add just measured): 0.70 multiplied by +2.00 gives +1.40, which is what the intermediate band should read once the distance reading is subtracted. Half the near add gives +1.00 and describes a 50 percent design, not the 70 percent design supplied. Treating the ratio as a flat 0.70 dioptres discards the near add value completely. Subtracting seventy hundredths of a dioptre from the near add turns a proportion into a subtraction and gives +1.30.
  248. The correct answer is A (40 percent, since 1.30 divided by 3.25 gives 0.40): dividing the intermediate add by the near add returns the design ratio, and 1.30 divided by 3.25 is exactly 0.40. A 50 percent design would have shown an intermediate of +1.63 against this near add, and a 70 percent design would have shown about +2.28. The result is a clean stock ratio, so there is no odd figure here and no reason to repeat either reading. The check works precisely because the ratio lands on one of the manufactured values.
  249. The correct answer is C (near +2.50 D, intermediate +1.25 D, a 50 percent trifocal): the near add is +0.50 minus -2.00, which is +2.50, and the intermediate add is -0.75 minus -2.00, which is +1.25, giving a ratio of 0.50. Recording +0.75 as the intermediate add uses the raw band reading without subtracting the distance power. Recording +0.50 as the near add makes the same mistake with the near reading. A near add of +1.50 does not follow from these three readings at all.
  250. The correct answer is B (near +1.50 D, intermediate +1.05 D, a 70 percent trifocal): the near add is +2.50 minus +1.00, which is +1.50, the intermediate add is +2.05 minus +1.00, which is +1.05, and 1.05 divided by 1.50 is 0.70. Recording +2.05 as the intermediate add uses the raw band reading instead of the difference. Recording +2.50 as the near add repeats that error on the near segment. A ratio of 50 percent would require an intermediate add of +0.75 against this near add.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (the ratio may be 40 or 70 percent, so read the band): stock trifocals are manufactured at 40, 50 and 70 percent, so assuming half the near add will produce a wrong figure on any design that is not a 50 percent, and the examination expects a measured value. Intermediate adds are recorded on the result sheet for a trifocal, so the reading is not wasted effort. Band depth does not systematically depress the reading. There is no rule permitting calculation above a particular near add.
  2. The correct answer is A (take both readings again before recording, since one of them is wrong): 2.17 divided by 3.50 is about 0.62, which matches no manufactured ratio, so the sensible conclusion is a bad reading rather than an unusual lens. Recording the odd ratio as measured passes a known error onto the sheet when the ratio check exists precisely to catch it. Forcing the intermediate to +1.75 to make a tidy 50 percent is inventing a figure the instrument never showed. Leaving the intermediate blank loses a required entry for a trifocal.
  3. The correct answer is C (height 18 mm and drop 2 mm, two numbers for one segment): height is measured from the lowest point of the aperture up to the segment top, which is 18 mm, while drop is measured from the major reference point down to the segment top, which is 20 minus 18, or 2 mm. Reporting 20 mm as the height quotes the position of the major reference point instead. Reversing the two figures swaps the reference points the measurements start from. A drop of 22 mm would require the major reference point to sit above the top of the lens.
  4. The correct answer is D (5 mm, measured from the major reference point down to the segment top): the major reference point sits 22 mm above the lowest point of the aperture and the segment top sits 17 mm above that same point, so the drop is 22 minus 17, or 5 mm. Adding the height to the half B measurement gives 39 mm and combines two figures that do not belong together. Quoting 17 mm treats drop and height as the same measurement, which is the classic error. Subtracting the height from the full B measurement gives 27 mm and uses the wrong starting reference.
  5. The correct answer is B (drop runs from the major reference point down to the segment top): drop and height start from different references, so an accurate measurement taken from the bottom of the aperture is a segment height and belongs in the height field, not the drop field. The top rim of the frame is not a reference for either measurement. Drop is recorded as a plain positive distance below the major reference point, not as a negative value. Nothing requires the lenses to be removed from the frame to establish drop.
  6. The correct answer is A (at the deepest part of the eyewire groove below the segment): segment height is measured from the lowest point of the lens aperture, which is the bottom of the groove, straight up to the segment top. Starting at the outer rim edge begins below the true aperture, which is why the figure came out too large. The geometric centre of the aperture is a layout reference, not a height reference. The lowest point of the frame front including the bridge has no relationship to where the lens aperture starts.
  7. The correct answer is C (the reading to the straight top edge, which defines segment height): segment height is measured to the straight top edge of a flat top, the line itself, since that is where the add begins for the wearer. Measuring to the lowest point of the curve gives the bottom of the segment, a figure nobody orders or verifies. Averaging two references produces a number that matches no convention and cannot be checked against a work order. Choosing whichever value matches the order fits the measurement to the answer rather than measuring honestly.
  8. The correct answer is B (at the lower lid margin, then confirmed against task and posture): the lower lid margin is the common starting position for the top edge of a flat top, and it is then checked against how the wearer holds the head and what work is being done. Setting the segment top at the pupil centre would place the line across the distance field. The lower rim of the frame sits below the aperture and would put the segment far too low. The horizontal centre line and the B measurement describe frame geometry, not where the segment should sit on a face.
  9. The correct answer is D (near PD 60 mm, 30 mm each eye, the inset applied to each eye): each eye is inset 2 mm, so 32 minus 2 gives 30 mm per eye and a binocular near PD of 60 mm, which is 4 mm less than distance and consistent with normal convergence at 40 cm. Applying the inset once to the binocular figure gives 62 mm and forgets that both eyes converge. Leaving the PD unchanged ignores convergence altogether. Doubling the inset at each eye gives 56 mm, far more convergence than a 40 cm task requires.
  10. The correct answer is A (no, the near PD is 57 mm with 28.5 mm to each eye): each eye insets 2.5 mm, so 31 minus 2.5 gives 28.5 mm per eye and a binocular near PD of 57 mm. The recorded 59.5 mm came from taking the inset off the binocular figure once, which credits only one eye with convergence. A near PD of 59.5 cannot sit alongside monocular values of 28.5, since those two figures do not sum correctly. Doubling the inset at each eye gives 52 mm and overstates convergence at this working distance.
  11. The correct answer is B (the eyes converge for near work, so the near visual point sits nasally): at a 40 cm task the visual axes turn inward, so the point the wearer looks through for near sits nasal to the distance point and the segment is placed to meet it. Glass thickness across the blank has nothing to do with segment position. Pantoscopic tilt is a vertical fitting matter handled by segment height, not by horizontal inset. Frame shape does not dictate inset, since inset is driven by the wearer's convergence.
  12. The correct answer is C (the 1.5 mm difference is outside the allowance, so the pair fails): 19.5 minus 18.0 is 1.5 mm, which is greater than the 1.0 mm permitted between the two segment heights, so the job goes back. Calling that difference acceptable misreads the arithmetic against the stated allowance. The vertical difference allowance applies to the pair, not to each lens considered on its own. No allowance doubles because the two figures happen to straddle a particular value.
  13. The correct answer is A (the left segment tilt is outside the allowance and the pair is returned): the standard measures segment tilt from the 180 line and permits 2 degrees, so a left segment rotated 3 degrees from 180 is out and the pair must go back. Comparing one segment against the other uses the wrong reference, since the reference is 180 and not the fellow lens. Tilt applies to any lined segment with a straight top edge, not to executives alone. A single lens out of tolerance is enough to fail the pair.
  14. The correct answer is D (lay a straight edge across both segment tops and compare against 180): a straight edge laid across the two segment tops shows at once whether they sit level with each other and whether either is rotated away from the 180 line, which is the reference the standard uses. Comparing add figures on the lensometer checks power, not position. Judging the segments against the bridge by eye has no reference line and no reliable resolution. Lens diameters describe the shape of the aperture and say nothing about segment placement.
  15. The correct answer is B (the add is recorded as a separate positive number, not the near total): the convention is distance power in minus cylinder form followed by the add written as a plain positive figure, so this entry belongs as Add +2.00 rather than as a near power. The near total of -0.50 -1.00 x 180 is arithmetically right but answers a question the field did not ask. Cylinder convention is not the fault here, since the distance power was already in the expected form. The axis of 180 is unchanged by the add and does not become 090.
  16. The correct answer is C (record each eye separately, right eye first, stating both add values): the two eyes carry different adds, and the convention is to record each eye on its own line with the right eye first, so both +2.25 and +2.50 must appear. Recording only the higher add loses a real difference between the eyes. Averaging the two adds produces a figure that belongs to neither lens. Noting that the difference is within tolerance sidesteps the requirement to state what was actually measured on each side.
  17. The correct answer is D (segment height 18 mm, naming the reference and the unit used): heights are recorded with the reference named and the unit written out, because 18 alone could equally be a segment height, a fitting cross height or a drop. Relying on a column heading to supply the reference leaves the entry ambiguous once it is read on its own. Adding the unit but not the reference still leaves the reader guessing which vertical measurement was taken. Naming a bare height without the unit fails the other half of the convention.
  18. The correct answer is A (FT-28, naming the flat top style and its top edge width): trade shorthand names the style and the width of the straight top edge together, so a 28 mm straight edged segment is recorded as FT-28. Writing bifocal 28 gives the width but leaves the style unstated, so the entry could describe a round segment of that size. Combining D and round in one entry names two different shapes at once and cannot be filled by any lab. Recording 28 mm as a bare segment width omits the style code the sheet expects.
  19. The correct answer is B (return, because 0.6 mm exceeds the 0.5 mm allowance for segment size): the segment measures 0.6 mm wider than the ordered 28 mm, and the permitted variation in finished segment size is 0.5 mm, so the job goes back. Treating 0.6 mm as inside a 0.5 mm allowance simply misreads the comparison. The segment size allowance applies to finished segments generally and is not restricted to round styles. Demanding an exact match to the ordered figure ignores that a tolerance is allowed at all.
  20. The correct answer is C (the add is out by 0.15 D, which exceeds the allowance, so it fails): 2.40 minus 2.25 is 0.15 D, and an add up to +4.00 D may vary by only 0.12 D, so the lens fails on add power. Calling 0.15 acceptable against a 0.12 allowance reverses the comparison. An error of 0.25 D would follow only from comparing against +2.15 or some other figure the order never specified. The size of a drum step is not the standard, the permitted variation is.
  21. The correct answer is C (it passes, because 0.15 D sits inside the 0.18 D allowance that applies): the ordered add is above +4.00 D, so the larger allowance of 0.18 D governs, and the measured difference of 4.65 minus 4.50, or 0.15 D, falls inside it. Applying the 0.12 D figure uses the band for adds up to +4.00 D and fails a lens that is in fact acceptable. Adds above +4.00 D are not required to match exactly, since the standard states a tolerance for them. Add allowances apply across the whole add range, high adds included.
  22. The correct answer is D (return the job, since the 3 mm error exceeds the 2.5 mm allowance): the finished near PD of 63 mm sits 3 mm from the specified 60 mm, and segment horizontal location may differ from the specified near PD by 2.5 mm, so the pair goes back. Treating 3 mm as inside a 2.5 mm allowance reverses the comparison. Horizontal location tolerances cover lined multifocals as well as progressives, with a wider figure allowed for lined work. Requiring an exact match to the ordered near PD ignores the tolerance the standard grants.
  23. The correct answer is B (record FT-25 and FT-35, the wider segment giving the wider reading field): flat tops are named by the width of the straight top edge, so these are an FT-25 and an FT-35, and the wider segment gives the wearer more horizontal reading field at the same add. Segment width has no effect on add power, which is set by the surface curvature rather than the size of the segment. Recording both as FT-28 substitutes the most common size for the measured one. Flat top designations name width, not depth.
  24. The correct answer is D (segment height, near or distance PD, and base curve for the pair): a multifocal pair carries distance power and add for each eye plus the physical aspects normally recorded, which are segment height, the relevant PD figures, and base curve. Vertex distance, pantoscopic tilt and frame eye size belong to fitting and layout work rather than to the neutralization record. Material, coating and manufacturer are not measurements taken at the lensometer or with a rule. Spherical equivalent and edge thickness are not asked for on a neutralization sheet.
  25. The correct answer is B (Search the surface for permanent engravings over a dark ground with raking light): a progressive has no line, no ledge and no blur ring, so on sight it looks exactly like a single vision lens.
