Every progressive carries permanent engravings cut in at manufacture, so the only safe way to rule a progressive out is to positively confirm that no engravings exist. Recording the lens as single vision without that search is the most costly identification error in this section. Repeating the fingernail test only re-tests a feature a progressive never has, and reading at the geometric centre records a power from a point nobody has verified.
  26. The correct answer is D (The add, the fitting cross height and the monocular pupillary distances): a progressive pair carries several recorded results, and calling it single vision drops every one of the progressive specific entries. A pair with a perfect distance reading and no add is still a lost pair. Base curve is recorded on single vision work as well, so it is not lost by the misidentification. Axis is written in the same minus cylinder form on any lens type, and the marks for a multifocal pair are spread across all the recorded fields rather than concentrated in the distance power.
  27. The correct answer is A (The nasal mark is not the second engraving, since conventional spacing is 34 mm): the two permanent circles sit horizontally 34 mm apart on every conventional progressive design regardless of brand. That fixed spacing is what confirms both marks are genuine engravings rather than one mark and a scratch, so a 29 mm separation means the search is not finished. Corridor length varies between designs but the engraving separation does not shorten with it. A blended bifocal shows a smudged circular boundary and no engravings at all, and permanent engravings are cut in at manufacture and cannot be polished out in normal service.
  28. The correct answer is C (Seventeen millimetres from each circle, midway along the line joining them): the circles sit 34 mm apart, so the midpoint is 34 divided by 2, which is 17 mm from each one. The standard specifies prescribed prism at that point and unwanted prism is checked there. An unequal split such as eleven and twenty three does not fall on the midpoint of a 34 mm separation. Thirty four millimetres from one circle lands on the far side of the other circle entirely, and the point sitting a few millimetres above the midpoint is the fitting cross rather than the prism reference point.
  29. The correct answer is C (The manufacturer or design mark and the add power of the lens): one circle carries a brand mark as a logo, initials or symbol, and the other carries the add engraved without a plus sign and usually without a decimal point, so 250 reads as an add of +2.50 D. The usual arrangement puts the brand mark under the nasal circle and the add under the temporal one, though makers vary, which is why an identifier chart exists. Base curve, centre thickness, corridor length and minimum fitting height are all properties looked up from the design chart once the brand is identified, not figures cut into the lens beside the circles.
  30. The correct answer is A (Add +2.25 D, since the engraved figure omits the sign and decimal): makers cut the add as a bare number, so 225 means +2.25 D, and the same value appears as 2.25 or 22 on other brands. Adds are always recorded as positive figures, never as a minus value and never as the total near power. Reading the digits literally as 22.5 gives a power no progressive is made in. Truncating to the first two characters throws away the quarter dioptre that the engraving actually states.
  31. The correct answer is D (Because makers use different formats, so both figures can mean a +2.00 add): the add is engraved without a plus sign and usually without a decimal point, so a +2.00 add can appear as 200, 2.00 or 20 depending on the maker. The identifier chart for that brand settles which format is in use, and the measured add at the near reference circle confirms it. Corridor length is not what the character group beside the circle states. Some makers do add a further character for material or index, but that does not turn 20 into a different add value, and add engravings appear on progressives right across the add range.
  32. The correct answer is B (Hold the lens over a dark matte surface with light raking across it): the engravings are shallow and scatter light, so they show as fine bright marks only when a single bright source strikes the front surface at a low angle over a dark, non reflective ground. A pale or cluttered bench under flat lighting makes them nearly invisible, which is exactly the condition described, so more of the same overhead light does not help. Wetting the surface floods the engravings rather than picking them out, and the lensometer eyepiece views the instrument target rather than the lens surface.
  33. The correct answer is A (Fog the lens with a short breath and watch the condensation pattern): the engraved marks hold moisture differently from the polished surface, so they stand out clearly for a second or two after fogging, which is long enough to dot them with a marker. This works with no special equipment and does no harm to the lens. Heating a lens in a salt pan is a lens insertion technique and risks the coating. Dry rubbing simply cleans the surface without revealing anything, and running water sheets off too evenly to outline the engravings.
  34. The correct answer is B (It lights the lens correctly over a dark field and often carries a template): these units set the illumination angle and the dark background for you, which is exactly what makes shallow engravings visible, and many have an integral marking template so the layout can be transferred at the same time. That saves time in a section where five pairs share seventy five minutes. The unit does not measure power, so the lensometer is still used for the distance reading and the add. It also has no bearing on the monocular pupillary distances, which remain a required entry on every progressive job.
  35. The correct answer is D (Positively confirming absence of engravings before calling either lens single vision): the failure here is an identification failure, not a power reading failure, so only a proper engraving search fixes it. Confirming there are no engravings, rather than simply failing to notice any, is the step that separates a genuine single vision lens from a progressive. Repeating the distance sphere reading confirms a number that was already correct. Base curve and frame mount type are separate recorded observations that do nothing to reveal whether the lens carries a progressive surface.
  36. The correct answer is D (Line both dots up with the 34 mm reference line printed on the chart): the chart is registered to the engraving separation, so seating both dots on that reference line fixes the lens in the correct position and orientation before anything else is transferred. Once aligned, the fitting cross, prism reference point, distance circle and near circle can all be marked from the chart. Measuring a vertical drop happens later, when the lens is in the frame and the fitting cross height is taken. Seating a dot on the lens stop is a power reading step, and erasing an engraving dot destroys the very reference the chart needs.
  37. The correct answer is C (Fitting cross, prism reference point, distance reference circle and near reference circle): these four are the working points of a progressive layout, and none of them except the prism reference point can be located without the chart for that specific design. Segment top edge and segment drop belong to a lined multifocal, which has a visible segment rather than an inked layout. Boxed centre, datum line and mechanical centre are frame and layout measurements taken from the frame, not from a lens marking chart. Blend zone borders are not marked points on any marking chart.
  38. The correct answer is A (The fitting cross is inked by the lab and wipes off with normal cleaning): only the two circles and the character groups beside them are cut in at manufacture, and every other point in the layout is temporary ink. A pair in wear for six months will have lost that ink long ago, which is normal and says nothing about the quality of the job. The fitting cross is inked on every progressive regardless of prism or add power, so its absence is not a clue to either. Marked points cannot migrate across a lens surface as a coating ages.
  39. The correct answer is B (The offset varies by design, commonly two to four millimetres, and comes from the chart): the fitting cross sits above the prism reference point on conventional designs, but the exact figure belongs to the design and is read from that design's marking chart. Two progressives from one maker can place it at different heights, so a fixed four millimetres will be wrong on some lenses. The fitting cross does not sit below the prism reference point on conventional layouts. Monocular pupillary distance governs where the lens is set in the frame horizontally rather than the internal geometry of the design, and the prism reference point stays fixed midway between the circles whatever the add.
  40. The correct answer is C (Distance reference circle over the stop, back surface against the stop): a prescription states distance power as a back vertex value, so the back surface goes against the stop, and the distance reference point is the place on a progressive where the full distance power is present. The fitting cross locates the lens in front of the pupil and is not the point the standard names for the distance power reading. The near reference circle carries the distance power plus the full add. The midpoint of the two circles is the prism reference point, used for the prism check rather than for the distance sphere and cylinder.
  41. The correct answer is D (The reading is being taken in the corridor, partway through the progression): the corridor is the narrow band of gradually changing power between the fitting cross and the near circle, so a reading there gives a fraction of the add together with the design's unwanted cylinder at a meaningless axis. Targets that will not come to a clean focus in both meridians together are the warning that the reading point is wrong. An unfocused eyepiece blurs both meridians equally rather than creating a false cylinder at a random axis. Warpage and a rotated prism compensator would affect readings taken anywhere on the lens, including at the distance and near reference points.
  42. The correct answer is A (The +1.30 D result is a corridor reading and the near circle must be used): subtracting the distance value from the lower reading gives -0.70 minus -2.00, which is +1.30 D, a figure between zero and the engraved +2.50 add. Any value in that range taken between the fitting cross and the near circle is a partial progression rather than the add. The add is read at the near reference circle, where the full addition is present, and it should then agree with the engraved 250. Permanent engravings are cut at manufacture and belong to the lens they sit on, so a mismatch points at the reading point, and a figure near +4.50 D appears only if the minus sign on the distance power is dropped and the magnitudes 2.00 and 2.50 are added, when the true near total on this lens is +0.50 D.
  43. The correct answer is A (Add +2.25 D, confirmed by an engraving reading 225 beside one circle): the add is the near reference circle reading minus the distance reference point reading, so +0.50 minus -1.75 gives +2.25 D. Both readings are taken front vertex because the addition sits on the surface being measured, and the engraved figure omits the plus sign and the decimal point, so it appears as 225. Adding the two readings instead of subtracting them gives -1.25 D, which is not an add at all. Recording the near circle value on its own, or the distance magnitude on its own, ignores the subtraction the method depends on.
  44. The correct answer is C (A measured add of +2.50 D, which agrees with the engraved figure): subtracting the distance reference point value from the near reference circle value gives +3.75 minus +1.25, which is +2.50 D, and the engraved 250 states the same add without a plus sign or decimal point. Agreement between the measured value and the engraving is the confirmation the candidate is looking for before recording. Summing the two readings gives +5.00 D, which is a mistake in the direction of the subtraction. Recording the near circle reading alone gives the total near power rather than the add, and the distance sphere on its own is not an add value.
  45. The correct answer is B (Accept, because the 0.12 D difference sits at the stated allowance): +2.62 minus +2.50 is 0.12 D, and the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows an add up to +4.00 D to vary by 0.12 D, so the lens sits at the limit rather than beyond it. A value at the limit is within tolerance, not outside it. Add power does carry an allowance below +4.00 D, and that allowance widens to a different figure above that value. Rejecting every difference would fail lenses the standard accepts, and candidates must always work from the chart supplied on the day.
  46. The correct answer is D (Measured add +2.18 D, outside the allowance, so the pair is returned): the add is the near circle reading minus the distance reference point reading, so +2.43 minus +0.25 gives +2.18 D. That is 0.18 D away from the ordered +2.00, and the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows only 0.12 D on an add up to +4.00 D, so the lens fails on add power. Summing the two readings gives +2.68 D, which reverses the subtraction. Reading the near circle value alone as the add ignores the distance power already present at that point, and neither of those figures can be called within the allowance once the arithmetic is done correctly.
  47. The correct answer is C (Take both readings again, since a disagreement means one of them is wrong): the engraved figure and the measured add are two independent statements of the same value, so when they disagree the candidate has either landed off the near reference circle or misread the drum. Re-taking both readings settles which one is at fault, and only then is a figure written down. Treating either source as automatically superior records a number the candidate has not actually confirmed. Averaging two values that contradict each other produces a figure that neither the lens nor the instrument supports.
  48. The correct answer is B (The prism reference point, because the standard specifies prism there): that point sits midway between the two permanent circles, 17 mm from each, and it is where prescribed prism is specified and where unwanted prism is measured on a progressive. Using any other point mixes the design's own optics into the reading. The fitting cross locates the lens in front of the pupil for fitting purposes and is not the prism reference. The near reference circle sits well down the lens where the full add is present, so a reading there includes prism the design creates by geometry, and the engraving circles are identification marks rather than measuring points.
  49. The correct answer is A (The lens exceeds the allowance and the pair should be returned to the lab): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows no more than 0.33 prism dioptres at the prism reference point, and 0.50 is larger than that, so the lens fails on unwanted prism. The check exists precisely because unwanted prism has to be found on lenses where none was ordered, so the absence of a prism order is not a defence. Prism thinning is a genuine design feature on some progressives, but it is stated on the work order when it is applied and this order excludes it. The separate 1.0 mm rule on where the prism reference point sits applies when prism is prescribed, and it does not suspend the prism allowance itself.
  50. The correct answer is D (Prism at the near circle comes from the design, not from a lab error): the near reference circle sits well below and nasal to the distance optical centre, so the plus add and the offset position both create prism by geometry on any correctly made progressive. The prism check has one specified location, the prism reference point midway between the two circles, and only a reading taken there can be compared with the allowance. Prism allowances do apply to progressives, and the near circle certainly does show measurable prism, which is exactly why it is the wrong place to look. An unfocused eyepiece blurs the target rather than displacing it from the reticle centre.
  51. The correct answer is B (The deepest part of the eyewire groove, directly below the fitting cross): heights on both progressives and lined multifocals start at the lowest point of the lens aperture inside the groove, which is where the lens itself actually ends. Measuring from the outer rim edge adds the thickness of the eyewire to every reading and inflates the height by a real and measurable amount. A measurement taken down from the horizontal midline of the aperture describes a drop from the major reference point, which is a different quantity from a height. The bridge is a horizontal reference and has no part in a vertical height measurement.
  52. The correct answer is B (The centre of the pupil, viewed with the wearer in habitual posture): the fitting cross is the point that must sit in front of the pupil centre, which is why fitting cross height is recorded from the deepest part of the groove up to that inked cross. The lower lid margin is the common starting position for the top edge of a flat top segment, not for a progressive fitting point, and using it drops the corridor several millimetres too low. The upper lid margin plays no part in either measurement. The inner canthus is a horizontal landmark used in some facial measurements and gives no vertical information at all.
  53. The correct answer is C (Fitting cross height 22 mm, half the B measurement plus the offset): the horizontal midline of the aperture sits half the B measurement above the lowest point, so 34 divided by 2 gives 17 mm, and the pupil centre sits 5 mm above that, giving 22 mm from the deepest part of the groove up to the fitting cross. Adding the offset to the whole B measurement gives 39 mm, which sits above the top of the aperture. Ignoring the offset records the midline rather than the pupil centre, and subtracting the offset gives 12 mm and moves the cross the wrong way entirely.
  54. The correct answer is A (One is set to the pupil centre, the other to the lower lid margin): the fitting cross goes at the pupil centre, while the common starting position for the top edge of a flat top segment is the lower lid margin, confirmed afterwards against the wearer's task and posture. Because the lid margin sits below the pupil centre, a flat top segment height in one frame on one face lands several millimetres below the fitting cross height for that same face. Both measurements start from the same place, the lowest point of the aperture inside the groove, so the starting point is not the difference. Both are also taken separately for each eye and both are recorded in millimetres.
  55. The correct answer is D (Segment height 19 mm, since the lid margin sits below the pupil centre): both heights start at the deepest part of the eyewire groove, so a landmark 4 mm lower than the pupil centre gives a height 4 mm smaller, and 23 minus 4 is 19 mm. That is the practical reason a flat top segment height and a fitting cross height on one face are different numbers. Adding the 4 mm gives 27 mm and treats the lid margin as sitting above the pupil, which reverses the anatomy. Quoting the same figure for both assumes a shared landmark that does not exist, and halving the fitting cross height has no basis in either measurement.
  56. The correct answer is C (Wrong twice, since the reference point is wrong and the field is wrong): heights start at the deepest part of the eyewire groove, so starting at the outer rim edge adds the whole thickness of the eyewire and inflates the figure. Writing a fitting cross height into a segment height field also fails, because a correct measurement written in the wrong field scores the same as a wrong one on this examination. The two fields describe different measurements taken to different landmarks, so they are never interchangeable. The rim edge and the groove are separated by a real and measurable amount on a metal frame rather than by a negligible one.
  57. The correct answer is A (Splitting it assumes a symmetric face, and most faces are not symmetric): dividing 64 mm into two equal halves of 32 mm places both reference points as though each eye sat the same distance from the bridge of the nose, and real faces rarely do. A horizontal error of even two millimetres puts the line of sight into the blend zone beside the corridor, where the unwanted cylinder lives, and the wearer reports swim and a narrow reading field. Binocular figures can be taken for distance or for near, so the working distance is not the problem. Progressives are ordered on monocular distance figures, and a binocular value is already stated in millimetres.
  58. The correct answer is D (1.5 mm on each side, the right nasally and the left temporally): an even split puts both points at 32 mm from centre, so the right point sits 33.5 minus 32, which is 1.5 mm too far toward the nose, and the left point sits 32 minus 30.5, which is 1.5 mm too far toward the temple. The 3.0 mm total difference between the eyes is shared as 1.5 mm each, not applied whole to each side and not halved again to 0.75 mm. The left figure does not match the split either, since 30.5 mm is not 32 mm. Both errors also exceed the 1.0 mm the standard allows a mounted progressive reference point to sit from the specified monocular value.
  59. The correct answer is B (Out of allowance, since 1.5 mm exceeds the 1.0 mm mounted figure): the even split sets the right reference point at 33 mm, and the specified monocular value is 34.5 mm, so the point sits 1.5 mm from where it should be. The ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows a mounted progressive reference point only 1.0 mm from the specified monocular value, so the lens fails. The 2.5 mm mounted allowance belongs to single vision and lined multifocal work and does not apply here. Meeting the binocular total says nothing about where each individual point sits, and the 3.0 mm figure is the difference between the two eyes rather than the error on one lens.
  60. The correct answer is A (The line of sight falls in the blend zone, where unwanted cylinder lives): a horizontal displacement of about two millimetres moves the eye off the corridor and into the blended periphery of the design, which produces exactly the narrow reading field and the swimming sensation described. This is the practical reason progressives are set to monocular figures rather than to half a binocular value. An add ground two dioptres stronger than ordered would show up immediately as blurred distance and a wrong measured add, which verification did not find. A flat base curve and a vertical fitting cross error are real faults, but neither is what a purely horizontal displacement produces.
  61. The correct answer is C (The single vision pair passes and the progressive pair fails on placement): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows a mounted single vision or lined multifocal reference point to sit up to 2.5 mm from the specified distance pupillary distance, so two millimetres is inside that figure. A mounted progressive is held to 1.0 mm from the specified monocular value, so the same two millimetres puts the progressive outside its allowance. The tighter figure exists because a progressive corridor is narrow and a small horizontal shift moves the eye into the blend zone. Neither a common pass nor a common fail reflects the two different allowances the standard sets.
  62. The correct answer is D (1.5 mm, since half the frame plus bridge total is 35 mm): the A measurement plus the distance between lenses gives 52 plus 18, which is 70 mm, and half of that is 35 mm, the distance from the frame centre to the boxed centre of each lens. Subtracting the right monocular figure of 33.5 mm leaves 1.5 mm of inward decentration for that eye. Splitting the 64 mm binocular value evenly gives 32 mm and a decentration of 3.0 mm, which is exactly the shortcut a progressive job cannot use. Forgetting to halve the 70 mm total produces the 36.5 mm figure, and the 2.5 mm value is a mounted allowance from the tolerance chart rather than a layout result.
  63. The correct answer is D (4.5 mm, because 35 mm minus 30.5 mm gives the left figure): half of the A measurement plus the distance between lenses is 35 mm for both eyes, but the monocular values differ, so the two decentrations differ as well, giving 4.5 mm on the left against 1.5 mm on the right. Unequal decentration is the normal result of an asymmetric face and is exactly what monocular figures exist for. Assuming equal decentration would set both reference points at the same place and defeat the purpose of measuring each eye separately. Sharing a total between the eyes or doubling one figure to balance the other has no basis in the layout formula.
  64. The correct answer is B (A progressive identifier chart listing each brand's logo and marking layout): these charts are published as booklets and posters, and they show each maker's engraved logo, where that maker places the add engraving, and the geometry of the marking layout for each design. Without one, an engraved symbol names nothing. The tolerance chart states allowances for finished work and carries no brand information at all. The lensometer reads power rather than identification marks, and a lens clock reads surface curvature, which is not brand specific in any usable way.
  65. The correct answer is C (Two designs from one maker can place the fitting cross at different heights): the offset from the prism reference point up to the fitting cross belongs to the design rather than to the brand, and so do the positions of the distance and near reference circles. Using the wrong layout from the right maker puts every inked point in the wrong place. The separation between the permanent circles stays at 34 mm on conventional designs regardless of maker or design, so it cannot be used to tell the four apart. Add engravings appear across a maker's whole range, and a marking chart covers the geometry of a design rather than one add value.
  66. The correct answer is A (The lens must give at least 18 mm below the fitting cross to the aperture edge): the published figure states how much lens depth the design needs beneath the fitting cross to contain the corridor and the full near zone before the aperture ends. Since fitting cross height is measured from the deepest part of the groove up to the cross, that height must equal or exceed the published minimum for the design to work in that frame. The A measurement is a horizontal dimension and says nothing about vertical depth. Add power limits are stated separately by the maker, and the engraving positions are fixed by the design rather than by a fitting rule.
  67. The correct answer is B (Yes, because the 20 mm fitting cross height clears the minimum by 2 mm): the horizontal midline sits half the B measurement above the lowest point of the aperture, so 30 divided by 2 gives 15 mm, and the pupil centre 5 mm higher gives a fitting cross height of 20 mm. Twenty millimetres is more than the published 18 mm, so the design fits with two millimetres to spare. Comparing the full 30 mm B measurement against the minimum confuses a whole lens depth with the depth below the fitting cross. Ignoring the 5 mm offset gives 15 mm and would wrongly rule the design out, and the minimum is defined against the fitting cross height rather than against the B measurement.
  68. The correct answer is C (Short by 1 mm, so a shorter design or a deeper frame is needed): half of the 28 mm B measurement is 14 mm, and the pupil centre 3 mm above that gives a fitting cross height of 17 mm, one millimetre less than the 18 mm the design needs below the cross. Comparing the full 28 mm depth against the minimum measures the wrong quantity, because only the part below the fitting cross counts. Using a 5 mm offset instead of the stated 3 mm produces the 20 mm figure, and ignoring the offset entirely gives 14 mm and an apparent 4 mm shortfall. Dropping the fitting cross to the midline to force a fit moves the corridor away from the pupil centre and creates a worse problem than the one it solves.
  69. The correct answer is D (A height of 26 mm, clearing the minimum by 12 mm with room to spare): half of the 44 mm B measurement is 22 mm, and the pupil centre 4 mm above that puts the fitting cross 26 mm above the deepest part of the groove, well clear of the 14 mm the design needs. Ignoring the offset gives 22 mm and understates the height by the whole 4 mm. Subtracting the offset instead of adding it gives 18 mm and moves the cross the wrong way. A generous fitting cross height does not force a longer corridor, because corridor length belongs to the design rather than to the frame.
  70. The correct answer is C (The left lens fails on its own height and the pair fails on difference): the left lens sits 23.5 minus 22.0, which is 1.5 mm above the ordered height, and the standard allows a mounted progressive only 1.0 mm of vertical error on each lens. That same 1.5 mm is also the vertical difference between the two lenses, and the difference between lenses is separately held to 1.0 mm, so the pair fails on two counts. The right lens is at exactly the ordered height, so it is not the lens at fault and nothing about it should be changed. A difference between two lenses is compared whole against the allowance rather than shared or halved.
  71. The correct answer is A (The add was taken off the wrong circle, since the near circle carries it): the full addition is present only at the near reference circle, well below the fitting cross, so a reading taken at the fitting cross carries little or none of the add and the subtraction returns nearly zero. The engraved 200 states a +2.00 add, so the near circle should read two dioptres more plus than the distance reference point. Reversing the direction of the subtraction would give a negative figure of the right size rather than a figure near zero. The prism reference point is used for the prism check, and turning the lens around changes the vertex convention rather than supplying the missing two dioptres.
  72. The correct answer is B (Adds can differ between eyes, and heights very often do as well): each eye is read and recorded separately, right eye first, and copying one figure across assumes an equality nobody has verified. Fitting cross heights differ between eyes even more often than adds do, so the same shortcut costs marks in the height fields too. Both lenses of a progressive pair carry their own engravings, including the add figure, so the left value was available and simply was not read. There is no fixed ratio between the two eyes' adds, and base curve does not set the add power of a finished progressive.
  73. The correct answer is C (it displaces a ray of light 1 centimetre at a distance of 1 metre): that is the whole definition of the prism dioptre, and every prism calculation on the bench is built on it. Because displacement is linear with distance, the same prism gives 2 cm at 2 metres and 1 mm at 10 cm. A displacement of 1 millimetre at 1 metre describes one tenth of a prism dioptre, and 10 centimetres at 1 metre describes ten of them. Quoting the displacement at 10 metres rather than 1 metre changes the reference distance and gives a figure ten times too small.
  74. The correct answer is A (15 centimetres, because 3 centimetres of displacement per metre is multiplied by five): one prism dioptre gives 1 cm of displacement at 1 metre, so 3.00 prism dioptres gives 3 cm at 1 metre, and at 5 metres that becomes 3 x 5 = 15 cm. Displacement grows in direct proportion to distance, so the distance is a multiplier and never a divider. Quoting 3 centimetres ignores the distance entirely and reports only the value at 1 metre. Dividing by the distance gives 0.6 cm and halving gives 1.5 cm, both of which run the proportion backwards.
  75. The correct answer is D (3.00 prism dioptres, dividing the displacement in centimetres by the distance in metres): the prism dioptre is defined per metre, so 6 cm measured at 2 metres is 3 cm per metre, which is 3.00 prism dioptres. Reading the 6 cm straight off as the answer ignores that the screen sat at twice the reference distance. Multiplying 6 by 2 gives 12.00 and runs the relationship in the wrong direction entirely. Halving twice gives 1.50 and applies the distance correction one time too many.
  76. The correct answer is B (8 millimetres, since 2 centimetres per metre scaled to 0.4 metres gives 0.8 centimetres): 2.00 prism dioptres displaces 2 cm at 1 metre, so at 0.40 m the displacement is 2 x 0.4 = 0.8 cm, which is 8 mm. The reading distance must be converted to metres before it is applied, because the definition is anchored at 1 metre. Reporting 20 millimetres treats the displacement as fixed at all distances, which contradicts the definition. Reading the prism amount straight across as millimetres gives 2 mm, and halving the one centimetre per metre definition before applying it at 0.40 m gives 5 mm, neither of which uses the definition correctly.
  77. The correct answer is A (2.00 prism dioptres, scaling 1.2 centimetres at 0.6 metres up to one metre): the measured 12 mm is 1.2 cm, and it was taken at 0.60 m, so the displacement at 1 metre is 1.2 divided by 0.6 = 2.0 cm, which is 2.00 prism dioptres. The measured distance always has to be brought back to the 1 metre reference before the number can be called prism dioptres. Reporting 1.20 uses the raw centimetre figure without correcting for the shorter distance. Multiplying 1.2 by 0.6 gives 0.72 and inverts the correction, while calling each millimetre one prism dioptre gives 12.00 and ignores the definition.
  78. The correct answer is C (light bends toward the base and the image is displaced toward the apex): light entering a prism is deviated toward the thick edge, which is the base, so the image the patient perceives lies toward the thin edge, which is the apex. This is why candidates who describe where the image moved end up recording the base backwards. The two do not travel together, so any statement putting light and the image in the same direction is wrong whichever edge it names. Reversing the pair, with light toward the apex and the image toward the base, describes the opposite of what a prism does.
  79. The correct answer is B (they point opposite ways, since base out is named toward each eye's own temple): horizontal base names are defined against the patient's own nose, so base out means temporally in that eye. The right eye's temporal side and the left eye's temporal side face opposite ways, and base out in both lenses therefore describes two opposing physical directions that compound as a vergence demand. Naming the direction from the operator's own viewpoint is exactly the error the examiners look for. The claim that horizontal names ignore which eye is involved is the opposite of the convention, and the amounts carried by the lenses have no bearing on which way a base points.
  80. The correct answer is D (base up points the same physical direction in both eyes, unlike base out): vertical base directions are absolute, so up is up in the right lens and in the left lens, while horizontal names are referenced to the patient's nose and therefore reverse between eyes. Two lenses both carrying base up displace the images the same way, so no vertical imbalance results. Saying base up reverses between eyes applies the horizontal convention where it does not belong. Vertical base is not measured from the temple, and the two amounts being equal or unequal changes the imbalance but never changes which way up points.
  81. The correct answer is A (180 degrees, because the right eye's temporal side sits at the 180 position): facing the patient, zero degrees lies at your right hand, which is the patient's nasal side for the right eye, so base in for the right lens is 0 and base out is 180. That reverses for the left lens, where base out is 0 and base in is 180. Calling 0 degrees base out for the right lens states the convention backwards. The 90 and 270 positions are reserved for base up and base down, and the circle is read counterclockwise as you face the patient, not clockwise.
  82. The correct answer is B (prism directed up and out, with equal horizontal and vertical parts): in the 360 degree convention 90 degrees is straight up and 0 degrees is base out for the left lens, so a base at 45 lies midway between them and points up and out. Because 45 sits exactly halfway, the horizontal and vertical components come out equal in size. A base pointing up and in would be written near 135 for that eye, and anything pointing down falls in the lower half of the circle, between 180 and 360. Getting this the wrong way round is one of the quickest ways to record a base backwards on the sheet.
  83. The correct answer is D (a base has a direction while a cylinder axis only has an orientation): a cylinder axis lying along 20 degrees is the same meridian as one lying along 200 degrees, so 180 degrees of scale is enough. A prism base at 20 degrees points somewhere quite different from a base at 200 degrees, so the full circle is needed to say which way the thick edge faces. A cylinder axis can certainly sit obliquely, so the claim that it can only be horizontal or vertical is false. Both scales are read counterclockwise as you face the patient, and the starting reference is horizontal for each, so neither of those differences explains the extra 180 degrees.
  84. The correct answer is C (3.00 prism dioptres, since each printed ring step represents one prism dioptre): on this instrument each ring equals one prism dioptre, so a target centred on the third ring reads 3.00 prism dioptres, and the direction it moved gives the base. Half dioptre rings are found on some instruments, and reading them as if they were half steps here would give 1.50 and understate the prism by half. Dividing a ring count by ten belongs to Prentice's rule with millimetres, not to reading a reticle. Counting rings across the full diameter rather than out from the bullseye doubles the answer to 6.00.
  85. The correct answer is A (2.50 prism dioptres, five ring steps of half a prism dioptre each): the reticle on this instrument is marked in half prism dioptre steps, so five steps equals 5 x 0.50 = 2.50 prism dioptres, which sits neatly within the five prism dioptre range described. Treating each ring as a whole prism dioptre gives 5.00 and doubles the true reading, which is exactly why the ring value must be confirmed during familiarization time. Halving the count twice gives 1.25 and applies the correction one time too many. Doubling the count to 10.00 corrects in the wrong direction and also exceeds the range the reticle can display.
  86. The correct answer is B (the target moves toward the base, opposite to what the patient sees through it): the lensometer images the target onto a reticle rather than into an eye, so the displacement seen through the eyepiece runs toward the base of the prism in the lens. The patient, looking through the same lens, sees the image displaced toward the apex, which is why the two descriptions disagree. Reporting the direction as if it matched the patient's experience reverses every base entry on the sheet. The behaviour does not depend on whether the lens is plus or minus, and the displacement carries both the amount and the direction, not the amount alone.
  87. The correct answer is C (2.00 prism dioptres base out, since the base follows the target displacement): two ring steps at one prism dioptre each gives 2.00 prism dioptres, and the target moves toward the base, so a temporal displacement in the right lens is base out. Recording base in reverses the direction and is the single most common prism error in this section. Reading a purely horizontal displacement as base up confuses the two meridians entirely. Counting each ring as two prism dioptres gives 4.00 and doubles the amount on an instrument marked in single steps.
  88. The correct answer is A (fit the auxiliary prism compensator and dial prism in until the target centres): when the target runs off the reticle the prism exceeds what the rings can display, and the compensator is the instrument's own answer to that problem. It clips into the light path, its base is set to match, and known prism is added until the target returns to the bullseye. Turning the power drum changes the sphere and cylinder setting and does nothing to bring a displaced target back inside the rings. Raising the lens table shifts the target vertically only and cannot recover a horizontal excursion, and recording the reticle maximum reports a figure the candidate never actually measured.
  89. The correct answer is D (set its base direction, dial until the target centres, read the compensator scale): the compensator is a rotating prism of known value, so its base is oriented first, prism is then dialled in until the target sits back on the bullseye, and the amount is taken from the compensator scale rather than from the rings. That sequence gives one clean figure for the prism in the lens. Reading a leftover ring count after only partly neutralizing the displacement mixes two scales and produces an unreliable total. The power drum reads sphere and cylinder, not prism, and dialling a fixed value regardless of the lens ignores what the instrument is actually for.
  90. The correct answer is B (shift the lens until the target centres, mark that point, measure its offset): with no compensator available, the candidate finds the point in the lens where no prism exists, marks it, and measures how far that point lies from the position the order specified. Prentice's rule then converts that measured distance back into prism dioptres. Reading at the thin edge measures a point nobody looks through and answers a different question. Turning the lens over does not change the prism the lens carries, and recording the reticle maximum with a note reports a number that was never measured and cannot be verified.
  91. The correct answer is C (dot the optical centres and compare their separation with the ordered distance PD): if the two centre dots sit at the distance PD on the order yet prism is still present, the prism was ground into the lens and was almost certainly prescribed. If the dots do not match the ordered PD, the prism came from a decentration error and the pair goes back to the laboratory. Reading base curves tells you about surface power and material choice, not about where the optical centres landed. Comparing edge thicknesses is crude and gives no measured figure, and rotating a lens to watch the axis tests cylinder orientation rather than prism origin.
  92. The correct answer is A (accept the pair on prism, because the prescribed prism is present and correct): prism that appears on the work order should be found at the prism reference point in the amount and base specified, and when it is, that component passes. Calling correct prescribed prism an error costs the mark just as surely as missing unwanted prism does. Treating any prism found at neutralization as a layout fault ignores the order in front of the candidate. Base out is not converted to base in during splitting, and the small allowance of roughly a third of a prism dioptre applies to prism found when none was ordered, not to prism that was deliberately prescribed.
  93. The correct answer is B (return it, since unordered prism well above a third of a dioptre fails): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR permits only about 0.33 prism dioptres at the prism reference point when no prism was ordered, and 0.75 prism dioptres is more than double that. The reading is unwanted prism, most likely from a vertical centration error, so the pair goes back to the laboratory. Allowing anything under a full prism dioptre invents a tolerance that the standard does not give. Vertical prism is judged both as an absolute amount and as a difference between lenses, and the standard does allow a small measured amount, so a rule that fails any measurable prism at all is too strict.
  94. The correct answer is D (the optical centres were placed away from the positions the order specified): unwanted prism in a correctly powered pair with correct axes points straight at a centration or layout fault, because the patient's line of sight is passing through the lens away from the optical centre. A rotated lens, a badly edged lens, or a frame sprung out of alignment can do the same thing, but all of them share that same underlying cause. Base curve affects thickness and off axis performance, not the prism at the reference point. A higher refractive index changes the surface powers needed but does not create prism, and an unfocused eyepiece blurs the target rather than displacing it.
  95. The correct answer is A (1.50 base out in each lens, the total shared equally between the two eyes): the word split tells the laboratory to divide the ordered total equally between the two eyes, so 3.00 prism dioptres base out becomes 1.50 base out in each lens. Horizontal prism splits in the same direction in both eyes, because base out in each eye already opposes the other physically and the two amounts compound. Putting 3.00 in each lens doubles the ordered total to 6.00 across the pair. Splitting one lens base out and the other base in would cancel the intended effect, and loading the whole amount onto one eye leaves the pair looking uneven and thicker on one side.
  96. The correct answer is C (1.50 base up right and 1.50 base down left, opposing between the eyes): vertical prism is a relative displacement between the two eyes, so splitting 3.00 base up before the right eye gives half that amount base up on the right and the other half base down on the left. The two halves oppose, which is what preserves the ordered vertical effect while reducing thickness in each lens. Making both lenses base up produces a yoked shift with no vertical effect at all, which is the wrong outcome. Keeping the full 3.00 in each lens doubles the prescription, and leaving one lens with no vertical prism only delivers half the ordered amount.
  97. The correct answer is B (2.50 base in right and 2.50 base in left, halving the ordered total): a horizontal total marked split is divided equally between the eyes in the same named direction, so 5.00 prism dioptres base in becomes 2.50 base in per lens. The two halves compound back to the ordered 5.00 across the pair, because base in in each eye opposes the other physically. Leaving 5.00 in each lens ignores the instruction and doubles the effect. Pairing base in with base out cancels most of the intended prism, and loading the whole amount before one eye makes the lenses visibly unequal in thickness.
  98. The correct answer is D (check against 2.00 in each lens, since the order gives per lens values): when a work order lists a value on the OD line and a value on the OS line, those are the amounts required in each lens, and the candidate verifies against exactly what is written. Splitting on her own initiative changes the prescription and turns a correct pair into a fail on the sheet. Halving to 1.00 per lens assumes an instruction the order never gave. Compounding to 4.00 per lens confuses the binocular total with the per lens value, and no rule makes horizontal prism automatically shared once it has been specified eye by eye.
  99. The correct answer is A (3.00 prism dioptres base out, since base out in each eye compounds): base out in the right lens and base out in the left lens point in opposite physical directions, so their effects add and the pair delivers 2.00 plus 1.00 = 3.00 prism dioptres base out in total. This is why a total on a work order can be split between the eyes and still give the ordered effect. Subtracting the smaller from the larger gives 1.00 and treats the two as opposing when they reinforce. Reporting only the larger lens ignores the second eye completely, and averaging to 1.50 halves the real binocular demand.
  100. The correct answer is C (3.00 prism dioptres, since opposite vertical bases compound between the two eyes): base up in one eye and base down in the other displace the two images in opposite directions, so the effects add and the pair delivers 1.50 plus 1.50 = 3.00 prism dioptres of vertical prism. That is exactly how a prescribed vertical total is split across a pair. Calling the result zero treats opposite bases as cancelling, which is what happens only when both eyes carry the same base. Counting one lens alone gives 1.50 and ignores the second eye, and halving the smaller figure to 0.75 has no basis in the way vertical prism combines.
  101. The correct answer is D (it creates no net vergence demand, shifting both images the same way): base in before the right eye and base out before the left eye both point toward the patient's left, so the two prisms are yoked and displace both images together rather than pulling the eyes apart or together. A yoked pair like this is used to shift gaze, not to relieve a phoria. Adding the two amounts to 4.00 assumes the bases oppose, which they do not in this combination. There is no vertical element anywhere in the reading, so a vertical demand cannot arise from two horizontal bases.
  102. The correct answer is B (5.00 prism dioptres, combining the two components as a right angled triangle): horizontal and vertical prism act at 90 degrees to each other, so the resultant is the square root of 3.00 squared plus 4.00 squared, which is the square root of 9 plus 16 = 25, giving 5.00 prism dioptres. Adding the two amounts straight together gives 7.00 and would only be right if both acted along the same line. Subtracting them gives 1.00 and treats them as directly opposing. Averaging to 3.50 has no geometric basis at all and always falls between the two components rather than above the larger one.
  103. The correct answer is C (2.50 prism dioptres, taking the square root of the summed squared components): the two components lie at right angles, so the resultant is the square root of 2.00 squared plus 1.50 squared, which is the square root of 4 plus 2.25 = 6.25, giving 2.50 prism dioptres. A useful check is that a valid resultant is always larger than either component and smaller than their sum. Adding straight to 3.50 overstates the resultant and ignores the right angle between the meridians. Subtracting to 0.50 and averaging to 1.75 both fall below the larger component, which is geometrically impossible.
  104. The correct answer is A (3.00 prism dioptres, the square root of the two squared components summed): squaring gives 2.40 squared = 5.76 and 1.80 squared = 3.24, and their sum is 9.00, whose square root is 3.00 prism dioptres. The horizontal and vertical meridians are 90 degrees apart, so the components combine as the sides of a right angled triangle rather than arithmetically. Adding them straight gives 4.20 and overstates the true resultant. Averaging gives 2.10 and subtracting gives 0.60, and both sit below the larger of the two components, which no genuine resultant ever does.
  105. The correct answer is B (4.24 prism dioptres base 45, the components being equal in both meridians): the amount is the square root of 3.00 squared plus 3.00 squared, which is the square root of 18, or 4.24 prism dioptres. Because the horizontal and vertical components are equal, the resultant lies exactly midway between base in at 0 and base up at 90, which is 45 in this eye. Adding the components to 6.00 ignores the right angle between them. Writing base 135 measures the angle from the wrong reference and would place the base up and out instead of up and in, and quoting only the vertical component discards the horizontal element entirely.
  106. The correct answer is D (2.00 prism dioptres base out with 3.46 prism dioptres base up): the horizontal component is 4.00 x cos 60 = 4.00 x 0.500 = 2.00, and the vertical component is 4.00 x sin 60 = 4.00 x 0.866 = 3.46. In the left lens zero degrees is base out and 90 degrees is base up, and a base at 60 lies in that upper outer quadrant, so both components carry those directions. Swapping the two figures puts 3.46 horizontally and 2.00 vertically, which is what happens when sine and cosine are applied to the wrong meridian. Calling the horizontal component base in reverses the eye convention, and calling the vertical component base down puts the base in the wrong half of the circle.
  107. The correct answer is A (4.33 prism dioptres base in with 2.50 prism dioptres base up): the horizontal component is 5.00 x cos 30 = 5.00 x 0.866 = 4.33, and the vertical component is 5.00 x sin 30 = 5.00 x 0.500 = 2.50. In the right lens zero degrees is base in and 90 degrees is base up, so a base at 30 sits just above the horizontal and points up and in. Reversing the two figures gives 2.50 horizontally and 4.33 vertically, the result of applying sine and cosine to the wrong meridians. Naming the horizontal component base out reverses the right eye convention, and naming the vertical component base down places the base below the horizontal instead of above it.
  108. The correct answer is C (4.24 prism dioptres base in with 4.24 prism dioptres base up): at 45 degrees the sine and the cosine are both 0.707, so each component is 6.00 x 0.707 = 4.24 prism dioptres. In the right lens zero degrees is base in and 90 degrees is base up, and 45 lies exactly between them, so the base points up and in with equal parts in each meridian. Splitting the 6.00 arithmetically into 3.00 and 3.00 would only be right if the components added straight, which they do not at a right angle. Calling the horizontal component base out reverses the right eye convention, and calling the vertical component base down puts the base in the lower half of the circle.
  109. The correct answer is B (1.50 prism dioptres base in with 2.60 prism dioptres base down): at 240 degrees the cosine is minus 0.500 and the sine is minus 0.866, so the horizontal component is 3.00 x 0.500 = 1.50 pointing toward 180, and the vertical component is 3.00 x 0.866 = 2.60 pointing downward. In the left lens zero degrees is base out, so a horizontal component pointing toward 180 is base in, and the negative vertical value is base down. Swapping the two figures puts 2.60 horizontally and 1.50 vertically, which comes from applying sine and cosine to the wrong meridians. Calling the horizontal component base out ignores the minus sign on the cosine, and calling the vertical component base up ignores the minus sign on the sine.
  110. The correct answer is D (record the horizontal and the vertical components separately, which removes ambiguity): a neutralization sheet expects the two components written out, because that statement can be checked directly against a work order without anyone having to reconstruct an angle. It also removes any doubt about which quadrant the base sits in. Recording only the larger component throws away real prism the patient will experience. Recording only the vertical component assumes the horizontal element does not matter, which is a judgement the sheet does not ask for, and giving a bare resultant amount leaves the base direction unstated unless the angle is written with it.
  111. The correct answer is D (equal base down prism ground into both lenses to reduce edge thickness): prism thinning is a laboratory technique in progressive lenses where the same amount of base down prism is worked into each lens so that the thickness at the top and bottom of the blank evens out. It is applied deliberately by the laboratory, not requested by the prescriber to treat a phoria, so it will not appear as prescribed prism on the prescription line. A grinding fault would give unequal or mismatched readings between the two lenses rather than the matched pair found here. It is a lens surfacing choice and has nothing to do with adjusting the frame.
  112. The correct answer is C (equal and matching bases shift both images together, so no imbalance results): thinning prism is deliberately made the same amount and the same base in both lenses, so the two images move together and the eyes are never asked to fuse a vertical difference. Vertical prism only troubles a wearer when the two eyes receive different amounts or opposite bases. Accommodation is a focusing response and does not cancel a prismatic displacement. The amounts used are quite large enough for a reticle to display, which is exactly why candidates find them, and nothing in the corridor design cancels ground prism at the reference point.
  113. The correct answer is B (within standard, because equal thinning prism was called for on the order): the order does specify prism thinning, so the matched base down readings are expected and are not unwanted prism. The small allowance for prism at the reference point applies to prism found when none was called for, and it does not apply to a value the order itself requested. Thinning prism is a normal feature of progressive work rather than something restricted to single vision lenses. Vertical prism certainly is judged during progressive verification, both as an amount and as a difference between the two lenses, so no exemption exists there.
  114. The correct answer is A (report the direction the lensometer target moved, since it moves toward the base): on the instrument the target displaces toward the base of the prism in the lens, so the direction of movement is read straight off and named as in, out, up, or down relative to the patient. Reporting the direction of the patient's perceived image gives the apex instead and reverses every entry. Adding a second reversal after reading the target correctly undoes a correct answer and produces the same wrong result. Looking at the thin edge of the lens names the apex rather than the base, which is the same error described in different words.
  115. The correct answer is D (the direction the thick edge of the prism in that lens points): the base is the thick edge, and the base column asks which way that edge faces, written as in, out, up, or down relative to the patient. The direction the patient's image appears to move is toward the apex, which is the opposite direction, and reporting it fills the column with reversed entries. The thin edge is the apex, so naming its direction gives the same reversal. The direction the lens must be decentred belongs to a layout calculation and answers a different question altogether.
  116. The correct answer is C (horizontal base is named as in or out, relative to the patient's nose): the convention is anchored to the patient, so horizontal base is written base in toward the nose or base out toward the temple, and the same physical direction is named in for one eye and out for the other. Base right and base left describe the bench rather than the patient and cannot be transferred to a work order or checked against one. Horizontal base is not restricted to a degree notation, since in and out are the standard written forms. The names are never taken from the frame's temple ends, and no vertical prism is involved in this reading at all.
  117. The correct answer is B (1.00 prism dioptre base out, the base in amount opposing the base out): base out in the right lens and base in in the left lens point the same physical way, so they act against each other in vergence terms and the smaller subtracts from the larger, leaving 3.00 minus 2.00 = 1.00 prism dioptre base out overall. Adding the two amounts to 5.00 assumes both lenses carry the same named base, which they do not here. Naming the result base in lets the smaller reading govern the direction, which reverses the answer. Averaging the two readings to 2.50 has no basis in how prism combines between the eyes.
  118. The correct answer is A (return it, because the base direction is reversed from what was ordered): the amount matches, but base in delivers the opposite effect to the base out that was prescribed, so the pair fails on that component and goes back to the laboratory with that single reason given. Accepting on the strength of the amount alone ignores half of what a prism specification states. The one third of a prism dioptre allowance applies to prism found when none was ordered, and it has no bearing on a pair where 2.00 prism dioptres was requested. Base direction is checked on every pair carrying prism, prescribed or not.
  119. The correct answer is C (2.00 prism dioptres base down, since the target moves toward the base): two ring steps at one prism dioptre each gives 2.00 prism dioptres, and because the lensometer target displaces toward the base, a downward movement means base down. Recording base up reverses the direction and is the classic prism error in this section. Naming a purely vertical displacement as base in mixes the horizontal and vertical meridians. Reading two ring steps as 1.00 prism dioptre halves the amount and would only apply on an instrument marked in half dioptre steps.
  120. The correct answer is C (2.00 prism dioptres with the base pointing up in that lens): 4 millimetres is 0.4 centimetres, and 0.4 multiplied by 5.00 gives 2.00 prism dioptres. The millimetre form, 4 times 5.00 divided by 10, gives the same 2.00. A plus lens is thickest at its centre, so with the line of sight below that centre the base points back up toward it. The figures of 20.00 and 0.20 come from skipping the millimetre to centimetre conversion or applying it twice, and a base down answer reverses the plus lens rule.
