  152. The correct answer is B (Fusional reserves are large horizontally and very small in the vertical direction): the eyes converge and diverge constantly, so the horizontal fusional range is wide and a couple of prism dioptres of horizontal prism is absorbed without symptoms. Vertical fusional reserves are usually under about 1.50 prism dioptres and they do not build with practice, so the same amount vertically produces asthenopia. The claim that horizontal prism has a smaller optical effect is untrue, since one prism dioptre displaces an image the same 1 centimetre at 1 metre in any direction. The second lens does not cancel prism in the first, and vertical prism does not displace an image any further than horizontal prism does.
  153. The correct answer is A (The vertical prismatic imbalance present at the patient's actual reading level): anisometropia plus a symptom pattern that appears only at near points straight at induced vertical imbalance, because the two lenses of different power produce different amounts of prism as the line of sight drops. An ache around the eyes, a frontal headache after about half an hour of reading, and a compensating head tilt are the classic signs. A wrong cylinder axis or an incorrect base curve would usually blur or distort at distance as well, not only at near. Pantoscopic tilt and vertex distance change effective power slightly but do not produce a near only imbalance between the eyes.
  154. The correct answer is C (It adds counteracting prism below the line, cancelling the vertical imbalance): a slab off places base up prism across the reading portion of the more minus lens, opposing the base down prism that lens induces as the eye drops to near. The two eyes then receive matched vertical prism at the reading level and can fuse comfortably. It does not alter the distance powers, which stay exactly as prescribed for each eye. It does not move the distance optical centres, and although it changes the appearance of one lens it is not a weight balancing measure.
  155. The correct answer is D (Decentration avoids the extra thickness and weight that ground prism adds): prism ground into a surface thickens the lens toward the base and adds weight and edge thickness, while shifting the optical centre delivers the same prism with no added material. Where the power is high enough, a small shift of the centre supplies the ordered prism inside the normal blank, and this is usually the preferred route. Prism from decentration is not constant across the lens, since it grows with distance from the optical centre. Base curve choice is driven by power and thickness rather than by the prism method, and ground prism is read at the reference point on any manual lensometer without difficulty.
  156. The correct answer is A (The power is so low that the required decentration exceeds any blank): Prentice's rule rearranged gives decentration equal to prism divided by power, so 3.00 divided by 0.75 is 4 centimetres, or 40 millimetres, in each lens. No practical blank or frame allows the optical centre to sit 40 millimetres from the line of sight, so the prism has to be surfaced. Decentring a minus lens outward is a routine operation and does not damage the bevel. Base out prism is produced by decentration every day in stronger lenses, and low power never reverses the base direction obtained.
  157. The correct answer is B (The base always points back toward the lens own optical centre): a plus lens is thick at the centre and thin at the edge, and the base of the local prism always points toward the thicker part of the lens. From any point on a plus lens the glass thickens as you move toward the centre, so the base points back that way whatever the direction of the decentration. With the line of sight above the centre in this case, the base therefore points down. Pointing away from the centre describes the minus lens rule instead, the base is not fixed upward, and reporting the direction the image appears displaced gives the apex rather than the base.
  158. The correct answer is C (The base points away from the optical centre, following the displacement): a minus lens is thin at the centre and thick at the edge, and the base of the local prism points toward the thicker part, which is always outward from the centre. That means the base lies in the same direction as the displacement of the line of sight from the optical centre. Here the centre sits nasal to the line of sight, so the displacement is temporal and the base is out. Pointing back toward the centre is the plus lens rule, frame thickness has nothing to do with it, and describing the base as opposite to the displacement inverts the minus lens rule.
  159. The correct answer is D (The millimetre decentration was never converted into centimetres before multiplying): Prentice's rule takes the decentration in centimetres, so 0.6 multiplied by 5.00 gives 3.00 prism dioptres, not 30.00. The written figure is 6 times 5.00 with the division by 10 left out, which is by far the most common arithmetic slip on this rule. Halving for two lenses is not part of the rule, since each lens is calculated on its own decentration and its own power. Taking the absolute value of the power affects only the sign convention and not the size of the answer, and the decentration is always measured from the optical centre rather than the frame geometric centre.
  160. The correct answer is B (The power in the vertical meridian, which is -5.00 dioptres): with the cylinder axis at 180 the cylinder power acts in the meridian 90 degrees away, which is the vertical one, so the vertical meridian power is minus 1.00 plus minus 4.00, giving minus 5.00 dioptres. A vertical decentration must use that vertical meridian power, not the sphere. The sphere of minus 1.00 is the power in the horizontal meridian and is what a horizontal decentration would use. The spherical equivalent of minus 3.00 is an average that belongs to no real meridian, and the cylinder alone of minus 4.00 ignores the sphere it sits on.
  161. The correct answer is A (A minus lens thickens toward the edge, so the base is down): the arithmetic of 0.2 centimetres multiplied by 8.00 giving 1.60 prism dioptres is right, but the direction is not. In a minus lens the base points away from the optical centre, following the displacement of the line of sight, and here the line of sight sits below the centre, so the base is down. A minus lens induces vertical prism just as readily as horizontal prism, depending on which way the centre is displaced. Base direction is always named relative to the patient rather than the dispenser's side of the bench, and the direction never depends on the size of the error.
  162. The correct answer is D (Induced prism scales with power, so 0.10 becomes 1.40 prism dioptres): the same 0.2 centimetre error gives 0.2 multiplied by 0.50, which is 0.10 prism dioptres in the weak lens, and 0.2 multiplied by 7.00, which is 1.40 prism dioptres in the strong one. The first is far below anything the patient can detect, while the second is above the vertical fusional reserve of most wearers. Lens thickness affects glazing security only slightly and has nothing to do with the symptom. A weak lens does induce prism, just a very small amount, and base curve does not cancel prism produced by a decentred optical centre.
  163. The correct answer is C (Anisometropia in the vertical meridian together with a low reading level): induced vertical imbalance depends on the difference between the two vertical meridian powers and on how far the line of sight drops below the distance optical centres, so both features must be present. A large difference with a shallow drop may still be tolerable, and a deep drop with matched powers produces no imbalance at all. High cylinder with axes near 180 matters only if the vertical meridian powers actually differ between the eyes. A large add and a flat base curve change nothing about the prism at the reading level, and matched high minus powers cancel each other exactly however deep the drop.
  164. The correct answer is A (Refer back to the prescriber and discuss slab off or separate readers): the pair verifies correct against the order, so the fault is in the design rather than the workmanship, and 2.00 prism dioptres of vertical imbalance at near exceeds what most patients fuse comfortably. A slab off or a separate reading pair removes the imbalance at the reading level, and the choice belongs with the prescriber and the patient together. Telling the patient to persevere fails, because vertical fusional reserves are small and do not train up. Raising both segments equally leaves the difference between the eyes untouched, and returning a pair that matches its order will simply produce the identical result.
  165. The correct answer is B (Prism equals millimetres of decentration times the power, divided by ten): the classic rule uses centimetres, and a bench ruler reads millimetres, so dividing the millimetre product by 10 converts correctly. A 5 millimetre decentration in a 4.00 dioptre lens gives 5 times 4.00 divided by 10, which is 2.00 prism dioptres, matching 0.5 centimetres multiplied by 4.00. Multiplying millimetres by power without dividing gives an answer ten times too large, which is the most common error on this rule. Dividing by one hundred makes the answer ten times too small, and dividing power by decentration inverts the relationship completely.
  166. The correct answer is D (Multiply the difference between the vertical powers by the drop in centimetres): the two separate calculations share the same decentration, so subtracting them is the same as multiplying the power difference by that single drop. For a pair of minus 6.00 and minus 2.00 read 10 millimetres down, the difference of 4.00 dioptres multiplied by 1.0 centimetre gives 4.00 prism dioptres, the same as 6.00 minus 2.00 worked separately. Adding the two powers instead of subtracting them describes total prism rather than imbalance. Using the mean power against twice the drop and dividing the stronger power by the drop are not equivalent to the subtraction and give the wrong figure.
  167. The correct answer is C (displacement of the centre pin, the sagittal depth across the fixed pin span): the two outer pins set a fixed chord on the surface, and the spring loaded centre pin rides in or out by the sag of that chord. The dial converts that small linear movement into surface power using an assumed refractive index, which is why it is engraved in dioptres rather than millimetres. The instrument has no way of sensing what material it is resting on, and it never measures overall lens thickness. Radius of curvature is not shown on the dial at all.
  168. The correct answer is A (the surface being measured has a refractive index of 1.53): every lens clock dial is engraved for crown glass at n = 1.53, so it measures the true shape of the surface but converts that shape into power using 1.53. Polycarbonate at 1.586 bends light more strongly than 1.53, so the same physical curve carries more power than the dial shows. A dial engraved for 1.586 would need no correction on polycarbonate, which is not how these instruments are made. Nothing about the dial assumes a particular lens form or a blank diameter.
  169. The correct answer is D (reading multiplied by the actual index minus one, then divided by 0.53): true power equals the dial reading times (n actual minus 1) divided by (1.53 minus 1), and 1.53 minus 1 is always 0.53. The denominator is therefore a fixed 0.53 and the numerator carries the material being measured. Putting 0.53 on top and the material index underneath inverts the whole correction and drives high index readings downward instead of upward. Dividing by the raw index rather than by the index minus one also gives a factor far too small to be right.
  170. The correct answer is B (record the readings as they stand, because the multiplier equals exactly one): Trivex has a refractive index of 1.53, which is the very index the dial is engraved for, so the factor works out as (1.53 minus 1) divided by 0.53, or 0.53 divided by 0.53, which is 1.000. Multiplying either reading by one leaves +5.50 D and -7.50 D unchanged. The 1.106 factor belongs to polycarbonate at 1.586 and has no place on a 1.53 material. Dividing by 0.53 alone is not the correction formula, and the sign of a concave back surface stays negative.
  171. The correct answer is D (+6.63 D, the reading corrected upward for the higher index): the factor is (1.586 minus 1) divided by 0.53, which is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057. Multiplying 6.00 by 1.1057 gives 6.634, which rounds to +6.63 D. Recording the raw +6.00 D ignores the correction entirely and understates the surface by more than half a dioptre, which is most of the base curve allowance. Turning the factor upside down gives 6.00 times 0.9044, or +5.43 D, and dividing by 0.53 on its own gives an impossible +11.32 D.
  172. The correct answer is A (-10.11 D, the reading corrected upward for the higher index): the factor for 1.67 material is 0.67 divided by 0.53, which is 1.2642, and 8.00 times 1.2642 gives 10.113, rounding to 10.11 D. The surface is concave and reads minus, so the corrected value is recorded as -10.11 D. Leaving the reading at -8.00 D understates the surface by more than two dioptres on a strong back curve. Inverting the factor gives 8.00 times 0.7910, or -6.33 D, and multiplying by 0.67 alone gives -5.36 D, both of which move the value the wrong way.
  173. The correct answer is B (+5.58 D, the reading raised by the 1.74 index factor): the factor is (1.74 minus 1) divided by 0.53, which is 0.74 divided by 0.53, or 1.3962. Multiplying 4.00 by 1.3962 gives 5.585, which rounds to +5.58 D. The dial is engraved for 1.53 crown glass and never assumes a high index, so leaving the reading at +4.00 D understates the surface by nearly 1.60 D. Inverting the factor gives 4.00 times 0.7162, or +2.86 D, and multiplying by 0.74 alone gives +2.96 D, both of which flatten the surface instead of steepening it.
  174. The correct answer is C (+5.64 D, because the multiplier falls slightly below one here): the factor is 0.498 divided by 0.53, which is 0.9396, and 6.00 times 0.9396 gives 5.638, rounding to +5.64 D. Because CR39 sits just below the calibration index, the correction pulls the value down rather than up, and the shift is small enough that many shops read CR39 uncorrected. The correction still applies mathematically, so leaving the value at +6.00 D is a rounding convenience rather than a rule. Inverting the factor gives +6.39 D and multiplying by the index minus one gives +2.99 D, neither of which follows the formula.
  175. The correct answer is A (-6.79 D, the dial reading raised by the index factor): the factor for 1.60 material is 0.60 divided by 0.53, which is 1.1321, and 6.00 times 1.1321 gives 6.793, rounding to 6.79 D. The back surface is concave and reads minus, so the recorded value is -6.79 D. Leaving the value at -6.00 D ignores the correction and would throw off any surface power sum. Inverting the factor gives 6.00 times 0.8833, or -5.30 D, and multiplying by 0.60 alone gives -3.60 D, both of which move the surface flatter instead of steeper.
  176. The correct answer is C (-3.32 D, from correcting each surface and then adding them): the polycarbonate factor is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057, so the front becomes 6.00 times 1.1057, which is +6.63 D, and the back becomes 9.00 times 1.1057, which is -9.95 D. Adding +6.63 and -9.95 gives -3.32 D, the same result as correcting the net -3.00 D directly. Adding the raw dial readings gives -3.00 D and leaves out the correction altogether. Inverting the factor gives -2.71 D, and adding the corrected magnitudes rather than the signed values gives an impossible -16.58 D.
  177. The correct answer is B (2.50 D, the difference between the two principal meridian readings): surface cylinder is the difference between the highest and lowest readings taken ninety degrees apart, so 8.50 minus 6.00 gives 2.50 D. The two readings are the principal meridians of the toric back, and the gap between them is what the cylinder grinding produced. Averaging the two readings gives the mean sphere of that surface, not the cylinder. Adding them has no optical meaning at all, and halving the difference confuses cylinder with the spherical equivalent shift.
  178. The correct answer is D (the back is toric, so record the spherical front surface reading): a needle that holds one value everywhere on the front and swings between two values on the back is the signature of a spherical front with a toric back, which is the standard modern form and the reason minus cylinder is the working convention in Canadian practice. Base curve is the front surface reading unless the order says otherwise, so +4.00 D is what goes on the sheet. Neither back meridian is the base curve on a back toric lens. A front toric design would show the swing on the front instead, and averaging readings from both surfaces has no meaning as a base curve.
  179. The correct answer is A (rock the clock gently and read the needle at its extreme): all three pins must touch at once with the instrument held perpendicular to the surface, and rocking it slightly finds the position where it is truly normal, which is where the needle reaches its extreme value. Tilting the clock shortens the effective chord and gives a flatter, unrepeatable reading. Sliding out to the bevel puts a pin on ground glass rather than the optical surface, so the reading is meaningless. Pressing harder simply bottoms the spring, and lifting a pin destroys the fixed chord the whole measurement depends on.
  180. The correct answer is D (the convex surface reads plus and the concave surface reads minus): a surface that bulges toward the instrument pushes the centre pin in and reads plus, while a surface curving away lets the centre pin extend and reads minus. On a conventional lens that gives a plus front and a minus back, which is exactly what the strong minus lens in this scenario shows. The sign follows the physical shape of the surface, not the power of the finished lens or the way the lens sits in the frame. Reading the hollow inner surface as plus would reverse the convention and produce a nonsensical surface power sum.
  181. The correct answer is C (plus or minus 0.75 dioptres either side of the specified base): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows about three quarters of a dioptre either side of a base curve when one has actually been specified on the order. That is a generous allowance compared with power tolerances because base curve affects cosmetics and off axis performance rather than the prescription itself. The 0.13 dioptre figure belongs to sphere meridian power at low powers, and 1.00 dioptre is the separate allowance for warpage. There is no 0.25 dioptre base curve figure, and the chart supplied in the exam room is the value that counts on the day.
  182. The correct answer is B (return it, since the true 7.74 D is 1.74 D off specification): the polycarbonate factor is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057, so 7.00 times 1.1057 gives 7.740, which is +7.74 D. That sits 1.74 D above the specified 6.00 D base, well beyond the three quarters of a dioptre the standard allows, so the lens goes back to the laboratory. Comparing the raw +7.00 D reading against specification skips the correction and understates the error as 1.00 D. Inverting the factor gives +6.33 D and would wrongly pass the lens, and the base curve allowance is 0.75 D rather than 1.00 D.
  183. The correct answer is A (not within tolerance, the true base curve is 8.85 D): the 1.67 factor is 0.67 divided by 0.53, or 1.2642, and 7.00 times 1.2642 gives 8.849, rounding to 8.85 D. That is 0.85 D above the specified 8.00 D base, which exceeds the 0.75 D allowance, so the component fails. The raw +7.00 D reading is 1.00 D away from specification and would also fail, but accepting it as close enough ignores the correction the material demands. Inverting the factor gives 5.54 D, a value that could never come from a surface ordered at 8.00 D, and 8.60 D does not follow from any correct working.
  184. The correct answer is B (outside, because the true base curve works out to 6.98 D): the 1.74 factor is 0.74 divided by 0.53, or 1.3962, and 5.00 times 1.3962 gives 6.981, rounding to 6.98 D. The difference from the specified 6.00 D base is 0.98 D, which is larger than the 0.75 D allowance, so the base curve component fails. Using the 1.67 factor by mistake gives 6.32 D and would wrongly pass the lens, which is why the material must be confirmed before the arithmetic. Leaving the raw 5.00 D reading uncorrected is exactly the habit that fails high index work, and inverting the factor gives an impossible 3.58 D.
  185. The correct answer is D (+9.06 D, the reading raised by the 1.60 index factor): the factor is 0.60 divided by 0.53, which is 1.1321, and 8.00 times 1.1321 gives 9.057, rounding to +9.06 D. A 1.60 material sits above the 1.53 calibration index, so the true surface power is always larger than the dial shows. Recording +8.00 D leaves out a full dioptre of surface power on a curve this steep. Inverting the factor gives 8.00 times 0.8833, or +7.07 D, and multiplying by 0.60 alone gives +4.80 D, both of which flatten the surface rather than steepening it.
  186. Both surfaces of a polycarbonate lens need the same factor, so correcting the front and leaving the back raw would produce a badly wrong surface power sum. The raw -9.00 D understates the surface by nearly a dioptre. Inverting the factor gives -8.14 D and multiplying by 0.586 alone gives -5.27 D, neither of which follows the formula.
  187. Inverting the factor gives -5.30 D, and adding the corrected values as magnitudes gives -13.59 D, which no real lens would show.
  188.   189.   190. The correct answer is A (its index of 1.53 matches the index the dial assumes): the lens clock dial is engraved for n = 1.53, so on any 1.53 material the factor becomes 0.53 divided by 0.53, which is exactly one, and the dial reading is already the true surface power. Polycarbonate at 1.586 gives a factor of 1.1057, so its readings must be raised by about 11 percent. The correction has nothing to do with density, since the clock measures shape and not weight. Abbe value governs chromatic aberration, and the form of the front surface has no bearing on the calibration index of the dial.
  191. The correct answer is C (read the back surface and recorded it as the base curve): a negative value in the base curve box on a conventional lens is the classic sign that the concave back was measured instead of the convex front. On a conventional lens the front reads plus and only the back reads minus, so the sign itself gives the mistake away. Base curve means the front surface unless the order specifies a front toric design, and the candidate must state which surface was measured when it does. A sign written down backwards, an off perpendicular hold, or a missing index correction would all change the number without producing a clean minus value like this.
  192. The correct answer is D (CR39, because its index of 1.498 sits very near 1.53): the CR39 factor is 0.498 divided by 0.53, or 0.9396, so an uncorrected reading is only about 6 percent out, and on a 6.00 D base that is a 0.36 D error, inside the base curve allowance. Polycarbonate at 1.586 gives a factor of 1.1057, an error of about 11 percent, and 1.67 material gives 1.2642, an error of about 26 percent, both of which blow straight past three quarters of a dioptre on a moderate curve. A 1.74 material is worse again at about 40 percent. High index curves are not inherently flat or low reading, so neither high index option is safe to read raw.
  193. The correct answer is B (3.61 mm, from a radius of 88.33 mm across the blank): the radius is 0.53 divided by 6.00, which is 0.08833 m, or 88.33 mm. Sag equals the radius minus the square root of the radius squared minus the semi diameter squared, so 88.33 minus the square root of 7802.19 minus 625, which is 88.33 minus 84.72, giving 3.61 mm. The 132.5 mm radius belongs to a 4.00 D base and yields 2.38 mm, while a 66.25 mm radius belongs to an 8.00 D base. Sag does not simply double when the semi diameter is used, because the relationship with chord width is not linear.
  194. The correct answer is C (the steeper base bulges the lens forward and out of a shallow frame): the 6.00 D base is 1.23 mm deeper across the same 50 mm blank than the 4.00 D base, and that extra depth stands in front of the frame plane, so the lens sits proud of a shallow rim. Centre substance is not what changes, because steepening the front by 2.00 D forces the back 2.00 D more concave and the two sagittal changes cancel, leaving centre and edge thickness essentially fixed for a given power. Flattening the base settles the lens into the rim, which is why laboratories flatten for cosmetics, though flattening too far degrades off axis performance. The flatter base is the shallower one, so it cannot be the choice that deepens the lens, and treating base curve as an edge thickness effect misses the plate height entirely.
  195. The correct answer is A (it rises to about 5.25 mm because the chord is wider): with the radius unchanged at 88.33 mm and the semi diameter now 30 mm, sag equals 88.33 minus the square root of 7802.19 minus 900, which is 88.33 minus 83.08, giving 5.25 mm. Sag grows as the chord widens even though the curve itself is untouched, which is why a larger blank on the same base carries more substance. The 3.61 mm value belongs to the 50 mm blank and cannot survive a change of diameter. Scaling the old sag by the ratio of diameters gives 4.33 mm and is wrong, because sag rises roughly with the square of the semi diameter rather than in direct proportion.
  196. The correct answer is B (about 6.00 D for plano, flattening as minus power increases further): classic base curve selection guidance puts a plano lens near a 6.00 D base, flattens the base as minus power rises to keep edge thickness and edge rings under control, and steepens it as plus power rises. Steepening a strong minus lens would push the edge thicker and heavier for no optical gain. A 2.00 D base is far too flat for plano work and would degrade off axis performance. Holding one very steep base across the whole power range ignores both cosmetics and peripheral optics.
  197. The correct answer is D (the lens has warped in the frame, adding surface cylinder): a front surface ordered spherical that now reads two different values ninety degrees apart has been bent, and a tight plastic frame gripping a lens for a year is a common cause. The 1.00 D spread between meridians is the measure of that warpage. A genuine front toric design would have been ground that way from the start and would appear on the work order, which specified a spherical front here. A lens clock that has lost calibration shifts every reading by the same amount rather than creating a difference between meridians, and a missing index correction scales both meridians equally instead of separating them.
  198. The correct answer is C (plus or minus 1.00 dioptres, a separate warpage allowance entirely): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR carries a warpage allowance of 1.00 D, which is listed separately from the base curve allowance and applies to bending found in the finished lens. A 1.00 D spread therefore sits right at the limit and should be flagged and rechecked rather than waved through. The 0.75 D figure applies to a specified base curve, not to warpage, and 0.13 D belongs to sphere meridian power at low powers. There is no half dioptre warpage figure, and the chart supplied in the exam room is the version that governs on the day.
  199. The back surface reading is recorded separately if the sheet asks for it, but it is not the base curve. Adding the two surfaces gives an approximate lens power that ignores centre thickness, which is a useful cross check rather than a base curve entry. Choosing whichever surface happens to read steeper would put a back curve in the base curve box on almost every minus lens.

Recording the raw -8.00 D would wreck any surface power sum built on it. Inverting the factor gives -5.73 D and multiplying by 0.74 alone gives -5.92 D, neither of which follows the formula.
  203. The correct answer is C (2.21 D, from correcting each meridian and taking the difference): the polycarbonate factor is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057, so the flatter meridian becomes 4.00 times 1.1057, which is 4.42 D, and the steeper becomes 6.00 times 1.1057, which is 6.63 D. The difference of 6.63 minus 4.42 is 2.21 D, the same as correcting the raw 2.00 D difference directly. The correction does not cancel across the meridians, because it scales both readings and therefore scales the gap between them as well. Inverting the factor gives 1.81 D, and adding the two corrected meridians gives 11.05 D, which is not a cylinder at all.
  204. The correct answer is D (the 6.00 D base gives the thicker edge and heavier lens): holding power and centre thickness fixed, a steeper front forces an even steeper back, and the back sag grows faster than the front sag, so substance piles up at the edge. Working it through on a 60 mm blank for a lens around four dioptres of minus, the 2.00 D base gives roughly a 5.0 mm edge and the 6.00 D base roughly a 5.6 mm edge. That is why laboratories flatten the base as minus power rises, trading a little off axis performance for a thinner, lighter edge. Edge thickness therefore depends on both power and base curve, and it is not fixed by centre thickness alone.
  205. The correct answer is B (take the reading at the centre with all pins on the surface): the base curve reading is taken at the centre of the lens with all three pins on the optical surface and the clock held perpendicular. A pin riding on the bevel sits on ground edge work rather than the polished curve, which shortens the effective chord and flattens the reading unpredictably. Averaging several bad readings does not remove a systematic error, it only hides it. Recording the flatter value keeps the error, and applying the index correction twice compounds one mistake with another instead of fixing the contact problem.
  206. The correct answer is C (strong plus lenses can read plus on both surfaces normally): a strong plus lens can be made with a convex front and a convex back, and some flat front designs do the same, so a plus value on both sides is a legitimate result rather than an instrument fault. The candidate records both readings with their true signs and adds them for the surface power check. Reversing the sign of the back reading to force a minus would falsify the record and wreck the sum. Front toric designs are defined by a swing across the front meridians, not by the sign on both surfaces, and a clock reading plausible values on both sides is not showing a calibration failure.
  207. The correct answer is A (the surface power sum ignores the centre thickness of the lens): adding two corrected surface powers gives a thin lens approximation, and a thick plus lens carries enough substance that its real back vertex power drifts from that sum. A 0.20 D gap on a thick plus lens is the expected consequence rather than a fault in either instrument. The lensometer reads back vertex power, which is the value the prescription is written to, so it is the reference and the surface sum is the rough check. Correcting only the front surface would leave a much larger error, and the lens clock never reads finished lens power at all.
  208. The correct answer is D (within tolerance, the true base curve is 4.42 D): the polycarbonate factor is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057, and 4.00 times 1.1057 gives 4.423, so the true base curve is 4.42 D. That sits 0.42 D from the specified 4.00 D base, inside the three quarters of a dioptre the standard allows, so the component passes. Calling the same 4.42 D value a failure misapplies the allowance, since 0.42 D is comfortably under 0.75 D. The raw reading does need correcting, but the corrected result still passes, and inverting the factor gives 3.62 D, which is not the working the formula calls for.
  209. The correct answer is C (the concave back reading was recorded as plus instead of minus): on a conventional minus lens the front is convex and reads plus while the back is concave and must be recorded as minus, so the back value should have been -8.00 D and the sum should have been about -2.00 D rather than +14.00 D. A single dropped sign is enough to swing the estimate by sixteen dioptres, which is exactly the size of the discrepancy described. A missing index correction shifts a result by 6 to 26 percent, nowhere near enough to explain this. Reading the wrong surface would not produce two plus values, and the two surface powers are genuinely added, not subtracted.
  210. The correct answer is B (+5.43 D, the true power divided by the index factor): the polycarbonate factor is 0.586 divided by 0.53, or 1.1057, and this question runs the correction backwards, so 6.00 divided by 1.1057 gives 5.427, rounding to +5.43 D. The dial always reads lower than the truth on a material above 1.53, which is the same relationship seen from the other direction. Multiplying by the factor gives +6.63 D and would be right only if 6.00 D were the dial reading rather than the target. Assuming the dial shows true power ignores the calibration index entirely, and multiplying by 0.586 alone gives +3.52 D.
  211. The correct answer is A (-4.75 D, the true power divided by the index factor): the 1.67 factor is 0.67 divided by 0.53, or 1.2642, and going from true power back to a dial reading means dividing, so 6.00 divided by 1.2642 gives 4.746, rounding to -4.75 D. On any material above 1.53 the dial understates the surface, so the expected reading is flatter than the true power. Multiplying instead of dividing gives -7.58 D and applies the correction twice. The dial and the true power agree only on a 1.53 material, and multiplying by 0.67 alone gives -4.02 D, which skips the 0.53 denominator.
  212. The correct answer is D (about 26 percent, so a strong surface is badly understated): the factor for 1.67 material is 0.67 divided by 0.53, which is 1.2642, so the true surface power is about 26 percent higher than the dial shows. On an 8.00 D reading that is a gap of more than two dioptres, far outside the three quarters of a dioptre allowed on a specified base curve. About 11 percent is the polycarbonate figure at 1.586 and about 40 percent belongs to 1.74 material, so neither applies here. A 6 percent shift is what CR39 shows, and it is the only common material where reading straight off the dial is defensible.
  213. The correct answer is B (the surface sum of -4.53 D agrees closely with the lensometer): the 1.60 factor is 0.60 divided by 0.53, or 1.1321, so the front becomes 2.00 times 1.1321, which is +2.26 D, and the back becomes 6.00 times 1.1321, which is -6.79 D. Adding them gives -4.53 D, within 0.03 D of the -4.50 D read on the lensometer, which is as close as a thin lens surface sum can be expected to come. The uncorrected sum of -4.00 D would suggest a problem that does not exist. Inverting the factor gives -3.53 D, and adding the corrected values as magnitudes gives -9.05 D, neither of which is a valid surface power sum.
  214. The correct answer is C (Distance powers, adds, dot both lenses, PDs, segment heights, then base curve): power is taken while the instrument is already set up and the eyepiece is fresh, and dotting straight afterwards creates the reference points every later measurement depends on. The PD is measured dot to dot, so it cannot be recorded before those dots exist, which rules out any sequence that puts PDs or heights ahead of the lensometer work. Starting with the base curve or the heights wastes the instrument setup and forces the pair to be handled twice. Reading power on all five pairs and returning later means finding, reseating and re-centring each pair a second time, and that rebuilding is where candidates lose the clock.
  215. The correct answer is A (About -3.39 D, after correcting both surface readings for index): the lens clock dial is engraved for n = 1.53, so each reading is multiplied by (1.60 minus 1) divided by 0.53, which is 1.1321. The front becomes 4.00 x 1.1321 = +4.53 D and the back becomes 7.00 x 1.1321 = -7.92 D, giving a total of -3.39 D. Adding the raw dial figures gives -3.00 D and ignores the index entirely. Correcting only the front leaves -2.47 D, and using the polycarbonate multiplier of 1.1057 instead of the 1.60 multiplier gives -3.32 D, which is close enough to look right and wrong enough to fail a base curve check.
  216. The correct answer is D (1.50 prism dioptres base out, taken across the pair): the centres are 70 mm apart against a specified 64 mm, so the total error is 6 mm and each eye carries 3 mm. Prentice's rule in millimetre form gives (3 x 2.50) divided by 10 = 0.75 prism dioptres in each lens, and the two lenses add to 1.50 prism dioptres across the pair. Because the centres are too far apart, each line of sight passes nasal to its own centre, and a plus lens points its base back toward its own centre, which is temporal, so the direction is base out. Working from the full 6 mm without halving it gives 3.00, and reporting only one lens gives 0.75, both of which misstate what the pair actually carries.
  217. The correct answer is B (1.80 prism dioptres, and the 4 mm difference fails the tolerance): the heights differ by 26 minus 22 = 4 mm, and with equal powers the imbalance is (4 x 4.50) divided by 10 = 1.80 prism dioptres. The ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR judges the vertical difference on a mounted pair by the tighter of a 1 mm height difference or about a third of a prism dioptre, and above roughly 3.37 dioptres the prism figure binds first, so at 4.50 dioptres a 4 mm difference is far outside either reading. Dividing by 10 twice produces 0.18 and forgetting to convert millimetres to centimetres produces 18.0, and both are arithmetic slips rather than optical ones. Calling a 4 mm difference acceptable ignores the tightest linear allowance on the whole mounting standard.
  218. The correct answer is D (Monocular distance PDs and fitting cross heights for each eye): a progressive owes monocular figures because the standard requires each reference point to sit within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular PD, and a binocular figure split down the middle cannot deliver that on an asymmetric face. It also owes a fitting cross height for each eye, since the cross must sit at the pupil centre and the two eyes frequently differ. The base curve and the axis were still measured correctly and remain valid entries. Segment size, segment drop and an intermediate add belong to lined multifocals and trifocals, not to a progressive, and a distance power read at the distance reference point is still a legitimate back vertex reading.
  219. The correct answer is A (About 11.7 minutes each, so keep the routine and stop re-checking): 75 minus 40 leaves 35 minutes for three pairs, which is 11.7 minutes each, so the candidate is running roughly three minutes behind the 15 minute pace but is not in trouble. The recovery comes from cutting repeat readings and second looks, not from cutting whole measurements, because every blank box is a guaranteed loss while a slightly rushed reading may still score. Reading powers only abandons the PDs, heights and base curves that Section 2 explicitly asks for. The figures of 15 minutes and 8.3 minutes come from ignoring the elapsed time or dividing the wrong remainder, and both lead to the wrong decision.
  220. The correct answer is C (The right axis, so re-read it against the focused line orientation): with minus cylinder at axis 090 the cylinder acts in the 180 meridian, so the more minus meridian of the right lens should run horizontally, not vertically as the clock shows. The left entry at axis 180 puts its more minus meridian vertical, which is exactly what the clock found, so the left record is consistent. A 90 degree axis error is the commonest single mistake in this section and it is caught by cross checking the wheel against the orientation of the lines that focused. The sphere is not implicated because an eyepiece error shifts both lenses together, and turning further into minus would only add a cylinder that is not there.
  221. The correct answer is B (Base curve, which takes seconds with a lens clock): Section 2 records power plus the physical aspects normally recorded, and base curve is named among them alongside the PDs and the segment or fitting cross height. Base curve is the outstanding entry among the items offered here, and the lens clock reading costs about ten seconds. Vertex distance belongs to Section 3 and pantoscopic tilt belongs to bench alignment work, so neither is a Section 2 recording. Segment drop is measured from the major reference point down to the segment top and is a different quantity from the segment height already on the sheet, so it does not replace the missing base curve.
  222. The correct answer is D (Focus the eyepiece, then check ring value and which lines focus first): the eyepiece setting depends on the operator's own eye and the instrument was randomly assigned, so it may still carry the previous candidate's setting and shift every sphere reading in the same direction. Familiarization is also the moment to confirm whether each reticle ring equals one prism dioptre or half of one, and to establish with a lens of known power which set of target lines focuses first on that model. Practising on the strongest lens available tells the candidate nothing about the unknown pairs and burns the time. The moderator will not disclose the powers of the unknowns, and repeated dotting of a spare lens rehearses the easiest step in the whole routine.
  223. Bases in the same direction partly cancel for fusion, so the net imbalance is 5.85 minus 2.70 = 3.15 prism dioptres, and the power difference route confirms it as 0.9 x 3.50 = 3.15. Adding the two values instead of subtracting gives 8.55, which would describe a pair with opposing bases rather than this one. Dividing by 10 a second time gives 0.32, and calling the excess base up reverses the rule for a minus lens with the line of sight below centre.
  224. The correct answer is A (True curve is +6.36 D, within the three quarter dioptre allowance): polycarbonate at n = 1.586 gives a multiplier of 0.586 divided by 0.53 = 1.1057, so the dial figure of +5.75 D corresponds to a true surface power of 5.75 x 1.1057 = +6.36 D. That sits 0.36 D from the ordered 6.00 D base, comfortably inside the roughly three quarter dioptre allowance the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR gives a specified base curve. Inverting the ratio and multiplying by 0.53 divided by 0.586 gives +5.20 D, which would wrongly condemn a good lens. Recording the raw +5.75 D ignores the index correction altogether, and above n = 1.53 the multiplier is always greater than one, so the true curve is always steeper than the dial says.
  225. Prentice's rule gives (5 x 5.00) divided by 10 = 2.50 prism dioptres in each lens. Because the optical centre sits temporal to the line of sight, and a minus lens points its base away from its own centre in the same direction as the displacement, the base is nasal, which is base in. Calling it base out reverses the minus lens rule, halving the 5 mm again gives 1.25, and skipping the division by 10 gives an impossible 25.0.
  226. The correct answer is D (The whole pair, so label the sheet before reading anything): on this examination the record sheet is the answer, and a correct measurement written in the wrong field scores the same as a wrong one, so every swapped entry on that pair is lost. Labelling the eye columns before the first reading, and always working right lens then left lens, removes the entire class of error for the pairs still to come. The powers being individually accurate does not rescue them, because the marker reads the sheet and not the candidate's intention. Re-reading only the adds or only the heights would leave the sphere, cylinder, axis and PD entries still transposed.
  227. A figure that lands between zero and the full add is the signature of a reading taken in the corridor, where the power is only partway through its progression and unwanted cylinder makes a clean focus impossible in both meridians. Recording the +1.75 and ignoring the engraving would put a corridor value on the sheet as if it were the add. Nothing here supports a blended bifocal, which shows a smudged circular boundary and carries no permanent engravings at all.
  228. The correct answer is C (Add +3.00, because a front surface segment is read from the front): the add must be measured from the surface that carries the addition, and almost every lined multifocal made today carries its segment on the front. Two back vertex readings on a thick plus lens magnify the segment contribution by the lens substance, which is why the naive subtraction of +9.35 minus +6.00 over-reads at +3.35 D. That 0.35 D error is nearly three times the 0.12 D the standard allows on an add up to +4.00 D, so recording it would put a good lens outside tolerance on the sheet. Averaging the two methods at +3.18 has no optical basis, and the segment reading being numerically larger says nothing about which method is valid.
  229. The correct answer is D (A 70 percent trifocal, so record the intermediate as +1.75 D): dividing the intermediate by the near add gives 1.75 divided by 2.50 = 0.70, one of the standard manufactured ratios along with 40 and 50 percent. A ratio that lands on a stock value confirms the two readings agree with each other, so both can be recorded as measured. A 50 percent design would have produced an intermediate of +1.25 D and a 40 percent design +1.00 D, so neither of those figures belongs on this sheet. The self check only calls for re-reading when the ratio lands somewhere odd, such as 0.62, which is the warning that one of the two readings is wrong.
  230. The correct answer is B (Distance powers and adds for both eyes, then whatever time allows): power is what Section 2 is named for, it carries the heaviest weight on the sheet, and the lensometer is already the instrument in the candidate's hands. Four distance readings and two adds are achievable in four minutes on a lined bifocal, while the same four minutes spent on rulers and a lens clock leaves the entire power block blank. Segment size, base curve, segment heights and the near PD are all worth marks, but each is a single small entry compared with the power block. Starting with the PD and the optical centre heights also depends on dotting the lenses, which means setting up the lensometer anyway and then not using it for power.
  231. The correct answer is A (1 mm each eye and 0.15 prism dioptres per lens, both acceptable): the dots measure 66 mm against a specified 64 mm, a 2 mm total error shared as 1 mm per eye, and Prentice's rule gives (1 x 1.50) divided by 10 = 0.15 prism dioptres in each lens. The ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows a mounted single vision reference point to sit within 2.5 mm of the specified distance PD, so 1 mm passes comfortably. Unwanted prism at the reference point is held at or below 0.33 prism dioptres, and 0.15 in each lens keeps the pair inside that too. Assigning the whole 2 mm error to one eye doubles both figures, and quoting 1.50 prism dioptres per lens omits the division by 10 entirely.
  232. The correct answer is C (Height 17 mm and drop 4 mm, measured from the reference point): segment height is measured from the lowest point of the lens aperture straight up to the top edge of the segment, which the candidate has already established as 17 mm. With a 42 mm B measurement and no vertical decentration the major reference point sits 21 mm above that same lowest point, so the segment top lies 21 minus 17 = 4 mm below it, and that is the segment drop. Quoting the height as 21 mm confuses the reference point position with the segment position. Reversing the two values reports the drop as the height, which is the single most common confusion between these two measurements, and 25 mm is the sum rather than the difference.
  233. That 4 mm total reduction sits in the usual 3 to 4 mm range for a 40 cm working distance. Adding the inset instead of subtracting it produces 34, 33 and 67, which describes eyes diverging for near work. Halving the inset gives 31, 30 and 61, and quoting 63 mm as the binocular total contradicts the monocular figures written beside it, which is the kind of internal conflict a marker spots immediately.

Add the sphere and cylinder algebraically for the new sphere, -1.25 plus +2.00 = +0.75, change the cylinder sign to -2.00, and move the axis 90 degrees, subtracting because the original 105 is greater than 90, which gives 015 written as three digits. Subtracting the cylinder from the sphere instead of adding it gives -3.25, and adding 90 to an axis already past 90 gives 195, which is outside the 0 to 180 scale. The add is read as for any other round segment, at the centre of the circle and by the method matching the surface that carries the addition. Those are different targets on the lens, so the recorded numbers are not interchangeable, and the segment type entry is wrong as well because the pair is a lined bifocal and not a progressive. When the dots do not match the ordered PD, the prism found in the lens is almost certainly the product of that centring error rather than anything the prescriber asked for, and the sheet records the amount measured and the base direction observed.   241. The correct answer is B (Take the three missing base curves with the lens clock): base curve is one of the physical aspects Section 2 records, each reading takes about ten seconds with the clock, and three blank boxes are three certain losses that six minutes can convert into three certain marks. Re-reading axes or re-measuring PDs spends the time on boxes that already contain values, which at best confirms what is written and at worst introduces a change based on a rushed second look. Writing a typical value into a blank records a figure that was never measured, and a plausible guess on a high index or strongly powered lens will usually be wrong once the index correction is considered. Filling genuine blanks always outranks polishing entries that are already made.
  242. The correct answer is A (A front toric lens, so record both front readings and say so): rocking the clock across a surface and watching the needle change means that surface carries two principal meridians, and here the change is on the front while the back holds one steady value. That is a front toric lens, uncommon in new work but still met in older glasses, and the sheet must name the surface measured so a second optician is not left guessing. A back toric lens is the usual modern arrangement and would show the opposite pattern, a steady front and a changing back. Warpage is judged against the specified curve rather than diagnosed from two normal principal meridians, and a fitting cross is a progressive reference point that has no bearing on a single vision pair.
  243. On a lensometer the target moves toward the base, so a target displaced toward the patient's nose in the right lens means the base points nasally, which is base in. Assuming one dioptre per ring gives 3.00, which is the mistake the familiarization check exists to prevent, and reading the direction as base out reverses the target rule. A figure of 0.50 counts one ring instead of three.
  244. Running the usual sequence on both, and taking the highest value entries first, harvests power and PD marks from four lenses instead of every possible mark from two. Finishing one pair and leaving the other blank forfeits an entire pair's worth of boxes for the sake of a few extra entries on the first. Taking base curves before any power reading spends the scarce time on the smallest entries, and re-checking every reading twice guarantees the second pair is never touched at all.
  245. Adds do differ between the eyes and segment heights differ even more often, which is exactly why the second eye must be read rather than assumed. The right add is not affected by the left being copied, so treating both as lost overstates the damage and would waste time re-reading a sound value. The heights were separately measured and remain valid, and treating equal adds as normal practice is the assumption that caused the error.
  246. The candidate has already done the required cross check by taking both readings twice, so the engraved 200 has served its purpose as a sanity check and the measured value stands. Writing +2.00 substitutes the manufacturer's nominal marking for the candidate's own measurement, which is not what the section is testing. Recording a measured figure and then altering it to fit a tolerance turns a neutralization record into a verification decision, and the two are different tasks on this examination.
