A segment area is not present on a single vision lens, and a reading taken near the edge or on the bevel gives an unstable needle rather than a clean value of the wrong sign.
  250. The correct answer is D (The right lens is spherical and should be recorded as DS): when both sets of target lines sharpen together at one drum position, the lens has no cylinder, and the entry should read -1.50 DS or -1.50 sphere. Turning further because a cylinder was expected invents a cylinder that the lens does not carry, which is the classic error of stopping at a slightly ragged focus. The left lens focused its two meridians separately and produced a genuine -0.75 cylinder at 175, so that entry is a real measurement and must not be rounded to match the right. The sphere on the right is not in doubt, since an eyepiece problem would have shifted both lenses in the same direction rather than affecting one, and moving the invented cylinder to another axis does not make it real.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The right horizontal location, which exceeds the one millimetre allowance): the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR holds a mounted progressive to within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular PD horizontally in each lens, so the right lens at 1.5 mm is outside and the left at 0.5 mm is inside. The vertical difference between the two lenses is 0.5 mm, which is also within the 1.0 mm allowed, so nothing fails on the vertical axis. The 2.5 mm figure applies to mounted single vision and lined multifocal work, not to progressives, which is precisely why every progressive job carries monocular figures. Applying the looser allowance here would pass a lens whose line of sight sits beside the corridor in the blend zone.
  2. The correct answer is B (17 mm right and 19 mm left, recorded separately for each eye): a neutralization sheet records what the lenses actually measure, per eye, and segment heights very often differ between the two eyes on a real job. Substituting the ordered 18 mm records the work order rather than the lenses, which defeats the purpose of neutralizing them. Averaging the two readings hides a 2 mm difference that a verification step would need to see, and it produces a figure that matches neither lens. Taking the lower reading for both discards a genuine measurement on the left, and no rule makes the smaller of two heights govern a pair.
  3. The correct answer is C (0.80 prism dioptres per lens, base in for the wearer): the centres are 62 mm apart against a specified 66 mm, so the total error is 4 mm and each eye carries 2 mm, and Prentice's rule gives (2 x 4.00) divided by 10 = 0.80 prism dioptres in each lens. Because the centres sit too close together, each line of sight passes temporal to its own centre, and a plus lens points its base back toward its own centre, which is nasal, so the direction is base in. Calling it base out reverses the plus lens rule and would also reverse the advice given to the laboratory. Using the full 4 mm without halving it gives 1.60, and dividing by 10 twice gives 0.08.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reveal and re-ink the engravings, giving the cross and near circle): the two permanent circles sit 34 mm apart and anchor the whole layout, and once they are dotted and laid on the marking chart for that design the fitting cross, prism reference point, distance circle and near circle can all be transferred. Without those marks the candidate cannot centre the lens on the distance reference point for the power reading, cannot find the near circle for the add, and cannot measure a fitting cross height. Taking the base curve first does not locate any reference point, and corridor length is not read from a lens clock. A binocular PD is the wrong measurement for a progressive in any case, and reading power in the corridor gives a fraction of the add with unwanted cylinder attached.
  5. The correct answer is D (A one millimetre error each eye, giving swim and narrow reading): splitting a 64 mm binocular PD assumes both eyes sit equally far from the bridge, but the real monocular figures are 33 mm and 31 mm, so each eye is placed 1 mm out. On a progressive that is enough to push the line of sight into the blend zone beside the corridor, where the design's unwanted cylinder lives, and the wearer reports swim and a narrow reading field. It also puts each reference point exactly on the 1.0 mm horizontal allowance the standard sets for a mounted progressive, leaving no margin for any other layout error. The error is 1 mm rather than 2 mm per eye, a horizontal displacement does not by itself create vertical imbalance, and the effect reaches far beyond the distance sphere. Treating the matching binocular total as sufficient is exactly the assumption that causes the problem.
  6. The correct answer is C (Re-take the reading, checking the clock is on the front surface): a +2.00 D base under a +7.00 DS lens contradicts the classic pattern of steepening the base as plus power rises, so the reading deserves one quick repeat before it goes on the sheet. The most likely cause is measuring the wrong surface, since base curve means the front surface unless the order says otherwise, and a strong plus lens can read plus on both sides, which makes the mix up easy. Writing a suspect figure without a second look records a probable error, and the clock reading shape rather than power is not a reason to skip the check. Not every plus lens is high index, so an index correction is not automatically due, and averaging the front and back readings produces a number that describes neither surface.
  7. The correct answer is B (Finish each pair completely once, and stop returning to earlier pairs): 53 minutes across four pairs is a little over 13 minutes each, which is workable only if no pair is handled twice. Coming back to a pair means finding it, reseating it, re-centring the target and rebuilding the candidate's place in the routine, and that rebuilding is the single largest hidden cost in this section. Reading powers on all four pairs first guarantees four second handlings later. Dropping the base curve throws away marks that take seconds each to earn, and switching to back vertex subtraction for adds saves almost no time while over-reading the add on any plus or thick lens.
  8. The correct answer is A (The near PD, since convergence makes it smaller than the distance PD): the eyes converge for near work, so a measured near PD normally runs about 3 to 4 mm less than the distance PD at a 40 cm working distance, which would put this pair near 60 or 61 mm rather than 66 mm. A near figure larger than the distance figure describes eyes diverging for near, which does not happen, so that entry is the one to re-take. The distance PD of 64 mm is an entirely ordinary figure and nothing on the sheet challenges it. A 10 mm gap between distance and near is far larger than convergence produces, and equal distance and near figures would ignore convergence altogether.
  9. The correct answer is D (Monocular distance and near PDs, and fitting cross height per eye): a progressive record must carry monocular figures for both distance and near, because the standard holds each reference point to within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular PD and a binocular total split in half cannot deliver that on an asymmetric face. It must also carry a fitting cross height for each eye, measured from the lowest point of the aperture up to the cross, since the two eyes commonly differ. Segment size, segment drop and an intermediate add belong to lined multifocals and trifocals rather than to a progressive. Vertex distance and pantoscopic tilt are not Section 2 entries, corridor length is a design specification and not a measurement, and the powers and axes shown are already complete.
  10. The correct answer is B (Check every box is filled, legible, and labelled by the correct eye): the sheet is the answer on this section, so a value in the wrong row, an illegible digit or an empty box costs the same as a wrong reading, and a two minute sweep can catch all three across five pairs. That sweep also confirms that powers carry two decimal places and signs, and that axes are written as three digits, which prevents a 5 being read as 50. Re-reading one sphere per pair spends the time on entries that already exist and risks changing a sound value on a rushed second look. Re-measuring base curves has the same problem, and rewriting the whole sheet in two minutes invites fresh transcription errors on values that were already correct.
  11. The correct answer is B (the visual axis of the eye looking through that lens): the optical centre carries no prism, so it must sit in front of the line the eye actually sights along, which is why the corneal reflex is used as the visible landmark. The pupil centre shifts as pupil size changes with light and accommodation, so it is not a stable reference. The bridge midline is only the reference used for splitting a monocular value, not the target for the optical centre. The centre of the lens opening is a frame landmark and rarely coincides with either visual axis.
  12. The correct answer is D (2.00 prism dioptres base out, taken across the whole pair): a 4 mm error in the binocular figure decentres each lens by 2 mm, which is 0.2 cm, so each lens gives 0.2 x 5.00 = 1.00 prism dioptres and the two add to 2.00. The optical centres sit too far nasally, and a minus lens throws its base away from the centre, so both eyes pick up base out. Stopping at 1.00 reports one lens and ignores the second eye. Leaving the decentration in millimetres inflates the result tenfold, and calling it base in misreads how a minus lens behaves.
  13. The correct answer is A (induced prism rises in direct proportion to the lens power): Prentice's rule multiplies the decentration in centimetres by the power, so the same 2 mm per eye gives 0.10 prism dioptres on a +0.50 lens and 1.20 prism dioptres on a -6.00 lens. Only the second figure is large enough to force fusional work and produce symptoms. Base curve, lens weight, and slippage change comfort but do not set the amount of induced prism. Minus lenses minify rather than magnify, and image size is not what creates the prismatic demand.
  14. The correct answer is C (1.60 prism dioptres base in across the finished pair): the 4 mm error splits into 2 mm per eye, which is 0.2 cm, so each lens gives 0.2 x 4.00 = 0.80 prism dioptres and the pair totals 1.60. The centres sit too far temporally, and a minus lens throws its base away from the centre, so each eye sees base in. Quoting 0.80 reports one lens only. Applying the full 4 mm to each eye doubles the answer to 3.20, and calling it base out reverses how a minus lens works.
  15. The correct answer is A (1.75 prism dioptres of unwanted prism through that single lens): Prentice's rule needs the decentration in centimetres, so 2.5 mm becomes 0.25 cm and 0.25 x 7.00 = 1.75. Leaving the figure in millimetres gives 17.50, which is ten times too large and should look obviously wrong for a 2.5 mm error. Dividing the power by the decentration instead of multiplying gives 2.80. Dividing by 100 rather than by 10 produces 0.18.
  16. The correct answer is B (a binocular figure cannot say where the nose sits within it): 63 mm could be 32/31, 34/29, or anything else that adds to 63, and each split puts the corridor in a different place. A progressive must be centred to each eye separately because its corridor and near zone are narrow and unforgiving. Blank diameter and corridor length are design choices that do not tell the lab where to place the centres. A lensometer can read a finished progressive through its permanent markings, so verification is not the obstacle.
  17. The correct answer is D (1.50 prism dioptres base out over the finished pair): the 3 mm error gives 1.5 mm per eye, which is 0.15 cm, so each lens produces 0.15 x 5.00 = 0.75 prism dioptres and the pair totals 1.50. A plus lens throws its base toward the optical centre, and the centres sit too far temporally, so both eyes get base out. Reporting 0.75 covers one lens only. Applying the whole 3 mm to each eye doubles the figure to 3.00, and base in reverses how a plus lens behaves.
  18. The correct answer is B (halving puts each optical centre 2.5 mm from the true position): a 63 mm total split evenly gives 31.5 and 31.5, which is 2.5 mm inside the right value and 2.5 mm outside the left. The binocular total is preserved exactly, so nothing about the total is the problem. Nose pads move the frame on the face but cannot shift a ground optical centre back into place. Blank selection is a separate lab decision and has no bearing on where the centres end up.
  19. The correct answer is C (1.00 prism dioptres in each lens of the pair): each centre lands 2.5 mm from its true position, which is 0.25 cm, so 0.25 x 4.00 = 1.00 prism dioptres per lens. Using half of the 2.5 mm error gives 0.50 and understates the problem. Using the full 5 mm spread between the two eyes gives 2.00, but no single lens is 5 mm out of place. Leaving the decentration in millimetres produces 10.00, which is ten times too large.
  20. The correct answer is A (both eyes converge, so the axes cross closer together at the spectacle plane): fixating a near target rotates each eye inward, and the separation of the visual axes measured at the plane of the lenses shrinks by the amount of that rotation. Pupil constriction is part of the near reflex, but the pupil narrows about its own centre and does not move the corneal reflex nasally. A frame sliding down alters heights, not horizontal separation. Corneal curvature does not change with accommodation, so the reflex position is not shifted that way.
  21. The correct answer is D (about 64 mm, roughly 4 mm below the distance value): a typical adult reading at 40 cm loses about 3 to 4 mm of separation, and a wide distance value of 68 mm sits at the upper end of that range. A drop of only 1.5 mm suggests the pupilometer was left near its infinity setting or the operator did not move to the near position. A drop of 7 mm is far larger than convergence at 40 cm can produce. An unchanged value is a clear sign that nothing was altered between the two readings.
  22. The correct answer is C (a patient with a wide 72 mm distance value working at 30 cm): a wide separation and a short working distance both increase the angle each eye must turn, so the true loss runs close to 6 mm and a flat 3 mm subtraction leaves the near centres about 3 mm too far apart. The narrow 56 mm value at 45 cm and the 64 mm value at 50 cm each lose a little over 3 mm, so the habit lands very close to the truth for both of them. The 63 mm value at 40 cm loses about 4 mm, so there the habit runs roughly a millimetre short. The fix in every case is to measure the near value at the distance actually used.
  23. The correct answer is B (1.00 prism dioptres base out over the two lenses): the centres are 4 mm too wide in total, so each eye is 2 mm or 0.2 cm out, and 0.2 x 2.50 = 0.50 prism dioptres per lens for a pair total of 1.00. Plus lenses throw their base toward the optical centre, and the centres sit temporally, so the effect is base out and works against convergence at near. Quoting 0.50 counts one lens only. Applying the full 4 mm to each eye gives 2.00, and base in reverses the direction for a plus lens.
  24. The correct answer is A (right 32 and left 31 at distance, right 30 and left 29 at near): monocular values are written right eye first, then left, separated by a slash, and the distance and near pairs are labelled separately. Reversing the order would misplace both centres on an asymmetrical face by twice the asymmetry. A single slashed pair is never a binocular total, since a binocular 63 would be written as one number. Fitting heights are recorded separately and are vertical measurements, not horizontal ones.
  25. The correct answer is C (four millimetres in each lens, twice the asymmetry from the mean): the mean of 34 and 30 is 32, so each eye sits 2 mm from that mean, and swapping the values moves each centre across the mean by 4 mm. Quoting 2 mm reports the asymmetry itself rather than the shift caused by reversing it. The error falls on both lenses, not on one, so loading all 8 mm onto the right side is wrong. The binocular total does stay at 64 mm, which is exactly why this error survives a total only check.
  26. The correct answer is D (each lens needs 8 mm of decentration, so blanks run larger): the frame centre separation is 56 plus 20, or 76 mm, giving a geometric half of 38 mm against a 30 mm monocular value, which leaves 8 mm to move per lens. That much decentration pushes up the minimum blank size, and on a minus lens it would also thicken the temporal edge. Segment height is a vertical decision set by the fitting, not by this horizontal mismatch. Base curve is chosen for the power and the frame, and no tint alters the edge thickness.
  27. The correct answer is A (5 mm of nasal decentration for the right lens): the geometric centre distance is the A measurement plus the bridge divided by two, so 52 plus 18 is 70 and half of that is 35 mm, leaving 35 minus 30, or 5 mm per lens. Doubling to 10 mm applies the total decentration for the pair to a single lens. Halving to 2.5 mm splits a figure that was already per lens. Subtracting the monocular value from the A measurement alone gives 22 mm and ignores the bridge entirely.
  28. The correct answer is D (68 mm, which covers the diameter, both decentrations and edging): the blank must reach past the effective diameter by the decentration on each side, so 56 plus two lots of 5 mm gives 66 mm, and the 2 mm edging allowance brings it to 68 mm. Adding the decentration only once gives 63 mm and leaves the far side short. Stopping at 66 mm ignores the allowance the lab asked for. Doubling the 10 mm pair figure and then adding the margin produces 78 mm, which orders far more glass than the job needs.
  29. The correct answer is B (the progressive is held to 1.0 mm against the monocular value): mounted progressives are checked against the specified monocular figures with only 1.0 mm of horizontal freedom, while mounted single vision and multifocal pairs are checked against the specified distance value with 2.5 mm. Treating both as 2.5 mm would let a progressive corridor sit well outside its design position. Applying 2.5 mm to the progressive repeats that same mistake. Single vision work does carry a horizontal allowance, and candidates must read the actual chart supplied on exam day rather than work from memory.
  30. The correct answer is C (return, because the right point is 1.5 mm from its specified position): a mounted progressive allows 1.0 mm of horizontal freedom from each specified monocular value, and 33.5 against a specified 32 is 1.5 mm out. The left lens at 31 sits exactly on its figure, so the fault is one sided. A binocular total that happens to look reasonable does not rescue a lens that is individually out, since the standard is applied to each eye. Vertical location is graded separately and does not replace the horizontal check.
  31. The correct answer is C (about 0.4 mm of horizontal displacement from the intended position): rearranging Prentice's rule gives decentration equal to prism divided by power, so 0.33 divided by 8.00 is 0.04125 cm, which is roughly 0.4 mm. That is a demanding figure and shows why high powers leave almost no centring room. Multiplying instead of dividing gives 2.6 mm and points the arithmetic the wrong way. Reading the centimetre answer as millimetres gives 0.04 mm, and shifting the decimal the other way gives 4.1 mm.
  32. The correct answer is A (prescribed prism is measured from a centre that must be placed exactly): the prescribed amount is only correct when the prism reference point sits where it was specified, so any centring error adds unwanted prism on top of the prescribed amount. Grinding the prism in does not free the lab from placing that reference point, it makes the placement more critical. Binocular values are not made unreliable by the presence of prism, they are simply too coarse to place each point. Splitting prism between the two lenses is a separate decision that monocular values do not control.
  33. The correct answer is B (0.80 prism dioptres base out across the two eyes): a 2 mm total error is 1 mm per eye, or 0.1 cm, so the right lens gives 0.1 x 2.00 = 0.20 and the left gives 0.1 x 6.00 = 0.60, and the two add to 0.80. Both centres sit too far nasally on minus lenses, so both bases point outward and the effects add rather than cancel. Taking the difference between the two lenses gives 0.40, which would only apply to a vertical imbalance calculation. Applying the whole 2 mm to each eye doubles the answer to 1.60.
  34. The correct answer is D (adult values sit roughly between 54 and 74 mm, clustering near 63): 47 mm falls well below the adult range and is far more likely to be a misread rule or a reading taken from the wrong landmark than a true value. Refractive error does not set the separation of the eyes, so linking narrow values to myopia is wrong. Values are recorded to the nearest half or whole millimetre and odd numbers are perfectly normal. Monocular recording is good practice on many jobs but would not by itself flag an implausible total.
  35. The correct answer is A (31 mm, because the two monocular values must total 65 mm): the monocular figures are measured from the same midline, so they always sum to the binocular reading, and 65 minus 34 leaves 31. That sum check is the fastest way to catch a transposed or misread scale. Splitting the total evenly assumes a symmetrical face and would contradict the 34 mm already read. Faces are rarely mirror images, so matching the two sides is not a rule, and no side of the face is systematically wider than the other.
  36. The correct answer is C (a smaller eyesize or a narrower bridge, or both together): decentration is the frame centre separation halved minus the monocular value, so 54 plus 18 gives 72, a half of 36 mm, and 7 mm of decentration per lens against a 29 mm eye. Cutting either the eyesize or the bridge lowers that 36 mm figure and closes the gap. A deeper B measurement changes the vertical dimension and if anything raises the blank size. Temple length and base curve have no bearing on horizontal centre placement.
  37. The correct answer is D (1.80 prism dioptres base in across the finished pair): the 3 mm error is 1.5 mm per eye, or 0.15 cm, so each lens gives 0.15 x 6.00 = 0.90 prism dioptres and the pair totals 1.80. A plus lens throws its base toward the optical centre, and the centres sit too far nasally, so the induced prism is base in. Reporting 0.90 stops after one lens. Applying the whole 3 mm to each eye gives 3.60, and base out reverses the direction for a plus lens.
  38. The correct answer is B (record the values as measured and note the asymmetry on the order): the frame will sit on the displaced bridge, so the odd looking split is exactly where the optical centres need to be, and a note tells the lab the figures were checked rather than mistyped. Averaging would move each centre 3.5 mm from the eye it serves. Handing over a binocular total invites the lab to split it evenly and produce that same error. The bridge midpoint is the correct reference precisely because the frame rests there.
  39. The correct answer is B (1.00 prism dioptres of unwanted prism from that lens): Prentice's rule uses centimetres, so 1 mm becomes 0.1 cm and 0.1 x 10.00 = 1.00 prism dioptres. A single millimetre of error on a ten dioptre lens is therefore already a full prism dioptre, which is why high powers demand monocular measurement. Multiplying the millimetre figure directly by the power gives 10.00 and is ten times too large. Dividing by 100 instead of by 10 gives 0.10, and dividing twice gives 0.01.
  40. The correct answer is A (convergence can only bring the visual axes closer, never further apart): fixating a near target rotates both eyes inward, so the separation measured at the spectacle plane must fall below the distance figure, and a larger near value is a recording or instrument error every time. Rounding conventions cannot create a reversal of that size. The problem is not the size of the gap but its direction, so quoting a tolerance misses the point. Near values are measured at the working distance rather than derived from the distance figure.
  41. The correct answer is D (1.20 prism dioptres each, both bases the same way, no vergence demand): each centre lands 4 mm from its true position, which is 0.4 cm, so 0.4 x 3.00 = 1.20 prism dioptres per lens. The right centre sits too far nasally and gives base out, the left sits too far temporally and gives base in, and on minus lenses those two bases point the same way in space, so the pair acts as yoked prism that displaces the image rather than forcing fusional vergence. Adding the two as though they opposed one another gives 2.40 and misreads the geometry. Halving the error to 2 mm gives 0.60, and the effect does not cancel to nothing since the image still shifts.
  42. The correct answer is C (against the specified near value, with 2.5 mm of freedom): segment horizontal location is judged from the near figure written on the order, because that is where the segments were meant to sit, and the allowance is the wider 2.5 mm one. Comparing segments to the distance value would ignore the convergence the near figure already accounts for. The frame centre separation is a frame dimension and never appears as the specified target. The tighter 1.0 mm horizontal allowance belongs to mounted progressives, not to segment placement.
  43. The correct answer is A (3 mm of nasal decentration for the left lens): the eyesize plus the bridge is 70 mm, half of that geometric separation is 35 mm, and 35 minus the 32 mm monocular value leaves 3 mm to move in. Doubling to 6 mm applies the pair total to one lens. Halving to 1.5 mm splits a figure that was already per lens. Subtracting the monocular value from the eyesize alone gives 22 mm and leaves the bridge out of the calculation.
  44. The correct answer is B (heavy temporal edge thickness from 10 mm of decentration per lens): the frame centre separation is 58 plus 20, or 78 mm, giving a geometric half of 39 mm against a 29 mm monocular value, so each centre must move 10 mm nasally and the minus lens carries far more material on the temporal side. Cosmetics and weight both suffer, and the patient should hear that before the order is placed. Segment height is a vertical matter and this is a single vision job. Base curve follows the power and the frame, and coatings apply equally at any power.
  45. The correct answer is C (as right 31 and left 30 at near, a normal 4 mm reduction): monocular values are always written right first then left, at near as well as at distance, and 65 dropping to 61 is exactly the 3 to 4 mm of convergence expected at a normal reading distance. The two lines should not match, since convergence must narrow the near figure. A slashed pair is never a binocular total paired with a fitting height. The right eye value goes first at near just as it does at distance.
  46. The correct answer is D (1.35 prism dioptres base out through that single lens): 3 mm is 0.3 cm, so 0.3 x 4.50 = 1.35 prism dioptres, and a plus lens throws its base toward the optical centre, which here sits temporally, so the base is out. Calling it base in reverses how a plus lens behaves and would be right only for a minus lens in this position. Halving the displacement to 1.5 mm gives 0.68 and treats a single lens figure as though it were a pair total. Leaving the displacement in millimetres gives 13.50 and is ten times too large.
  47. The correct answer is C (the rule assumes an adult separation and a 40 cm working distance): the usual 3 to 4 mm reduction is a rough adult figure for reading at about 40 cm, and a 25 cm working distance demands far more convergence while a child's smaller separation and habits change the result again. Measuring at the distance actually used is the only reliable route. A near value is never larger than the distance value, because convergence cannot move the eyes apart. Convergence keeps increasing as the target comes closer, and children do converge, often more strongly at their shorter working distances.
  48. The correct answer is A (within tolerance, since the 2 mm error sits inside the 2.5 mm allowance): mounted single vision and multifocal pairs are checked against the specified distance value with 2.5 mm of horizontal freedom, and 66 against 64 is 2 mm out. The tighter 1.0 mm figure applies to mounted progressives and would fail this pair wrongly. Nothing in the standard forbids a finished value from sitting above the ordered one, since the allowance runs in both directions. Horizontal placement is graded on single vision work, just with a wider allowance, and the chart supplied on exam day governs the final call.
  49. The correct answer is D (64 mm, adding twice the decentration plus the edging margin): the geometric centre separation is 50 plus 20 halved, or 35 mm, so each lens moves 35 minus 31, which is 4 mm. The blank must clear the 54 mm effective diameter by that 4 mm on each side, giving 62 mm, and the 2 mm edging margin brings the order to 64 mm. Counting the decentration once yields 60 mm and leaves one side short. Stopping at 62 mm drops the margin, and doubling the 8 mm pair figure gives 70 mm and wastes glass.
  50. The correct answer is B (job two, because a progressive corridor must be placed for each eye): a progressive has a narrow corridor and a defined near zone, and the standard holds its reference points to 1.0 mm of the specified monocular values, so a split binocular figure cannot be relied on even at a modest power. Frame size affects decentration and blank size but does not by itself force monocular measurement. Low powered readers tolerate small centring errors because the induced prism stays tiny. A plano lens produces no prism at all when decentred, so any centring error there is purely cosmetic.
  51. The correct answer is B (the section requires the penlight and rule result taken and recorded first): NACOR sets the order so the candidate demonstrates the manual skill before any instrument value is in mind, and writing the penlight numbers down first stops the two sets blending in memory. A corneal reflection pupillometer does read monocular values against its own midline, so an inability to produce them is not the issue. These instruments need no warm-up period and give stable readings from the first use of the day. Seating a nose rest on the bridge does nothing to invalidate a rule measurement taken before or after it.
  52. The correct answer is D (the oblique sighting angle shifts the apparent reflex positions and skews the reading): the rule sits several millimetres in front of the cornea, so sighting from above views the rule and the reflexes at an angle and moves the apparent alignment. Sitting square and level with the patient is the fix, and candidates lose marks on eye level more often than on any other single element. The reflexes stay perfectly visible from above, so they do not fade or vanish. Height alone does not force convergence, and a monocular split can still be attempted, just as inaccurately as the binocular value.
  53. The correct answer is A (the reflex no longer marks the axis aimed at the sighting eye): the corneal reflex reports where the eye is pointing relative to the light source, so the penlight must sit immediately below the sighting eye for that reflex to represent the visual axis aimed at the observer. Held off to one side at chest height, the reflex reports the direction of the light rather than the direction of the sighting eye. Brightness is not the issue, since a penlight held low still gives a crisp reflex. A patient can fixate a light below the chin, and a light held to one side does not produce symmetrical convergence.
  54. The correct answer is B (the left eye, because it lies opposite the patient's right eye): examiner and patient face each other, so the examiner's left eye sits directly across from the patient's right eye, and only that eye gives a line of sight square to the rule at the point being marked. The eyes are then swapped before reading the left reflex, and that swap is what cancels the parallax. Sighting the patient's right eye with your own right eye views the mark obliquely, which is the very error the swap exists to remove. Keeping both eyes open leaves the operator unsure which line of sight produced the reading, and choosing by ocular dominance ignores the geometry altogether.
  55. The correct answer is C (the far reflex is viewed obliquely, so the recorded value is skewed): the rule lies several millimetres in front of the corneal plane, so a sighting eye held on one side views the far end of the rule at an angle and shifts the apparent alignment by a millimetre or more. Swapping to the right eye for the left reflex puts the line of sight square to the rule again and cancels that error. The rule staying still does not help, because the error comes from the observer's viewing angle rather than from rule movement. Fixation duration does not create convergence, and the binocular total is affected just as much as any split taken from it.
  56. The correct answer is A (the reflex position does not shift when the pupil size changes): the first Purkinje image is fixed by the geometry of the light source, the cornea, and the observer, so it stays put while the pupil constricts in bright light and dilates in a dim room. Pupils also do not always change size symmetrically about their own geometric centre, which is why a pupil centre estimate wanders between rooms. The reflex is a surface reflection on the cornea, not something sitting deeper in the eye near the fovea. It is also considerably smaller than the pupil, and it does not track pupil movement by any fixed amount.
  57. The correct answer is D (the limbus is a soft boundary and yields no reliable monocular split): the junction between cornea and sclera is a gradual transition rather than a point, so the edge chosen can vary by a millimetre, and nothing in the technique identifies where the bridge centre falls. Temporal to nasal reading cancels the corneal diameter rather than doubling it, which is precisely why the binocular estimate works at all. Nothing in the method fixes the working distance at 40 cm. Habitual spectacles are removed for any PD measurement, so that is not a limitation unique to this technique.
  58. The correct answer is B (at 40 cm with the penlight on the candidate's own midline): the near measurement must be taken at the patient's true working distance, and the target must sit on the midline so both eyes converge symmetrically on a single point that the candidate occupies. Holding the light beside the sighting eye is the distance technique and gives an asymmetrical convergence point. Lowering the light to the chin at arm's length changes neither the convergence demand nor the working distance to the one the patient actually uses. Measuring at 20 cm with the light beside the cheek forces far more convergence than the reading task requires and reads too narrow.
  59. The correct answer is A (the single midline target means switching eyes now adds an error): at distance the light sits beside the sighting eye and the swap cancels two equal and opposite oblique views, but at near the target is one point on the midline, so the head must stay still and centred for both readings. Swapping the sighting eye there introduces a lateral shift instead of cancelling one. Reflex brightness is unchanged by which of the observer's eyes is open. The patient's convergence is set by the position of the light, not by the observer's eyelids, and the rule still sits in front of the cornea, so parallax remains possible.
  60. The correct answer is D (the working distance dial was left at infinity for the near reading): convergence inside a pupilometer is created only by moving the internal target, so a dial left at infinity simply produces a second distance reading. A near value equal to the distance value is the classic sign, since a typical adult at 40 cm loses roughly 3 to 4 mm. Forgetting to change this dial is the single most common pupilometer error at the exam. The vertex control shifts the plane of reference by a fraction of a millimetre, not by the whole convergence allowance. Loss of fixation or hairlines set on a pupil edge would upset the monocular sums, which here add correctly.
  61. The correct answer is C (reflections of the light source from the front surface of each cornea): a corneal reflection pupillometer produces its own first Purkinje image on each eye and the hairlines are set on those, which is why the landmark is independent of pupil size. The internal target is what the patient fixates, and it is not what appears as the two bright dots to the operator. Magnified entrance pupils would be broad discs rather than small bright points, and they would move with light level. Hairlines are physical reticle lines inside the eyepiece and form no image on the patient's retina.
  62. The correct answer is B (it refers the reading to the intended spectacle plane rather than the cornea): some models let the operator enter the vertex distance to be fitted, commonly 12 to 14 mm, so the values report the separation of the visual axes where the lenses will actually sit. The plane of measurement matters most in strong prescriptions, which is why the control exists at all. Pupil diameter is never dialled in, because the instrument reads corneal reflexes and not pupil edges. Segment height is a separate fitting measurement taken on the frame worn, and no pupilometer compensates for a patient's refractive error.
  63. The correct answer is B (disinfect the forehead rest and the nose rest before any contact): those are the two parts that touch skin, they touched the previous candidate's patient, and disinfecting them is the routine infection control step expected before the instrument goes near a face. Wiping the eyepiece with a dry cloth cleans an optical surface but does nothing about the contact points on the patient's brow and nose. Handing the instrument to the patient does not remove contamination and destroys the operator's control of alignment. Running water is never used on a pupilometer, since it damages the internal optics and electronics.
  64. The correct answer is A (one reflex shifts relative to the other and both monocular values drift): the instrument reads each eye against a midline fixed by the nose rest, so any tilt rotates that reference and moves both scale positions away from the true values. The cure is to hold the forehead rest in contact and keep the instrument level before touching a hairline. The binocular total is not somehow preserved while the monocular values fail, because the total is built from the same two hairline positions. Tilt does not change the working distance the internal target sits at, and the reflexes stay visible, which is why a tilted reading looks deceptively normal.
  65. The correct answer is D (directly bisecting the corneal reflex of the eye it belongs to): the hairline is set so the bright reflex is cut in half, because the reflex is the visible landmark closest to the visual axis and it does not move as the pupil changes size. The right and left values are then read from the two side scales and checked against the binocular total. A nasal pupil edge is a moving boundary that shifts with light level and gives no repeatable landmark. Splitting the gap between the reflex and the limbus invents a point with no optical meaning, and the geometric pupil centre is the very estimate the corneal reflection design exists to avoid.
  66. The correct answer is C (reseat the instrument and repeat, because the values do not sum): 33 plus 31 gives 64, not the 63 shown on the binocular scale, and that mismatch means either a hairline was not truly bisecting a reflex or the instrument was not sitting square. The sum check exists precisely to catch this before anything reaches the order sheet. Recording the numbers as read passes a known internal contradiction to the lab. Splitting 63 evenly throws away the monocular information that a progressive job needs, and simply writing 64 assumes the two side scales are right when the fault could equally lie there.
  67. The correct answer is A (occlude one eye and measure each fixating eye to the bridge centre): a deviating eye is not aimed at the target, so its corneal reflex is displaced and any reading taken with both eyes open is wrong. Covering one eye, having the patient fixate the penlight with the eye being measured, and reading that reflex to the bridge midpoint records where each eye looks when it is the fixating eye, which is where the optical centre belongs. Averaging two readings that are both contaminated by the deviation produces a number matching neither eye. Doubling the straight eye assumes a symmetry the patient does not have, and a pupilometer cannot occlude an eye or detect that one was not fixating.
  68. The correct answer is C (set it to match the seeing side, or to whatever looks symmetrical): a prosthesis has no visual axis and no meaningful corneal reflex, so nothing optical governs that side and cosmetic appearance takes over. The seeing eye is measured monocularly to the bridge centre in the normal way, and the artificial side is then set to whatever makes the finished pair look balanced. Treating a reflex off a prosthesis as a real landmark records a number with no optical meaning behind it. Leaving the box blank invites the lab to split a binocular value that was never valid, and subtracting an arbitrary 2 mm has no basis in the anatomy of a socket.
  69. The correct answer is B (remeasure now, using a bright target and a shortened working distance): a child's PD grows with the skull well into the teens, so a value from two years ago is no longer the child's PD, and a bright target with a slightly shorter working distance holds attention long enough to get the reading. A pupilometer is often quicker than a rule with a restless child. PD is not fixed once the eyes form, so copying the old value guarantees an error on a growing face. Adding a fixed amount per year invents growth that varies from child to child, and monocular values can be taken on children as readily as on adults.
  70. The correct answer is D (the value will read wider, because strong plus magnifies the reflexes): a high plus lens magnifies everything seen through it, including the corneal reflexes, so the two landmarks appear further apart than they truly are and the PD is recorded too wide. Strong minus does the reverse and makes the eyes look closer together. The correct practice is to take the measurement with the habitual spectacles removed. Plus lenses magnify rather than minify, so a narrower reading is the wrong direction, and the shift is caused by lens magnification rather than by any movement of the frame plane. The reflex sitting on the cornea does not protect the reading, because the lens alters how that reflex is seen.
  71. The correct answer is D (record 35 and 29 as measured and note the facial asymmetry): if the nasal bridge midpoint is itself displaced, an unequal monocular split is the true finding, and the frame will sit on that same displaced bridge, so those are the values that put each optical centre in front of each visual axis. The readings sum correctly to 64 and repeat, which is the evidence that the measurement is sound. Forcing 32 and 32 or easing toward 33 and 31 moves both optical centres away from the eyes for the sake of a comfortable looking number. Leaving the monocular boxes empty hands the lab a binocular value it will split evenly, producing exactly the error the measurement was taken to prevent.
  72. The correct answer is A (repeat both measurements and look for the specific fault in each): a 3 mm disagreement is well outside the roughly 1 mm agreement expected, so one of the two procedures has a fault that needs finding rather than smoothing over. Check the eye level and sighting eye swap on the penlight side, and check that the instrument sat square and was set to infinity on the pupilometer side. Averaging blends a correct value with a wrong one and produces a number that is right for nobody. Picking either instrument on principle, whether by tradition or by preference, records a value that has not been shown to be reliable in this particular measurement.
  73. The correct answer is C (the pupilometer value, because it removes the operator's own parallax): for a cooperative patient with clear binocular fixation the instrument gives repeatable monocular values against a fixed midline and takes the observer's viewing angle out of the result, which is why it is the value trusted for a progressive. The tighter progressive horizontal tolerance in the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR makes that repeatability worth having. The rule does not sit in the frame plane at all, it sits in front of it, which is the source of the parallax in question. Averaging carries the manual error forward at half strength, and deliberately choosing the narrower number is guesswork rather than measurement.
  74. The correct answer is B (the occluded penlight value, because the instrument cannot occlude an eye): a pupilometer needs both eyes fixating the internal target at once and gives no warning when one of them is not, so with unsteady or amblyopic fixation it reports a confident number built on a bad assumption. The occluded penlight technique measures each eye while that eye is the fixating eye, which is exactly what the optical centre position depends on. A fixed instrument midline does not help if the eye being referenced to it was not looking at the target. The internal target does not control an eye that is not fixating, and amblyopia is no bar to a progressive lens.
  75. The correct answer is A (4 mm on each side, twice the 2 mm asymmetry that was present): the right optical centre is placed at 30 mm when it should be at 34 mm, and the left is placed at 34 mm when it should be at 30 mm, so each is out by 4 mm. That is twice the 2 mm each eye sits from the 32 mm average, which is why reversing the sides doubles the asymmetry rather than reproducing it. A 2 mm figure would be the deviation of each eye from the mean, not the error created by swapping them. An 8 mm figure doubles the answer a second time, and the unchanged binocular total of 64 mm is exactly what hides this error from a quick glance.
  76. The correct answer is B (to the 35 cm position, the distance actually used for reading): convergence depends on the working distance, so the collar is set to the distance the patient really uses for the task being measured, and this patient reads print at 35 cm. The 65 cm screen task is a separate working distance and would be measured separately if an intermediate value were needed. Setting the collar to infinity and subtracting a fixed 3 mm is the derived value the exam does not accept, since the reduction varies with the working distance and the PD itself. Using 65 cm or a made up 50 cm midpoint records convergence the patient does not use at either task.
  77. The correct answer is D (read the bridge midpoint against the rule without moving it at all): with the zero already on the right reflex, the millimetre mark falling on the bridge midpoint gives the right monocular near value directly, and subtracting it from 59 gives the left, so both values come from a single rule placement and are guaranteed to sum to the binocular total. Halving 59 into 29.5 each assumes a symmetry the face may not have. Moving the zero to the bridge introduces a second placement and a second chance to shift the rule, which is what the stem rules out. Subtracting a fixed amount from the distance values is a derived near PD rather than a measured one.
  78. The correct answer is C (the patient converges on the light, so the value reads too narrow): a distance PD assumes the visual axes are effectively parallel, and a target at 20 cm forces far more convergence than a target at about 40 cm, so the reflexes move nasally and the recorded value comes out short of the true distance PD. Working at roughly 40 cm, or 50 cm if that is the habit, keeps the convergence small and consistent. Rule steadiness is not the limiting factor at close range, and reflex brightness is easy to control with a small penlight. The first Purkinje image is a front surface reflection and does not form behind the cornea at any working distance.
  79. The correct answer is C (the near total exceeds the distance total, which convergence cannot produce): converging the eyes rotates them inward, so the separation measured at the spectacle plane always falls, and a near value larger than the distance value is always an error. A typical adult at 40 cm shows a near PD roughly 3 to 4 mm smaller. Identical monocular values are perfectly possible on a symmetrical face and are not in themselves a fault. Totals of 62 and 64 sit comfortably inside the usual adult range of roughly 54 to 74 mm. Whole millimetres are the normal recording convention, so a missing decimal place is not the problem here.
  80. The correct answer is A (the lab cannot tell which pair is distance and which is near): monocular values must be labelled, as in Distance PD 33/30 and Near PD 31/28, because an unlabelled pair of pairs invites the lab to guess, and putting near values into the distance boxes fits a progressive several millimetres too narrow. The slash convention already fixes right before left, so the sides are readable, but nothing on the sheet identifies the working distance. A lab reading 33/30 will not mistake a slashed pair for a binocular total. Monocular pairs do sum to their binocular total, and that check is available here, and no lab automatically applies near values to distance.
  81. The correct answer is B (the section requires a measured near value, and the reduction varies): the examiner is watching for a genuine near measurement taken at the working distance, and the drop from distance to near is not a fixed number, since it depends on the working distance, the distance PD, and the vertex. A wide PD at a short working distance can lose 4 or 5 mm while a narrow PD at a longer distance loses only 2 or 3. Swapping one fixed figure for another does not fix the underlying problem, because no single subtraction fits every patient. Near PD can be taken by penlight or by pupilometer, and the derived value fails equally in the monocular boxes and the binocular one.
  82. The correct answer is D (locate the working distance dial and vertex control, then focus the eyepiece): familiarization exists so that unfamiliar controls are found before the clock starts, and those three items are the ones that cost time or cost accuracy once the section is running. Hunting for the near setting with the timer going is how candidates end up recording a second distance value. The penlight sequence needs no familiarization with the equipment, since a rule is a rule on any bench. Taking a full trial set of readings burns the setup time on work that has to be repeated anyway, and disinfecting instruments not assigned to this candidate wastes it entirely.
  83. The correct answer is A (the first Purkinje image, from the front surface of the cornea): the small bright dot used for PD work is the reflection of the penlight from the anterior corneal surface, which is the brightest of the four images and the one closest to the visual axis. It is the landmark for both the rule method and any corneal reflection pupillometer. The reflection from the posterior corneal surface is far weaker and is not what is bisected in practice. The images formed at the front and back surfaces of the crystalline lens are dimmer still, lie deeper in the eye, and are not usable landmarks at the spectacle plane.
  84. The correct answer is D (2.5 mm on each side, which fails the progressive horizontal tolerance): splitting 63 mm evenly places both centres at 31.5 mm, so the right is 2.5 mm short of its true 34 mm and the left is 2.5 mm wide of its true 29 mm. The ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR holds a mounted progressive to 1.0 mm horizontally from the specified monocular PD, so 2.5 mm is well outside it. A 1.5 mm figure understates the displacement and would still exceed the progressive limit anyway. A 5.0 mm figure is the full spread between the two true values rather than the error on each lens, and 2.5 mm would only pass under the looser single vision allowance, which does not apply to a progressive.
  85. The correct answer is B (a lateral parallax error is added to the recorded binocular value): the rule sits in front of the corneal plane, so moving the head sideways between the two readings changes the viewing angle onto the rule and shifts the alignment even though the sighting eye stayed the same. Head position must be held still and the eyes swapped deliberately, not the other way round. Keeping the same sighting eye does not protect the reading, since the error comes from where the head is rather than which eye is open. The patient's convergence is unchanged by a small observer movement, and the corneal reflexes stay exactly where the light puts them.
  86. The correct answer is C (the centre of the bridge, taken midway between the two sides): monocular PD is defined as the distance from the centre of the nasal bridge to each corneal reflex, and using the same midpoint for both sides is what makes the two values sum to the binocular total. That midpoint also corresponds to where the frame will centre itself on the face. The tip of the nose sits well forward and below the frame plane and moves with head tilt. A single pad contact point is on one side only and gives no common reference for the other eye, and the nasal notch is below the level at which the frame bridges the nose.
  87. The correct answer is D (ask the patient to look at the target and hold it): the whole reading depends on both eyes aiming at the internal target, because a drifting eye moves its corneal reflex and shifts that monocular value without any warning appearing in the eyepiece. Confirming steady fixation before setting each hairline is the correct step, and the values are read only once both reflexes are stable. Widening the nose rest changes the instrument's seating, not where the patient is looking. Changing the working distance dial alters convergence and would corrupt a distance reading, and reading through a known drift records a value the operator already has reason to doubt.
  88. The correct answer is A (the two sets blend in memory and get transposed on the sheet): the section is deliberately structured so the penlight values are written down before the pupilometer is picked up, because four numbers from one method and four from another are easy to swap once both sets are in the head at the same time. Writing as you go also lets the monocular sums be checked while the patient is still in position. Measured values do not expire and there is no five minute window. Nothing requires a signature before the instrument work begins, and time to check the sums is available at the end whether or not the numbers were written down earlier.
  89. The correct answer is C (the method gives a binocular estimate only, with no reliable monocular split): reading temporal limbus to nasal limbus cancels the corneal diameter and approximates the total separation, but nothing in the technique references the centre of the bridge, so it cannot produce the two monocular values a progressive order needs. It is a cross-check on a binocular figure, never a primary technique. The method is not tied to a 20 cm working distance, and habitual spectacles are removed for any PD measurement. A corneal scar that obscures a reflex does not necessarily disturb the limbus boundary, which is why a candidate might reach for this method in the first place.
  90. The correct answer is B (distance exceeds near, monocular sums match, values plausible, right side first): those four checks take about fifteen seconds and catch the errors that actually cost marks, namely a dial left on distance, a hairline off a reflex, a transposed pair, and a value outside the usual adult range of roughly 54 to 74 mm. Pupil size and room conditions are not recorded values and cannot be checked on the sheet at all. Frame eyesize, bridge, and temple length belong to frame selection rather than to the PD sheet. Lens power, add, base curve, and segment height are verification and multifocal measurements handled in other parts of the examination.
  91. The correct answer is C (from the back surface of the lens to the front of the cornea): every power on a Canadian prescription is a back vertex power, so the concave back surface of the lens is the correct starting point and the corneal apex is the correct end point. Starting at the front surface of the lens reads too large by the whole centre thickness, which is several millimetres on a high plus lens. The pupil centre sits behind the cornea and is not the surface the definition uses. The eyewire groove is a frame landmark rather than a lens surface, and it sits away from the visual axis.
  92. The correct answer is A (it gains effective plus power, so the patient is now over-plussed): as any lens moves away from the eye its effect at the eye shifts in the plus direction, and for a plus lens that shows up as extra effective plus. To deliver the same correction from the farther position the laboratory would have to be sent less plus than the prescribed figure, not more. Back vertex power is a property of the lens itself and does not change when the frame slides, but the power acting at the eye does change. A plus lens moving away cannot lose plus or gain minus, and that reversed direction is the single costliest error in this topic.
  93. The correct answer is D (the lens loses effective minus power, so more minus should be ordered): moving any lens away from the eye shifts its effect at the eye in the plus direction, which for a minus lens reads as a loss of effective minus and the under-corrected, softer distance image the patient is describing. Compensating for the farther seated position therefore calls for a stronger minus figure on the work order. Claiming the lens gains minus, or that extra plus is needed, reverses the direction and would leave the patient blurrier still. Effectivity acts on minus powers exactly as it acts on plus ones, so no power is exempt from it.
  94. The correct answer is B (order +8.25 D and note the vertex distance beside the compensated power): the lens moves 4 mm closer, so d is +0.004 m, d multiplied by F is +0.032, and 1 minus 0.032 leaves 0.968. Dividing 8.00 by 0.968 gives 8.2645 D, which rounds to +8.25 D at the quarter dioptre step the laboratory works in. Ordering +7.75 D comes from running the effectivity direction backwards, since a plus lens moved closer loses effective plus and needs more plus, not less. The +11.75 D figure comes from using 0.04 in place of 0.004, the decimal slip that follows from mishandling the millimetre to metre conversion, and leaving the power at +8.00 D ignores a change of a full quarter dioptre.

The prescribed power was correct for the phoropter position it was taken at, and a real reduction in distance acuity is not a matter of opinion.

  97. Above that figure the change starts to reach a full step, and beyond about 8.00 D it becomes clinically obvious to the patient. Setting the trigger at 2.00 D would generate calculations that never change anything on the order. Waiting until 8.00 D leaves a whole band of powers where a real and orderable change is being missed, and 6.50 D belongs to the ANSI Z80.1 sphere tolerance bands used by NACOR rather than to vertex compensation.

Accommodation and fixation do not shift the corneal apex enough to matter over a 12 mm gap. The scale reads the gap in front of the eye and never reads the centre thickness of the lens.

The reading is recorded as it stands, per eye, with the eye clearly labelled.

The +17.25 D figure comes from putting the new vertex of 0.009 m into the formula instead of the 4 mm change, and leaving the power at +15.00 D wastes the calculation entirely.
  109. The correct answer is D (yes, because tilt pulls the lower edge in and pushes the top out): rotating the front forward moves different parts of the lens in opposite directions, so the distance from the back surface to the cornea measured along the visual axis is not what it was before the tilt was added. That makes any vertex taken before the tilt was set unusable, and the reading has to be repeated. Tilt is not a neutral rotation that leaves the visual axis point untouched, and it does not move the whole lens uniformly closer either. The bridge and pads set how deep the frame rides, but tilt changes the vertex independently of them, which is why tilt is set first and vertex measured afterwards.

The -14.25 D figure reverses the sign of d and takes minus off when minus should be going on.   111. The correct answer is A (5 mm below pupil centre, one millimetre for every two degrees): the trade rule of thumb drops the optical centre about 1 mm for each 2 degrees of pantoscopic tilt, so 10 degrees calls for a drop of about 5 mm and the eye still looks through the optical centre rather than a tilted off part of the lens. Matching the drop in millimetres to the degrees of tilt gives twice the drop the rule calls for and would put the centre well below the useful part of the lens. Dividing by four halves the correction the rule actually calls for. Doubling the degrees is further wrong again, and none of these alternatives reflects the ratio used at the bench.

  113. The correct answer is B (vertex OD 13 mm, OS 14 mm, right eye recorded before left): Canadian practice records the right eye first and labels each eye, using OD and OS or R and L consistently with whatever the order form uses. Vertex is recorded per eye because faces are not symmetrical and a real 1 mm difference between the two sides is common and worth carrying forward. Listing bare numbers without eye labels, or listing the left eye first, invites the laboratory to apply them to the wrong side. Averaging the two eyes into one binocular figure throws away the difference the measurement exists to capture, and taking whichever reading is larger is simply discarding data.

The cylinder is then -10.50 less -7.75, which is -2.75 D, and the cylinder shrinking is the whole reason for working meridian by meridian. Prescription powers are back vertex powers, so the back surface is the only correct starting point for the measurement. A front surface reading cannot come out too small, because the front surface is farther from the eye than the back one. The base curve is a separate ordering decision that has no bearing on effectivity, and a compensated lens is not out of tolerance, because the compensated figure is what the order specifies and what verification is judged against.

  124. The correct answer is A (the frame reference method, useful only as a check on the others): reading a rule against the eyewire and bevel position measures a frame landmark rather than the back surface of the lens on the visual axis, so it carries the largest built in error of the three and is best kept as a rough sanity check. The distometer is the purpose built instrument and is the most accurate of the three, not the least. The side ruler method is genuinely hard to do well but it is still a direct reading of the actual gap, and its usefulness does not stop at 4.00 D, since that is the power where compensation starts to matter. Using the least accurate method as the primary reading on any patient, including a child, defeats the point of measuring at all.

  130. Quoting 4 mm reports the first frame's drop rather than the difference the question asks for, and quoting 6 mm reports the second frame's drop for the same reason.   131. The correct answer is C (the lower lid margin, confirmed afterwards against the patient's usual habits): a flat-top segment height runs from the deepest part of the eyewire groove up to the top of the segment, and the lower lid margin is the usual starting reference for where that top edge belongs. That starting figure is then adjusted for the patient's height, posture, and occupation before it goes on the order. The centre of the pupil is the reference for a progressive fitting cross, and borrowing it for a bifocal drives the segment line straight across the pupil. Neither the lower limbus nor the upper lid margin is a recognized reference for segment height.
  132. The correct answer is A (the centre of the pupil in primary gaze at distance): fitting cross height is measured from the deepest part of the groove up to pupil centre, and the manufacturer builds the corridor and near zone around that point. Using the lower lid margin sets the cross about 5 mm too low on a typical adult and pushes the near zone off the usable part of the lens, which is the classic Part C marked error. The geometric centre of the lens shape is a frame reference, not an anatomical one, and it ignores where the eye actually sits in the shape. The upper lid margin has no role in any height measurement.

Anything shallower puts the fitting cross below the design minimum and the lens cannot be cut. Choosing 18 mm confuses the fitting height with the full depth of the lens shape and forgets that half the shape lies above the cross. The 27 mm and 24 mm figures come from arbitrary margins rather than from the geometry of the frame.

Estimating the droop and subtracting it afterwards guesses at a quantity that can be measured properly in less time.   141. The correct answer is B (parallax places the mark too high, so heights read too large): marking from above the patient's eye level means the line from the marker's eye through the pupil strikes the demo lens above the true point, so the dot lands high and the measured distance from the groove is too great. The error repeats identically on both eyes, which is why it hides itself and passes an internal consistency check. Marking from below the eye produces the opposite error, a low mark and heights that read too small. Parallax in this task is a vertical error, so it does not merely shift the mark sideways, and it certainly does not cancel between the two eyes.
  142. The correct answer is C (the deepest part of the groove cut into the lens edge): a nylon mount has no lower eyewire, so the reference is the bottom of the groove cut around the lens edge at its deepest point on the vertical meridian, which is the same principle as the deepest part of the eyewire groove in a full metal frame. Measuring to the nylon cord reads a fraction of a millimetre short because the cord sits inside the groove rather than at its base. The upper eyewire is nowhere near the lower edge of the lens and gives a figure with no relation to the segment position. The bevel apex on the nasal edge is neither the deepest point nor on the vertical meridian.
  143. The correct answer is D (supporting the lens from behind with a finger while marking lightly): a nylon mount lens is held only by cord tension along the lower edge, so counter pressure from behind stops the lens flexing out of the groove while the dot goes on. Refitting a lens that has popped free costs minutes a candidate does not have in a 20 minute section. Removing the lens to mark it on the bench destroys the relationship between the mark and the eye, which is the whole point of the measurement. Marking the eyewire adds a transfer step and its own error, and loosening the cord makes the lens even more likely to come free.
  144. The correct answer is A (faces are rarely symmetrical, so each eye is measured and labelled): heights are recorded separately for the right and left eye because pupil positions, lid levels, and frame seating all differ slightly between the two sides. Canadian practice labels them OD and OS, or R and L, and a bare pair of numbers with no eye label is incomplete regardless of how similar they look. The frame type has no bearing on whether both eyes are recorded, since a metal frame needs two heights just as a nylon mount does. The left lens is 1.5 mm above the ordered 21.0 mm, and the difference between the lenses is also 1.5 mm, so the pair fails on both counts.   150. The correct answer is C (the pupil centre sits roughly 4 mm to 6 mm higher): on a typical adult face the centre of the pupil lies about 4 mm to 6 mm above the lower lid margin, which is exactly why a progressive measured to the lid ends up around 5 mm too low. Knowing the usual gap lets a candidate cross check a fitting cross height against a segment height taken on the same eye in the same frame. A gap of only 1 mm to 2 mm would make the two references nearly interchangeable, which they are not. A gap of 10 mm to 12 mm is far larger than normal anatomy allows, and the pupil centre is never below the lower lid in primary gaze.
  151.
