A gap of only 1 mm means the progressive mark landed close to the lid rather than at the pupil, which is the commonest Part C error. It is not a case of the two numbers agreeing, because the expected separation is several millimetres, not one. The segment height itself reads sensibly against the lid, and a frame seated too high would shift both heights together rather than only one.
  152. The correct answer is D (have the patient fixate the candidate's left eye, closing the other): to mark the right lens the candidate's own left eye must be aligned with the patient's right eye, so the patient fixates that eye while the candidate closes the unused eye to remove any second viewing line. This puts the marker's line of sight along the patient's line of sight and eliminates parallax at the moment of marking. Having the patient fixate the candidate's right eye aligns the wrong pair of eyes and shifts the mark horizontally. A distant target behind the head breaks the alignment altogether, and looking down at the chin changes the vertical gaze position and lowers the mark.
  153. The correct answer is B (have the patient look away and back, then recheck the alignment): verification means confirming the dot still sits in front of the pupil centre after the eye has moved and returned to primary gaze, which catches a mark placed during a momentary shift in fixation. It costs seconds and is the accepted final step before a rule touches the frame. Asking the patient how the dot looks to them puts the judgement in the wrong place, since the patient cannot see their own pupil position. Comparing the dot to the geometric centre of the lens shape tests the frame rather than the eye, and removing the demo lens destroys the alignment being verified.
  154. The correct answer is C (the fitting cross sits at pupil centre and the design compensates already): the rule of dropping the optical centre about 1 mm for every 2 degrees of pantoscopic tilt applies to single vision optical centres, not to a progressive fitting cross. Progressive designs are built with the tilt allowance already in the surface, so the fitting cross belongs at pupil centre and moving it down 5 mm would drop the corridor and near zone by that amount. Doubling the drop makes the same error twice as badly. The rule does not have a 15 degree threshold, and tilt moves the required centre downward rather than upward, so raising the cross is wrong in both size and direction.
  155. The correct answer is A (44 mm, because the low pupil costs 2 mm of usable height): with the pupil centre sitting 2 mm below the vertical midpoint, the fitting cross height equals half the B measurement minus 2 mm. Setting half of B minus 2 equal to the 20 mm minimum gives half of B as 22 mm, so B must be at least 44 mm. A 40 mm frame would give only 18 mm and falls short, because it ignores the 2 mm the low pupil position costs. A 36 mm frame gives just 16 mm, and a published minimum is a hard limit rather than a figure with a hidden safety margin. The 22 mm answer confuses half the B measurement with the B measurement itself.
  156. The correct answer is B (the vertical depth of the lens shape at its deepest point): the B measurement is the vertical depth of the lens shape, and it is the value that decides whether the fitting cross height can reach a design's published minimum. A shallow B leaves too little lens below the fitting cross for the corridor and near zone, so checking it early saves a remake. The horizontal width of the shape is the A measurement, which governs decentration and blank size rather than fitting height. The gap between the two shapes at the bridge is the distance between lenses, and the corner to corner diagonal is the effective diameter used for blank size.
  157. The correct answer is D (fitting cross down to the lower edge of the finished lens): minimum fitting height is the smallest vertical distance from the fitting cross down to the bottom edge of the cut lens that still leaves a usable corridor and near zone. Since the fitting cross sits at pupil centre and the bottom edge sits at the deepest part of the groove, the measured fitting cross height must equal or exceed that published figure. Measuring upward to the top edge describes the distance portion above the cross, which no manufacturer specifies as a minimum. The distance optical centre to near zone gap and the lid to groove distance are different quantities entirely and neither is what the manufacturer publishes.
  158. The correct answer is A (offer a short corridor design whose published minimum is 15 mm or less): when the available fitting cross height is 15 mm and the chosen design needs 18 mm, the honest options are a deeper frame, a design with a shorter minimum, or a different lens type. Modern short corridor designs commonly work at 14 mm to 18 mm, so one of them may fit this frame properly. A published minimum is set by the surface geometry and no lab can shorten the corridor on request. Pushing the frame up the nose changes vertex and tilt and gives a fit the patient will not wear, and writing a false height on the order simply guarantees a remake and a patient who cannot read.
  159. The correct answer is C (the height reads too small, because the rim sits above the groove): the front rim of a metal eyewire sits above the base of the groove that actually holds the lens bevel, so starting the rule at the rim shortens the measured distance up to the reference point. The recorded height comes out smaller than the true value and the segment or fitting cross is then cut lower than the patient needs. Reading too large would require a starting point below the groove base, which is outside the frame entirely. The rim and the groove base are at clearly different levels, so the error is real rather than nil, and lens thickness plays no part in a height measured on the frame.
  160. The correct answer is D (adjust and seat, mark at pupil centre, verify the mark, then measure): the frame must be adjusted and correctly seated first, because every height describes the lens position relative to the eye and a frame that moves later invalidates the number. With the frame settled, the dot goes on at pupil centre, gets verified after the patient looks away and back, and only then does the rule read from the deepest part of the groove. Marking before adjusting captures a frame position the patient will never wear. Measuring before the mark exists gives nothing to measure to, and measuring before verifying risks recording a distance to a dot that was never in the right place.
  161. The correct answer is B (only 6 mm of distance zone remains, a very small distance field): with a B measurement of 24 mm and a segment height of 18 mm, the lens depth remaining above the segment top is 24 minus 18, which is 6 mm. That leaves a very shallow distance portion, and the patient will be looking through the segment for much of their normal gaze range. The 6 mm sits above the segment, so it is distance lens rather than near lens. Claiming a full 18 mm above the segment reverses the two parts of the shape, and 12 mm would only apply if the segment sat at the geometric centre, which it does not in a 24 mm deep frame.
  162. The correct answer is A (a twisted front or uneven temple, so level the frame and remeasure): a 4 mm difference between the two eyes on a face with no noted asymmetry almost always means one eyewire is sitting higher than the other, which is what an uneven temple or a twisted front does. Standard bench alignment and correct seating come first, then both heights are taken again. Sending the order with a 4 mm difference bakes the frame fault into the finished lenses and breaks the 1 mm vertical difference the standard allows. Parallax from a poor marking angle shifts both eyes the same way rather than splitting them, and pupil size has no bearing on pupil centre height.
  163. The correct answer is C (5 mm of margin, so the design cuts in this frame comfortably): the measured distance from the deepest part of the groove to pupil centre is the fitting cross height, so 19 mm against a 14 mm published minimum leaves 19 minus 14, which is 5 mm of margin. That is ample and the design can be cut in this frame without a change of lens or frame. Claiming no margin misreads the measurement as starting from the geometric centre rather than from the groove. Adding the height and the minimum to reach 33 mm has no meaning, and quoting 14 mm as the margin simply repeats the minimum instead of subtracting it.
  164. The correct answer is B (the segment style and width, for example flat-top 28): a bifocal record needs the segment style and width alongside the labelled right and left heights, because a flat-top 28 and a flat-top 35 placed at the same height give different near fields and different segment widths. Without it the lab cannot cut what was measured. A binocular distance PD is a horizontal measurement and carries no information about segment style or height. The frame A measurement and distance between lenses belong to the layout and blank size calculation, and the demo lens power is irrelevant since demo lenses are discarded when the job is glazed.
  165. The correct answer is D (the candidate's viewing line no longer matches the patient's visual axis): the mark is only reliable when the marker's own open eye lies on the patient's line of sight, so the patient must fixate that eye rather than a target past the marker's shoulder. Looking over the shoulder swings the patient's visual axis away from the marking line and reintroduces the parallax that working at eye level was meant to remove. Distance fixation relaxes accommodation but does not move the pupil centre vertically. A distant target relaxes convergence rather than forcing it, and demo lens magnification does not shift where the visual axis crosses the lens.
  166. The correct answer is C (zero, the segment top sits level with the geometric centre): half of the 44 mm B measurement puts the geometric centre 22 mm above the deepest part of the groove, and the segment was ordered at exactly 22 mm, so the drop is 22 minus 22, which is zero. A drop of zero is a legitimate value and simply tells the lab that the segment top and the geometric centre share the same horizontal line. Reporting 22 mm repeats the segment height without referencing it to the centre, and 11 mm halves the height for no reason. Quoting 44 mm gives the full depth of the shape rather than a vertical distance to the segment top.
  167. The correct answer is B (the segment line cuts across the pupil and blurs distance vision): measuring a flat-top to pupil centre on a patient whose pupil sits 5 mm above the lid produces a segment height 5 mm greater than it should be, so the segment top lands right across the pupil in primary gaze. The patient sees the dividing line constantly and distance vision is broken by near power sitting in the straight ahead field. A segment that is too low, forcing the chin down for reading, comes from the opposite error. The two references are separated by several millimetres and are not equivalent, and this error shrinks rather than enlarges the usable distance field.
  168. The correct answer is A (a millimetre rule or fitting height gauge, read for each eye separately): with the frame still on the face, the height is read from the deepest part of the groove up to the dot using a millimetre rule or a purpose made fitting height gauge, taken twice and recorded separately for right and left. Both instruments are manual, which matters because automated equipment is not permitted in the examination. A pupilometer reads horizontal separation and cannot give a vertical height at all. A distometer measures the gap from lens back surface to cornea for vertex distance, and a lens clock reads surface curvature rather than any linear position on the frame.
  169. The correct answer is D (only frame two works, giving 17 mm against a 14 mm result): with the pupil centre at the vertical midpoint, the fitting cross height is half the B measurement, so the 28 mm frame gives 14 mm and the 34 mm frame gives 17 mm. Against a 16 mm published minimum the 34 mm frame clears by 1 mm while the 28 mm frame falls 2 mm short. Saying both work compares the B measurements directly to the minimum instead of halving them first. Saying neither works overlooks that 17 mm already exceeds 16 mm, and picking the 28 mm frame reverses which of the two heights belongs to which frame.
  170. The correct answer is C (his habitual head posture, measured in his natural working stance): the fitting cross always goes at pupil centre, but where pupil centre falls in the frame depends on how the patient actually holds their head, so a chin down worker must be measured standing as he works rather than seated and posed. Capturing the real posture is what makes the measured height match the lens he will wear at the counter. Standing height by itself sets nothing, since a tall patient with a level head needs no adjustment at all. Add power determines the corridor length chosen by the design, not the height, and the distance PD is a horizontal value with no vertical role.
  171. The correct answer is C (she must compare each reading to the ordered value against an allowance): neutralization is a discovery task where the reading itself is the answer, while verification is a comparison task where the reading is only half the work. The measuring technique is identical in both sections, so no finer resolution and no hand neutralization is introduced by the change of section. Recording findings before the lenses come out of the frame is sound habit but it is not what separates the two tasks.
  172. The correct answer is A (reading the whole work order and confirming the frame before measuring anything): the order names the lens design, which decides which allowances apply, and the frame lines are the ones candidates most often never check at all. Cleaning the lenses and focusing the eyepiece are genuine steps but neither protects against measuring a progressive as though it were single vision, or applying a segment allowance to a job with no segment. Writing patient identifiers is clerical and carries no marks on its own.
  173. The correct answer is D (return, because the frame supplied does not match the frame ordered): every line of a work order is a claim about the finished job, and the frame lines are checkable claims like any other. A perfect pair of lenses in the wrong frame is still a failed job. Colour has no dioptric allowance, so nothing on the supplied chart excuses the mismatch, and a note on the record does not make the eyewear what was ordered. Asking the patient to approve a substitution before the job has been verified reverses the order of the work.
  174. The correct answer is B (working one fixed sequence of checks on every pair, in the same order): 60 minutes across four pairs is roughly 15 minutes each including handling and writing, which is comfortable only when the hands move without the brain choosing the next step. Every ordered parameter is scored, so skipping the ones that look less significant loses marks outright. Reading all four orders in one block does not stop the wandering within each pair, and holding results to write up at the end is how findings get transposed between pairs.
  175. The correct answer is D (the frame make, model, colour, and eye size against the order): the frame check sits at the front of a sound sequence precisely because it is the item candidates most reliably miss once the lensometer is in reach. Add power, axis, and unwanted prism are all read on the instrument itself, so a candidate working through the lenses meets them in the normal course of the job. The frame is the one part of the order the lensometer can never tell you anything about.
  176. The correct answer is A (the chart supplied that day is the scoring standard, not her recollection): answers in both the written accept or return question and the practical verification section are marked against the chart on the desk, and a remembered edition may be out of date or wrongly recalled under time pressure. There is no section where memorized values replace the supplied chart. Nobody scores a candidate on whether the chart was consulted, and axis allowances are fixed by the standard rather than left to examiner judgement.
  177. The correct answer is B (that error may be inside the allowance while the real failure lies below): a 4 degree difference passes comfortably on a low cylinder and fails on a high one, so the number alone decides nothing until the cylinder power has been read off the order. The discipline is to work every line first, then choose the single most defensible failing. Listing several reasons loses marks rather than earning partial credit, there is no rule about which eye is named first, and an axis failure is a perfectly defensible single reason when it is genuinely outside.
  178. The correct answer is C (the lab has to rediagnose the job and may return it wrong twice): a useful return names the parameter, the ordered value, the measured value, the difference, and the allowance applied, which tells the lab exactly what to remake. Vague wording pushes the diagnostic work back down the chain and doubles the delay for the patient. Nothing about naming one lens obliges the lab to reject the return or calls for a patient signature, and a returned job does not invalidate the prescription behind it.
  179. The correct answer is A (state plainly that the job failed inspection and give a realistic remake date): a patient is entitled to know that the eyewear did not meet the standard and when the corrected pair is expected. Dispensing eyewear known to be outside tolerance, whether as a stopgap or as a trial to report back on, puts uncorrected optics in front of the patient and undoes the whole point of verifying. Blaming lab scheduling hides the real reason and leaves the record inaccurate.
  180. The correct answer is D (measure centre and edge thickness against what the ordered index predicts): index shows up physically, since a higher index lens of a given power is thinner and lighter than a lower index lens of the same power in the same frame and shape. Power, axis, and segment height all read correctly on a substituted material, because the lab still ground the ordered prescription, so re-reading them says nothing about what the lens is made of. Thickness, weight, and any material markings are what expose the substitution.
  181. The correct answer is C (reflected light off the surface shows a coloured residual reflection, not white): an anti-reflective stack cancels most of the reflected light and leaves a characteristic green, blue, or violet residual reflection, while an uncoated surface returns a plain white image of the light source. A faint body tint belongs to the substrate or to a tint order rather than to the coating. Coated lenses resist fingerprints only when a separate hydrophobic layer is added, and an anti-reflective coating raises transmission rather than lowering it.
  182. The correct answer is A (the lens design, single vision or conventional multifocal or progressive): progressives carry considerably tighter centration allowances than single vision lenses and conventional multifocals, so identifying the design is the step that selects the row. Material and index affect thickness and weight but no centration allowance. Frame dimensions and the specified monocular PD are the inputs you compare against, not the item that decides which allowance applies to the comparison.
  183. The correct answer is B (nothing to verify, because no base curve was specified on the order): a tolerance compares a measured value to a specified value, so a parameter the order never specified has nothing to be compared against and produces no verdict. The lab was free to choose a base curve suited to the power and material. Marking a failure against a value the candidate merely expected substitutes personal judgement for the work order, and a dioptre of latitude invented on the spot has the same problem. A lens clock reads a finished lens perfectly well, subject to the index correction it needs.
  184. The correct answer is D (run the same full sequence on each pair and record readings once): a fixed sequence with the verdict written as each item is measured is what makes roughly 15 minutes per pair achievable, and re-reading values already recorded is exactly the habit that consumed the lost time. Dropping heights or the frame check trades a certain loss of marks for time, and centration and frame errors are the failures this section is built to catch. Going back over the first pair again repeats the mistake that created the problem.
  185. The correct answer is A (centration and height errors that fail as often as power and carry marks): horizontal PRP position, vertical height in each lens, and the difference in height between the two lenses are separately scored items, and a job can pass every power check and still be returnable on centration alone. Axis is read on the lensometer along with the rest of the power, so dotting is not what reveals it. The add was already taken as the difference between the near and distance readings, and a material substitution is exposed by thickness and weight rather than by heights.
  186. The correct answer is D (the unwanted prism present at the PRP of each finished lens): the standard limits prism at the prism reference point whether or not prism was ordered, so a job with no prescribed prism still carries an unwanted prism check. Treating the absence of a prism order as the absence of a prism check is a common way to lose the item. A laboratory does not choose a base direction on its own initiative, and prism induced in the reading portion by the wearer's gaze is a design consequence rather than a measure against the order.
  187. The correct answer is B (special instructions are checkable claims and form part of the completed job): every line of a work order is a claim about the finished job, and the instruction block at the bottom is the part candidates most often skim past. A job that meets every optical allowance but ignores a written instruction is still incomplete. A patient supplied frame is verified like any other frame, against the order and for alignment and fit of the lenses. Nothing prevents returning a patient's old lenses, and no receipt is needed to verify a frame.
  188. The correct answer is D (read the model, colour code, and size markings stamped on the frame): manufacturers mark the model, colour code, eye size, bridge, and temple length on the temple or bridge, so one reading settles every frame claim on the order at once. Measuring a lens width confirms only the eye size and says nothing about model, colour, or temple length. A display sample may itself be a different colour or size from what was ordered, and telephoning the lab replaces a check that takes seconds with someone else's word.
  189. The correct answer is A (whether a flat-top, a progressive, or a single vision lens was ordered): a flat-top carries a segment height measured to the segment top, a progressive carries a fitting cross height measured to the pupil centre, and a single vision job carries an optical centre height. The design named on the order is what tells you which of those the figure means. The type of PD recorded, the frame construction, and any base curve or thickness entry all matter elsewhere in the job, but none of them defines what a vertical fitting figure refers to.
  190. The correct answer is C (recorded as a treatment failure, since the tint mismatches the order): treatments are ordered lines like any other, and a pair with mismatched density has not been made as specified. Absorption does not change dioptric power, so this is not a power item. The absence of a numeric row for tint does not make the treatment unverifiable, because matching the order is the whole test. Two lenses tinted to one order should match each other, and a visible difference between them is a defect rather than a normal appearance.
  191. The correct answer is B (rotate each lens against the known filter and watch for full extinction): a genuinely polarized lens goes dark as its axis crosses the known filter's axis, and the angle at which extinction occurs tells you whether both lenses are oriented the same way rather than one being mounted rotated. A lensometer target dims for a tint as readily as for a polarizer and shows nothing about orientation. Reflected colour off the surface identifies an anti-reflective coating, and an edge colour shift is a substrate observation.
  192. The correct answer is C (results held in the head get transposed between pairs and force re-measuring): the verdict for each item is written as it is measured precisely so that four pairs of results never have to be carried at once, and a candidate who loses track has to pick the eyewear up again and repeat work already done. No scoring penalty attaches to when a verdict is written, and the supplied chart stays on the desk throughout the section. The reason for the warning is practical time loss and error, not a procedural rule.
  193. The correct answer is A (optics are only part of the job, and defects make eyewear undispensable): scratches, chips, waves, striae, coating crazing, a sharp unbevelled edge, or a lens that rocks in the eyewire all make a pair unfit to hand over regardless of how the powers read. A tolerance chart lists dioptric and dimensional allowances and carries no defects row. Cosmetic faults are found by the dispenser at the last check before the patient sees the eyewear, and they occur on framed jobs as readily as on rimless ones.
  194. The correct answer is B (record the alignment fault and correct it before the pair is dispensed): four point touch and temple alignment belong to standard bench alignment, and a frame is returned to that standard by the dispenser as a normal bench task rather than sent back for a remake. The powers are unaffected, so this is not an optical return. Wearing habits explain why a frame drifts out of alignment over months, not why a new job arrives misaligned, and handing the pair over unadjusted moves the lenses off the positions just verified.
  195. The correct answer is C (locate the permanent engravings and the add marking on the lens surface): every progressive carries permanent circular engravings and an engraved add value, and finding them proves the design and gives the reference points from which the fitting cross and prism reference point are re-marked. A single vision lens shows no engravings at all, which is what exposes a substituted design. Edge thickness speaks to material rather than design, a matching distance reading occurs on either design, and a visible ledge belongs to a conventional multifocal.
  196. The correct answer is D (skip it, because nothing was ordered against which it could be compared): verification is a comparison, so a parameter with no specified value on the order yields no verdict and no marks, and time spent on it comes straight out of the 15 minutes the pair is allowed. Recording an unrequested measurement is harmless but pointless, and failing a job against a value never ordered substitutes personal judgement for the work order. Near centration is genuinely verifiable when a multifocal near PD was specified.
  197. The correct answer is B (confirming the frame make, model, colour, eye size, bridge, and temple): the frame check belongs at the front of the sequence because it is the item most often skipped once the lensometer is in reach, and lenses cannot be judged correct if they sit in the wrong frame. Hand neutralization is a separate skill and is not needed when a lensometer is available. Blank size belongs to layout before surfacing and edging, and habitual wearing position is a fitting observation rather than a check against the work order.
  198. The correct answer is A (the amount and base direction of prism, and the location of the PRP): a prism entry is verified for magnitude and for base direction, and the standard also constrains how far the prism reference point may sit from its specified position once prism is prescribed. Segment height is triggered only when a segment was ordered, base curve only when a base curve was specified, and centre thickness only when a thickness was specified. None of those three is brought into play by a prism entry on the order.
  199. The correct answer is C (name the single most defensible failure, the one furthest outside its allowance): the instruction asks for one reason, and listing several costs marks even when each one is correct. Working every line first is what puts a candidate in a position to choose, and the failure with the clearest margin beyond its allowance is the hardest to argue with. Position on the work order has no bearing on defensibility, and how easily a lab can remake an item is not the candidate's decision to weigh.
  200. The correct answer is A (tolerance is symmetric, so only the size of each difference matters): allowances are written as plus or minus a value, so a reading over the ordered power and one under it by the same amount are treated identically. Nothing in the standard grades minus errors more harshly than plus errors, and direction plays no part in choosing which row applies. Errors on two different lenses never cancel, because each lens is judged on its own against the value ordered for that eye.
  201. The correct answer is D (the work order and a written finding naming the remake required): the finding should state the parameter, the ordered value, the measured value, the difference, and the allowance applied, and the work order gives the lab the specification to work back to. Asking the lab to re-inspect and decide hands the diagnosis back to the party that made the error. Patient file material is not needed to remake a lens, and a laboratory works to the same published standard without being sent a copy of the chart.
  202. The correct answer is B (handling and re-edging can shift the right lens position and induce warpage): removing and remounting lenses, adjusting an eyewire, or springing a frame to seat a new lens can move the untouched lens off its verified position or stress it enough to alter its power. A replaced lens does not by itself change the optics of the lens beside it, and the original work order remains the specification unless the prescription changed. The first inspection is not void as a record, it is simply no longer current for the pair as now assembled.
  203. The correct answer is A (a groove cut too shallow or a lens edged undersize, recorded as failed): a lens that moves in its mount is not held at the axis and centration positions just measured, so the job cannot be dispensed as verified. Movement in a nylon supra mount is a defect rather than a characteristic of the mount, which relies on cord tension against a correctly cut groove of the right depth. The substrate index does not affect how a lens seats, and a warped front is a frame alignment issue rather than an optical failure of the lenses.
  204. The correct answer is C (confirm the material against the order and raise the choice before dispensing): verification checks the finished job against what was ordered, so a lens made in the material specified is not a lab error, and whether that material suits a child belongs to the dispensing decision. Children and any patient functioning with one good eye are normally dispensed in an impact resistant material. Changing the material at the bench without amending the order destroys the record of what was supplied, and dispensing with a caution to the parent leaves the risk in place.
  205. The correct answer is B (permanent markings on the frame and lenses, thickness, and side protection): prescription safety eyewear is a separate certified category with its own standard covering marked frames and lenses, minimum thickness, and side shields, and those items are confirmed on top of the ordinary optical checks. The optical allowances themselves are not tightened for safety eyewear. A second operator's reading and a base curve check are not what makes eyewear certified, and dress eyewear is never an acceptable substitute on a worksite.
  206. The correct answer is D (focus the eyepiece and learn the assigned instrument's controls and drum feel): the eyepiece must be focused to the individual operator before any reading can be trusted, and fumbling with unfamiliar controls once the clock runs is what turns 15 minutes a pair into 24. Equipment is assigned rather than chosen, so asking to swap is not open to a candidate. The familiarization period is for the equipment rather than the paperwork, and writing verdicts before measuring is guessing rather than verifying.
  207. The correct answer is A (one applied the single vision row and the other the progressive row): progressive centration allowances are considerably tighter than those for single vision lenses and conventional multifocals, so an identical millimetre difference can pass under one row and fail under the other. Choosing a row before identifying the lens design is one of the most common ways to lose marks in this section. A different measuring reference or a different instrument would have produced a different measurement, and the two candidates measured the same value.
  208. The correct answer is C (return, because the frame supplied is not the frame that was ordered): temple length is one of the frame claims on the work order, and a frame in the wrong length is a different frame no matter how well the lenses read. The absence of a dioptric row for temple length does not make that line unverifiable, since matching the order is the test. Bench adjustment does not create 5 mm of missing temple, and temple length has no effect on where the optical centres sit horizontally in the lenses.
  209. The correct answer is B (it governs a written accept or return decision and the practical verification section): the written section asks for accept or return with one reason using the chart supplied, and the practical verification section asks the same skill on four real pairs against their work orders. Neutralization supplies no work order to compare against, so nothing there is judged by a tolerance. Measurement is taken on a live examiner acting as patient, and bench alignment and lens insertion are service skills judged on workmanship.
  210. The correct answer is C (lenses pass against the order, and the transcription error must be resolved): verification compares the finished job to the work order, and the lab made exactly what it was told to make, so the lenses are not the failure. The failure sits upstream in transcription, and the prescription is the authority, so the order must be corrected and the job remade to the prescribed axis. Altering the prescription copy to match the order reverses that authority, and choosing between two axes on the patient's behalf is not an optician's call.
  211. The correct answer is A (complete every remaining component, since each one is marked separately): this section asks for each component of each job to be marked within or not within standard tolerance, so marks keep accruing after a failure has been found. Stopping at the first failure is the written accept or return habit applied in the wrong place, and even there the whole job is checked before one reason is chosen. Abandoning the right lens leaves scored items blank, and deferring components risks running out of clock with three pairs still waiting.
  212. The correct answer is B (it activates the base curve step, using a lens clock with index correction): base curve is verified only when the order specifies one, and a lens clock is calibrated for an index of 1.53, so a reading taken on any other material has to be corrected before it is compared. Base curve never substitutes for a power reading, since the same power can be made on several base curves. A base curve entry has no effect on centration allowances, and a lens clock reads the front surface of a mounted lens without removing it.
  213. The correct answer is C (the prism reference point, where prescribed prism is specified and measured): the standard states centration allowances in terms of the position of that point relative to the specified PD, and on a single vision job with no prescribed prism it coincides with the optical centre. The geometric centre of the edged shape is a frame property that moves with eye size and lens shape. The point is found by dotting on the lensometer, not from a segment top or from a lens clock reading of the front surface.
  214. The correct answer is D (query the order, because progressive centration is specified per eye separately): a progressive is judged against the specified monocular PD for each lens, and a binocular figure conceals asymmetry between the two eyes, which is common and clinically significant. Splitting a binocular figure in half invents monocular values that were never measured on the patient. A correct total can be produced by two wrong halves, so a matching total proves nothing, and the looser single vision row does not apply to a progressive.
  215. The correct answer is B (measure, subtract the ordered value, then compare that size against its allowance): the mechanics never change whatever the parameter, and the allowance chosen depends on the parameter, the power range it falls in, and the lens design. Recording readings and ordered values without doing the subtraction and the comparison leaves the verdict unmade, which is the step candidates skip under time pressure. Averaging repeated readings burns clock and hides a real measurement problem, and measuring until a reading agrees with the allowance is working backwards from the answer.
  216. The correct answer is C (Within tolerance: the 0.12 D difference is inside the 0.13 D allowance): subtracting the ordered -2.75 D from the measured -2.87 D leaves a difference of 0.12 D, and only the size of that difference matters. A sphere meridian of 2.75 D falls in the band running from 0.00 to plus or minus 6.50 D, where the standard allows plus or minus 0.13 D. Since 0.12 D is smaller than 0.13 D, the sphere power passes and the job is not returned on this item. The 2% rule only takes over above plus or minus 6.50 D, and no general 0.25 D sphere limit exists anywhere in the standard.
  217. The correct answer is A (Not within tolerance: the 0.14 D difference exceeds the 0.13 D allowance): the difference between the ordered +4.25 D and the measured +4.39 D is 0.14 D. A sphere meridian of 4.25 D still sits in the band from 0.00 to plus or minus 6.50 D, where the allowance is plus or minus 0.13 D. Because 0.14 D is larger than 0.13 D, the sphere power is out and this is a valid reason to return the job. The 0.15 D and 0.16 D figures belong to other rows, cylinder and progressive sphere, and a 2% calculation does not apply at all below plus or minus 6.50 D.
  218. The correct answer is D (Two percent of 8.00 D allows 0.16 D, so the sphere is within tolerance): the difference between the ordered -8.00 D and the measured -8.15 D is 0.15 D. A sphere meridian of 8.00 D is above plus or minus 6.50 D, so the fixed dioptric allowance gives way to 2% of the meridian power, and 2% of 8.00 D is 0.16 D. Since 0.15 D is smaller than 0.16 D the sphere passes, and holding this lens to the fixed 0.13 D figure would wrongly return good work. Nothing in the standard tightens the sphere allowance at high power, and there is no blanket 0.25 D limit.
  219. The correct answer is B (Not within tolerance: the 0.22 D difference exceeds the 0.19 D allowance): the difference between the ordered +9.50 D and the measured +9.72 D is 0.22 D. Above plus or minus 6.50 D the allowance is 2% of the meridian power, and 2% of 9.50 D is 0.19 D. Since 0.22 D is larger than 0.19 D the sphere is out and the job goes back for that reason. The fixed 0.13 D figure belongs to the lower band and is the wrong allowance to quote here, while neither 0.24 D nor 0.29 D comes from any sphere row at all.
  220. The correct answer is D (-6.13 D, taken from the fixed allowance for this sphere power band): a sphere meridian of 6.00 D sits inside the band running from 0.00 to plus or minus 6.50 D, where the allowance is plus or minus 0.13 D. Adding 0.13 D to 6.00 D gives 6.13 D, so a reading of -6.13 D is the most minus sphere that still passes and -6.14 D would fail. Applying 2% at this power gives only 0.12 D and would reject a lens the standard accepts, and the 0.16 D figure belongs to the progressive row rather than to single vision. Rounding to the nearest quarter dioptre has no place in a tolerance decision.
  221. The correct answer is A (-9.18 D, because 2% of 9.00 D gives a 0.18 D allowance): a sphere meridian of 9.00 D is above plus or minus 6.50 D, so the allowance is 2% of the meridian power, which works out to 0.18 D. Adding 0.18 D to 9.00 D gives 9.18 D, so -9.18 D still passes and -9.19 D does not. Keeping the fixed 0.13 D figure at this power is the most common mistake at the bench and would reject good work. The 0.16 D figure belongs to progressive sphere, and no 5% rule appears anywhere in the sphere rows.

A difference that equals the allowance is within tolerance, because a job fails only when the difference exceeds the allowance, so this sphere passes either way. That coincidence happens only at the boundary, and a step either side of 6.50 D separates the two figures.

A cylinder of 4.75 D is above 4.50 D, so it moves to 4% of the cylinder power, which is 0.19 D, and a 0.17 D difference is inside that and passes. The higher cylinder is judged more loosely because the percentage rule widens the allowance the moment it takes over. Nothing about the size of the error itself changed between the two lenses.

  17. Sphere power carries its own separate allowance and never selects the axis row. The ordered axis value itself and the add power play no part in choosing the axis allowance either.

There is no blanket ten degree rule, and each eye must be marked on its own cylinder power.

The correct answer is A (The reading must be corrected for the true index before it is compared): a lens clock measures surface curvature and converts it to power using a fixed assumed index of 1.53, so on a 1.67 index lens the number on the dial is not the true surface power. The reading is multiplied by the true index minus one, divided by 0.53, and only that corrected value is compared to the specified base curve and to the 0.75 D allowance. Skipping the correction can flip a verdict, since the error introduced grows with both the index and the curve. The allowance itself stays at 0.75 D for every material, the reading is not halved, and the front surface is not converted into a back surface power.

The correct answer is B (within tolerance, because 2 degrees equals the allowance and equality passes): segment tilt is allowed 2 degrees measured from the 180 reference, and a reading of 002 sits exactly 2 degrees away from that reference. A difference equal to the allowance is within tolerance, the same convention that governs every other line of the chart. Reading the error as 178 degrees comes from subtracting 2 from 180 rather than measuring the angle away from the horizontal reference. Progressives carry no segment at all, so segment tilt is a conventional multifocal measure.
  74. The correct answer is D (within tolerance, because 0.3 mm exactly equals the centre thickness allowance): 2.5 minus 2.2 is 0.3 mm, and a specified centre thickness is allowed 0.3 mm, so a difference equal to the allowance passes. Centre thickness is only verified when the order specifies a value, and here it does, so the check is live. A specified thickness does not have to be produced exactly, which is the reason an allowance exists for it. Passing powers do not remove the thickness check, because each parameter is marked on its own.
  75. The correct answer is A (not within tolerance, because the 0.4 mm shortfall exceeds the 0.3 mm allowance): 2.2 minus 1.8 is 0.4 mm, and a specified centre thickness is allowed 0.3 mm. Tolerance is symmetric, so a lens thinner than ordered fails exactly as a lens thicker than ordered would, and the direction of the error is never a defence. The allowance for a specified centre thickness is 0.3 mm, not 0.5 mm. The check applies to plus and minus lenses alike, and a thin centre on a plus powered rimless job also raises a real flexure and breakage concern.
  76. The correct answer is C (warpage, allowed up to 1.00 dioptre on the finished mounted lens): a lens forced into an undersized opening is bent by the eyewire, and that stress changes the power measured while the lens is mounted, which is exactly what warpage describes. Base curve is verified with a lens clock and only when a curve was specified, and it does not explain a reading that changes between mounted and unmounted states. Centre thickness is a linear measure taken with a gauge and is unaffected by mounting stress. Cylinder power has its own allowance but is a prescription parameter, not a description of frame induced stress.
  77. The correct answer is D (not within tolerance, because 1.25 dioptres exceeds the 1.00 dioptre warpage allowance): warpage is allowed up to 1.00 dioptre, and a 1.25 dioptre change between the mounted and unmounted readings is larger than that. Warpage has a published numeric allowance, so it is not treated as a purely cosmetic finding. Small amounts of measurable warpage are tolerated, which is why an allowance exists rather than an automatic remake rule. The remedy is a correctly sized lens for that opening, not a tighter screw or a stretched eyewire.
  78. The correct answer is A (the first three share 2.5 mm, and the progressive gets 1.0 mm): single vision and conventional multifocal lenses, which includes both the flat-top and the round segment bifocal, allow the PRPs within 2.5 mm of the specified distance pupillary distance. A mounted progressive is held to 1.0 mm from the specified monocular pupillary distance, considerably tighter than the others. Reversing the two figures is the classic error and turns good jobs into returns and returnable jobs into accepts. Horizontal allowances very much depend on lens design, so identifying the design comes before any measurement is compared.
  79. The correct answer is B (within tolerance, because the 2.5 mm error equals the horizontal allowance exactly): the specified distance pupillary distance is 33.0 plus 32.5, which is 65.5 mm, and 68.0 minus 65.5 is 2.5 mm. The mounted single vision allowance for the PRPs is 2.5 mm from the specified distance pupillary distance, and a difference equal to the allowance is within tolerance. The comparison is made between the measured separation of the two PRPs and the specified distance pupillary distance for the pair, so the error is not halved before comparing. Halving the difference would let a 5.0 mm error pass, which is why the method is wrong.
  80. The correct answer is C (within tolerance, because 1.0 mm equals the progressive horizontal allowance exactly): 34.0 minus 33.0 is 1.0 mm, and a mounted progressive allows the PRP within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular pupillary distance. A difference equal to the allowance passes, so this measure is marked within rather than returned. The progressive figure is compared per lens against that eye's monocular value and is not doubled to cover the pair. The 2.5 mm allowance belongs to single vision and conventional multifocal jobs, and applying it here would let a genuinely failing progressive through.
  81. The correct answer is B (only the progressive returns, since its horizontal allowance is 1.0 mm): a mounted progressive allows the PRP within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular pupillary distance, and 1.5 mm is larger than that. The single vision job is allowed 2.5 mm from the specified distance pupillary distance, so the same 1.5 mm error passes there. The single vision allowance is the looser of the two, not the tighter one, which is the reversal candidates fall into under time pressure. Identical measurement, different lens design, opposite verdicts, and only the design decides it.
  82. The correct answer is A (only the bifocal returns, since each segment carries a per lens vertical allowance): a segment must sit within 1.0 mm of the specified height on each lens, and a 1.6 mm drop fails that check on both lenses of the bifocal. The mounted vertical requirement for a single vision job is stated only as the difference in height between the two PRPs, no more than 1.0 mm, and that difference is zero here, so the single vision job passes. Equal heights satisfy the single vision requirement but do not satisfy a per lens requirement, which is what separates the two jobs. Progressives behave like segments here, carrying both a per lens vertical allowance and a limit on the difference.
  83. The correct answer is D (each cross is compared to its own monocular value, not a halved total): the order gives 30.0 mm right and 32.0 mm left, so the right cross is compared against 30.0 mm and the left against 32.0 mm. Checking both against a halved total of 31.0 mm builds a 1.0 mm error into each eye before the lenses are even measured, and with only 1.0 mm of allowance on a progressive that turns perfect work into a return and lets a 2.0 mm error pass. Halving a binocular total is never correct for a progressive, however the total was reached. Monocular data drives horizontal checks on progressives, not only vertical ones, and the bridge width is not part of this comparison.
  84. The correct answer is B (vertical location not within tolerance, difference between lenses within tolerance): each fitting cross sits 22.2 minus 21.0, which is 1.2 mm above the specified height, and that exceeds the 1.0 mm per lens vertical allowance for a mounted progressive. The two crosses match each other exactly, so the difference between lenses is zero and passes its own check comfortably. Matching heights satisfy only the difference requirement, and they never satisfy the per lens requirement when both lenses are wrong by the same amount. Mark the two measures separately, then return the job on the per lens failure.
  85. The correct answer is C (return the job to the lab and explain the finding to the patient): a deep scratch through the middle of the reading area degrades the vision the lens was made to deliver, so the job has failed inspection even though every numeric measure passed. Cosmetic and mechanical inspection is a scored part of verification and is not optional once the optics check out. Dispensing now with a promise to replace the lens later hands the patient a defective product and damages trust when the reason surfaces. A scratch through a coated surface cannot be polished out at the bench, and attempting it damages the coating further.
  86. The correct answer is A (return the second lens, and assess the first for edge integrity and security): a chip sitting 4 mm inside the lens opening is in the wearer's field of view and returns the job, since it scatters light and is plainly visible. A chip confined to the bevel edge and hidden by the eyewire is judged on whether the lens still seats securely and whether the chip could spread, so it needs assessment rather than an automatic verdict. Treating every chip as an automatic remake ignores that judgement and wastes lab time. Treating chips as purely cosmetic ignores both the scatter in the field of view and the risk of a lens working loose.
  87. The correct answer is D (surface waves in the lens, a cosmetic fault that returns the job): sweeping a straight line across the lens and watching it bow or ripple is the standard way to reveal surface waves, which come from uneven surfacing or polishing. Waves are a cosmetic and optical quality failure that passing power and centration readings will not detect. Peripheral softness in a progressive is expected in the outer zones and is not a rippling distortion in one localized patch of the lens. An unfocused eyepiece affects lensometer readings only, and tint density variation changes brightness rather than making straight lines bend.
  88. The correct answer is C (striae in the first lens and bubbles in the second lens): striae appear as fine streaks or strings inside the lens material from uneven flow during manufacture, and bubbles are trapped gas inclusions in the substrate. Both are material faults present before surfacing, so the lab remakes the lens rather than repolishing it, and neither cleans off. Crazing is a coating fault showing as a fine network of cracks on the surface, not as streaks within the substrate. Warpage is a measurable power change under stress rather than a visible mark, and drill stress cracks radiate from a rimless mounting hole.
  89. The correct answer is B (return it, since the edge finish ordered was not delivered on the job): the work order is a contract and a polished edge was ordered, so a grey matte edge is a failure to deliver what was specified. On a rimless job the edge is fully exposed and a frosted finish is highly visible on the wearer's face, so the cosmetic consequence is real. The absence of a numeric allowance for edge polish does not mean the absence of a requirement, since some items are pass or fail rather than measured. An unpolished edge is a cosmetic failure and is a separate matter from a sharp unbevelled edge, which is a safety failure.
  90. The correct answer is D (a safety fault, so the missing safety bevel must be corrected before dispensing): a sharp ridge standing proud of the eyewire can cut the wearer or anyone handling the eyewear, which makes it a safety matter rather than a matter of appearance. A safety bevel belongs on both the front and back edges of any lens that can contact the wearer, and its absence is a genuine defect. Passing every optical measure does not make a job dispensable, because mechanical and cosmetic inspection carries its own verdict. Reshaping the eyewire does not remove a sharp corner from the lens itself.
  91. The correct answer is A (on both front and back edges, removing sharp corners that could contact the wearer): the safety bevel is a small chamfer applied to the front and back edges of the finished lens so no sharp corner remains against skin or fingers. A thick edged minus lens is the usual case where the corners are most pronounced, but the practice is not limited to one power or one frame type. Covering the front edge with an eyewire does not remove the sharp corner underneath, and the lens is handled during glazing and adjustment. The V bevel exists to seat the lens in the groove of the eyewire and does nothing to soften the front and back corners.
  92. The correct answer is C (the lens needs a groove of correct depth, not a V bevel, for this mount): a nylon supra mount holds the lens with a cord seated in a groove cut into the lens edge, so a V bevel leaves the cord nothing to sit in and the lens works free. The bevel type must match the mount, a V bevel for a metal or plastic eyewire, a groove of correct depth and position for a supra mount, and clean drill holes for a rimless. Blaming the cord ignores that the lens was never finished for that mount in the first place. Supra mount lenses are neither edged flat and cemented nor simply edged smaller to fit.
  93. The correct answer is B (the lens is oversized and the sprung eyewire is warping it in the frame): a lens edged slightly too large springs the eyewire, and the stress bends the lens so the mounted reading differs from the reading taken off the frame. A screw that will barely close is the physical confirmation that the opening is being forced. The remedy is a correctly sized lens, not a tighter screw, and the condition is recorded as warpage against its published allowance. An undersized lens is loose rather than stressed, a coating does not change measured sphere power, and a drifting instrument would also change the unmounted reading.
  94. The correct answer is A (the lens was edged undersized, or the bevel is wrongly placed or shaped): a lens that rocks and pops forward under light pressure is not being gripped by the groove, and the two usual reasons are a lens cut slightly small for the opening or a bevel that is off centre, too shallow, or the wrong profile. An oversized lens produces the opposite picture, a tight fit with a sprung eyewire and stressed power readings. Warpage and lost face form change how the front sits on the face rather than how the lens sits in its opening. A missing safety bevel and a peeling coating are real faults but neither loosens a lens.
  95. The correct answer is D (return the job, since crazing and patchy coverage are coating faults): a fine network of surface cracks is crazing in the anti-reflective coating, and a dull patchy area is incomplete or failed coating coverage, both of which are manufacturing faults rather than dirt. Neither will lift with cleaning, and solvent on a compromised coating usually makes the appearance worse. Coating condition is part of the cosmetic inspection that every verification includes, so it sits inside the standard rather than outside it. Dispensing a defective coating and writing a note on the file simply moves the problem to the patient.
  96. The correct answer is B (return it, since the frame supplied does not match what the order specified): make, model, colour code, eye size, bridge, and temple length are all claims on the work order and all checkable, so a wrong colour and a wrong eye size mean the job as delivered is not the job that was ordered. A perfect pair of lenses in the wrong frame is a failed job, and confirming the frame is the most commonly skipped step in verification because candidates go straight to the lensometer. There is no eye size allowance that absorbs a 2 mm difference from the ordered frame. The centration already measured within allowance, so the failure is the frame itself rather than a consequence of it.
  97. The correct answer is C (dispense an impact resistant material for both as a matter of course): children's eyewear and any patient who depends on one good eye are the two standing cases where an impact resistant material is the expected default, whatever the wearer's daily activity. Dress lenses are expected to meet a basic impact resistance requirement in any case, which is why polycarbonate, Trivex, and treated materials dominate and untreated glass is now rare. Untreated glass is the poorest choice here, since better scratch resistance does not offset its behaviour on impact. The absence of a workplace hazard does not remove the everyday risk that both of these patients carry.
  98. The correct answer is A (dress eyewear will not serve, and certified eyewear adds marking and thickness checks): prescription safety eyewear is a separate category with its own standard, covering certified frames and lenses, minimum thickness, side protection, and permanent markings on both frame and lens. Verifying it means confirming those markings and that certification on top of every optical check the job already needs. Choosing polycarbonate for a dress frame does not make the finished eyewear certified for a workplace hazard, since the frame and the markings matter as much as the material. Clip-on side shields do not convert dress eyewear into certified safety eyewear either.
  99. The correct answer is D (a fault that harms vision, safety, appearance, or the order returns the job): a cosmetic or mechanical finding stands on its own when it degrades what the wearer sees, creates a risk of injury, is plainly visible on the face, or differs from what the order specified. A scratch through the reading area, a sharp unbevelled edge, a matte edge where a polish was ordered, and a crazed coating each meet that test with every numeric measure passing. Restricting returns to faults that shift a measured power would let all four of those through. Waiting for the patient to notice puts the judgement in the wrong hands and costs the dispensary the second appointment anyway.
  100. The correct answer is C (the right horizontal centration error of 1.4 mm against a 1.0 mm allowance): a mounted progressive allows the PRP within 1.0 mm of the specified monocular distance pupillary distance, so 1.4 mm is a measurable failure that can be stated with the ordered value, the measured value, the difference, and the allowance applied. A faint scuff outside the wearer's field is the weaker finding, and naming it when a measurable tolerance failure exists shows the chart was not applied. Part A asks for one reason, so listing both costs marks even when one of them is right. The job does not pass, because the 2.5 mm allowance belongs to single vision and conventional multifocal jobs.
  101. The correct answer is C (standard alignment is the reference state and must be restored first): The first task of the service section asks only for standard bench alignment, the symmetrical off the face condition the manufacturer intended, so no patient adjustment belongs in that task at all. Working from a distorted frame also makes diagnosis impossible, because a fault seen later on the face could belong to the face or to the frame. Nose pads are set after the front and the temples, so pad order does not justify shaping temple ends first. Neutralization belongs to a different section, and cold bending a metal temple end is normal practice rather than a fracture risk.
  102. The correct answer is A (the four point touch test has failed and the frame is out of plane): four point touch means the two lower eyewire rims and the two temple ends meet the bench at the same moment, so a rock under diagonal pressure proves one of those four points sits high. The cause is a twist at the bridge or an endpiece, or a temple bent out of plane, and it is corrected at the source rather than by bending a temple to hide it. A shallow bevel loosens the lens in the groove instead of lifting a corner off the bench. Neither bridge width nor screw torque produces a diagonal rock.
  103. The correct answer is D (decline, because metal frames are adjusted cold with nylon jaw pliers): heat belongs to zyl, cellulose acetate, propionate, and nylon based plastic fronts, not to metal. Warming a metal frame risks blistering or discolouring the plating and softening the solder joints at the bridge and the endpieces, and a mounted lens sitting in the hot air can craze an anti reflective coating. Face form on a metal frame is set cold at the bridge, one eyewire at a time, with nylon jaws that will not mark the finish. Removing the lenses or warming gently does not make heating the metal correct.
  104. The correct answer is B (retroscopic tilt, the reverse of the usual pantoscopic setting): pantoscopic tilt brings the bottom of the front back toward the cheeks when the temples are held level, so the opposite condition, with the lower rim standing away from the face, is retroscopic tilt. It is rarely a deliberate setting and is usually a fault created at the endpieces. Face form is the horizontal curvature of the front and is judged from above, not from the side. Vertical misalignment means one lens plane is rotated relative to the other, which is a bridge twist rather than a tilt of the whole front.
  105. The correct answer is A (the splay angle, the pad angle relative to the plane of the front): the splay angle is judged from above and governs whether the pad bears evenly from its front edge to its back edge. A pad touching at the front edge only, with its back edge standing away, has too much splay for that nose, so the splay is decreased with pad adjusting pliers. Contact from the top edge to the bottom edge is governed by the frontal angle, viewed from the front. Rotation of the pad in the side view, so that it follows the slope of the nose, is the vertical angle, and guard arm height simply raises or lowers the whole pad.
  106. The correct answer is C (nylon jaw pliers, which grip firmly without marking a plated surface): soft nylon jaws hold the eyewire securely while spreading the load over a wide contact area, which is why they are the standard choice for straightening eyewires, setting face form, and any work on plated or painted metal. Chain nose jaws are flat but bare, so they can burnish or scar gold plating. Snipe nose jaws are for reaching into tight spaces such as guard arms rather than for eyewire work. Angling pliers are built for temple butt and endpiece angles, not for restoring an eyewire curve.
  107. The correct answer is C (front work precedes pad work, or the pad setting must be redone): the accepted working order is front, then temples, then pads, because every change to the front moves the guard arms and therefore moves the pads with them. Correcting a bridge twist after the pads are set forces the whole pad step to be repeated, which wastes time in a thirty minute station. A twisted bridge remains correctable at any point, so the fitted pads do not lock it in. Pad adjusting pliers work on the pad itself and do not loosen a guard arm when the bridge is moved.
  108. The correct answer is B (the right temple is bent inward at its butt portion): crossed temples happen when one temple is angled in too far where it leaves the endpiece, so it swings past the centre line and rides over its partner. The correction is made at the butt portion with angling pliers, after warming on a plastic frame. A temple bowed outward along its length, or an endpiece angle that has opened, produces the opposite fault, a visible gap between the folded temple and the back of the front. Extra face form in one eyewire changes the horizontal curve of the front rather than the fold path of a temple.
  109. The correct answer is D (the damage is permanent and the front must be replaced): loss of gloss, bubbling, whitening, and a sagging bridge are the classic signs of an overheated plastic front, and the material has been changed rather than simply softened. Cooling does not restore the surface, and a second warming cycle only drives the distortion further. Bubbling is never a working temperature indicator, since correct warming is judged by flexing the eyewire gently and feeling the material turn supple. Short passes through the airflow with the front moving, rather than a long soak, prevent this outcome.
  110. The correct answer is A (temples open, with both lower rims and both temple ends touching): the test is performed face down on a clean hard flat surface with the temples fully open, and the four contact points are the two lower eyewire rims and the two temple ends. Gentle pressure at diagonally opposite corners then reveals any rock. Folding the temples removes two of the four reference points entirely, so neither folded option can be tested. Nose pads sit above the plane of the front when the frame is face down and never form part of the four points.
  111. The correct answer is B (in the front, which carries a twist across the bridge): vertical alignment is checked by sighting along the front from the temple side, and both lens planes should lie in the same plane. When one plane is rotated relative to the other while the temples fold cleanly and the frame does not rock, the twist sits in the front and is corrected at the bridge. A temple bent outward at the bend shows up in the fold and spread checks instead. A slack screw allows the lens to rattle, and unequal guard arm heights move the pads without rotating a lens plane.
  112. The correct answer is D (pad adjusting pliers, whose cupped jaw and peg jaw cradle the pad): the cupped jaw supports the face of the pad while the peg jaw locates behind it, so the pad can be rotated through its splay, frontal, or vertical angle without twisting the guard arm or scoring the pad box. Snipe nose jaws reach into narrow spaces but grip the arm rather than the pad. Half padded pliers protect one surface only and are shaped for endpiece and eyewire work. Angling pliers set temple and endpiece angles and are far too coarse for a nose pad.
  113. The correct answer is C (the frame pulls sideways and sits crooked on the face): one temple angled in and one angled out is the classic cause of a frame that pulls to one side, because the two temples press unevenly against the sides of the head. Standard alignment requires the temples to be parallel along their straight portion when viewed from above, or to diverge slightly and evenly. Lens fogging is a ventilation and vertex matter, not a temple parallelism fault. Riding too high with lashes touching comes from pad separation and vertex settings, and even bridge marking is a sign of good pad bearing rather than a complaint.
  114. The correct answer is A (sight from above and compare each eyewire's rearward curve against the bridge): face form is the horizontal curvature of the front, so it is judged by looking down on the frame from above and comparing how far each eyewire sweeps back relative to the bridge. Sighting from the side compares the two lens planes, which is the vertical alignment check. Rocking the front on the bench tests four point touch and reveals a twist rather than a face form difference. Folding the temples tests fold clearance for crossing or gapping and says nothing about horizontal curvature.
  115. The correct answer is A (move the front through the airflow in short passes, testing pliability by hand): moving the front spreads the heat across the whole eyewire instead of concentrating it at one point, and short passes let the material come up to temperature gradually. Pliability is judged by flexing the eyewire gently between the fingers, because acetate reaches working temperature well before it shows any visible change. Holding the bridge in the hottest part of the stream is exactly what produces whitening, bubbling, and a sagging bridge. A fixed timer ignores material thickness and blower output, and warming parts that are not being bent adds risk with no benefit.
  116. The correct answer is D (seat the lens fully in the groove, then snug the screw): a rattle with a gap at the eyewire joint means the bevel apex is not seated all the way around, so the eyewire cannot close properly. The lens is reseated first, then the screw is tightened until it is snug without being over torqued, and the seating is re checked by sighting around the bevel. Stacking a washer or adding face form hides the symptom while leaving the lens loose and free to shift axis. A gap at the joint is a fault in every case and is never left in place as a stress relief measure.
  117. The correct answer is B (pantoscopic brings the lower rim toward the face, retroscopic takes it away): with the temples held level, pantoscopic tilt places the bottom of the front closer to the cheeks, which follows the downward rotation of the eyes for near work. Retroscopic tilt is the reverse condition and normally appears as a fault rather than a deliberate setting. Reversing those two definitions is the most common error on this topic and describes neither condition correctly. Raising or lowering the whole front is a bridge and pad matter, and horizontal curvature of the front is face form, not tilt.
  118. The correct answer is C (angling pliers, with one wide flat jaw and one narrow jaw): the wide flat jaw braces the endpiece while the narrow jaw applies a controlled bend at the temple butt, which is exactly how spread angle is opened or closed. Nylon jaw pliers grip without marking but cannot deliver the localized leverage needed at a butt portion. Chain nose pliers are the general gripping tool for metal work rather than an angle setting tool. Pad adjusting pliers, with a cupped jaw and a peg, are built to hold a nose pad and have no role at the endpiece.
  119. The correct answer is D (a temple bowed outward or an endpiece angle that has opened): a gap on the folded temple means that temple is standing off the back of the front, which happens when the temple is bowed outward along its length or when the endpiece angle has opened past the design setting. A temple bent inward at the butt gives the opposite fault, crossing over its partner. A slack screw or an undersized lens produces rattle and lost axis rather than a fold gap. A wrong splay angle or a bent guard arm affects pad bearing, and excess face form or bridge height shows up in the front checks.
  120. The correct answer is B (removing the twist afterwards changes the tilt, so the tilt needs redoing): tilt is set at the endpieces, and a twist across the front moves both endpieces relative to one another, so any tilt set before the twist is removed is measured against a moving reference. Working horizontal before vertical, and front before temples, exists precisely to stop this rework. A twist does not become permanent because an endpiece was worked, since it remains correctable at the bridge. Tilt cannot square a twisted front by itself, and a twist usually shows as a rock on four point touch, so alignment work certainly remains.
  121. The correct answer is A (the blade must match the slot in width and in thickness): a blade narrower or thinner than the slot bears on a small area, cams out under load, and chews the slot into the burred state described. A blade that fills the slot transmits torque evenly and is the reason a graded screwdriver set belongs in the bench kit. Pressing harder with the wrong blade increases the damage rather than preventing it. Thread compound stops a good screw from backing out but does nothing for a ruined slot, and gripping the head with pliers marks the finish without turning the screw.
  122. The correct answer is C (the bridge, which sets eyewire height and distance from centre): bridge symmetry means the bridge places the two eyewires at matching heights and matching distances from the centre line, so a height and lateral difference together points to the bridge. Everything downstream, including pad bearing and lens height on the face, depends on this being right. An endpiece sets the temple angle to the front and would show up in the fold and spread checks. A guard arm moves only the pad, and a temple cannot raise one eyewire above the other while four point touch still passes.
  123. The correct answer is B (the coating has crazed from heat, and the lens must be replaced): a fine crackle pattern in an anti reflective coating is heat crazing, and it is permanent. Anti reflective, photochromic, and some high index and polycarbonate lenses tolerate far less heat than a basic CR 39 lens, so the frame is warmed while the lens is kept out of the airflow. Crazing is not moisture, so drying changes nothing. Loosening a screw relieves mechanical stress but has no effect on a damaged coating, and polishing a coated surface removes the coating rather than the pattern.
  124. The correct answer is D (the frontal angle, seen from the front in the vertical plane): the frontal angle is the tilt of the pad in the vertical plane as viewed from the front, and it governs whether the pad bears evenly from its top edge down to its bottom edge. A pad touching at the top edge only needs its frontal angle changed with pad adjusting pliers. The splay angle, judged from above, controls contact from the front edge to the back edge. The vertical angle is pad rotation seen from the side, and guard arm length simply widens or narrows the separation between the two pads.
  125. The correct answer is A (neither change can be credited, so the remaining error stays unknown): the standing rule for pad work is one angle at a time, then re check the bearing, because that is the only way to learn which movement produced which result. Changing two angles together leaves the candidate unable to say whether the improvement came from the frontal change, the splay change, or a partial cancelling of both. Guard arms do work harden with repeated bending, but that risk is not what makes this method unreadable. The two angles can certainly both be altered on one pad, and pad adjusting pliers are perfectly able to hold the pad for either movement.
  126. The correct answer is A (inspect and diagnose every fault before picking up any tool): the task is diagnostic before it is manual, and candidates lose marks by grabbing pliers and bending before the actual fault has been named, which usually stacks a second fault on top of the first. A full look over costs seconds and saves rework in a thirty minute station covering three tasks. Warming the whole front applies heat to areas that do not need it and invites whitening or distortion. Fixing the most obvious fault first ignores the working order, and loosening the eyewire screws only unseats the lenses.
  127. The correct answer is C (a hard flat surface is required, since padding hides a small rock): four point touch depends on a true reference plane, and any padding compresses under the high point so the frame appears to sit flat when it does not. A clean hard bench top gives the unforgiving surface the test needs. Scratching is managed by keeping the surface clean rather than by padding it. The rock is judged by gentle pressure at diagonally opposite corners and is easily missed by feel alone on a soft mat, and the test assesses whether the front and temples lie in one plane rather than how the temples fold.
  128. The correct answer is D (at the bridge, using nylon jaw pliers, one eyewire at a time): face form is the horizontal curve of the front and is set at the bridge, working one eyewire at a time so the two sides can be matched against each other. On a metal frame the work is done cold, and nylon jaws are chosen because they will not mark intact plating. Angling pliers at the temple butt change spread angle and tilt, not the curve of the front. The eyewire screw controls lens seating only, and the guard arm carries the nose pad rather than the eyewire curve.
  129. The correct answer is B (bring the right temple back into plane at its butt portion): when both lower rims sit flat and the front sights square from every direction, the fault has been isolated to the temple that floats, so the correction belongs at that temple's butt portion. Twisting the bridge introduces a front fault to compensate for a temple fault and fails the vertical alignment check afterwards. Bending the left temple upward makes both ends wrong instead of one and destroys the reference plane completely. Face form changes the horizontal curve of the front and has no effect on where a temple end lands on the bench.
  130. The correct answer is C (zyl, acetate, propionate, and nylon based fronts warmed, metal worked cold): plastic fronts must be warmed before bending, and metal frames are adjusted cold, which is why the service section issues one of each. Reversing the rule and warming metal risks blistered plating, softened solder joints at the bridge and endpieces, and heat damage to any mounted lens. Warming every front applies heat where it does nothing useful and where it can damage a finish. Working every front cold cracks or snaps zyl, since cold acetate has almost no give before it fails.
  131. The correct answer is D (otherwise a fault cannot be attributed to the face or the frame): a distorted frame gives no reliable starting point, so a crooked appearance on the face might come from the frame, from facial asymmetry, or from both, and the optician has no way to separate them. Restoring the manufacturing reference first makes the remaining error genuinely diagnostic.
