Bench alignment does not replace the patient adjustment, which still follows once the reference state exists. It is standard practice on every returned frame rather than a warranty formality, and it cannot change the power of a lens.
  132. The correct answer is A (half padded pliers, with one padded jaw and one bare jaw): this tool is built for exactly the case where one surface needs a firm bite and the opposing surface must be protected, so the bare jaw takes the load on the hidden inside face while the padded jaw sits against the visible plating. Chain nose pliers have two bare jaws and would mark the outer surface. Nylon jaw pliers protect both faces but give up the firm bite the inside surface needs. Snipe nose pliers are shaped for access into narrow spaces rather than for controlled gripping of an endpiece.
  133. The correct answer is B (repeated bending work hardens the metal until it finally snaps): every bend and counter bend in the same place stiffens the metal and reduces its remaining ductility, so the temple or endpiece eventually fractures with no warning. The habit is avoided by approaching the target angle in small steps and checking after each one rather than overshooting and correcting. Plating can lift with heavy handling, but that is a cosmetic fault rather than the reason a frame is lost. A screw thread is not loaded by an endpiece bend, and cold bending generates nothing like the temperature needed to flow solder.
  134. The correct answer is B (four point touch, face form, pantoscopic tilt, temple fold and spread, pads): this order works the front first and starts with horizontal alignment tested by four point touch, then the horizontal curve of the front, then tilt at the endpieces, before moving on to the temples and finishing with the pads. Every other order shown puts pads or temples ahead of front work, so the earlier steps get moved by the later ones and have to be repeated. Starting from the pads is the worst case, since levelling the front afterwards shifts the guard arms and wastes the entire pad setting.
  135. The correct answer is C (the vertical angle, aligning the pad with the length of the nose): the vertical angle is the rotation of the pad about a horizontal front to back axis as viewed from the side, so it decides how the pad lies along the slope of the nose rather than how it presses against it. A mismatch between left and right here is spotted from the side and corrected with pad adjusting pliers. The splay angle is judged from above and controls front edge to back edge contact. The frontal angle is judged from the front and controls top to bottom contact, and guard arm work changes separation rather than pad rotation.
  136. The correct answer is A (correct the twist at the bridge or endpiece, not by bending a temple): once the twist has been traced to the front, the correction is made where the fault lives, which on a metal frame means the bridge or the endpiece worked cold with nylon jaws. Bending a temple down to stop the rock hides the fault and then fails the vertical alignment and temple parallelism checks. Loosening the eyewire screws unseats the lenses without touching the twist at all. Warming a metal front breaches the heat rule and risks the plating and the solder joints for no gain.
  137. The correct answer is D (from above, where they run parallel or diverge slightly and evenly): temple parallelism is judged by looking down on the open frame and comparing the straight portions of the two temples, which should be parallel or should diverge by the same small amount on each side. One temple in and one out is the classic cause of a frame that pulls sideways. The side view is used for tilt and for temple end shape rather than for parallelism. A front view is blocked by the eyewires themselves, and judging from below simply repeats the four point touch information.
  138. The correct answer is C (seat the screw fully, then trim and peen it or apply thread compound): when the screw is already the right diameter, thread, and length and the lens is seated, the remaining variable is retention, so the correct repair is to seat it fully and then secure it by peening the trimmed excess or applying a thread retaining compound. Fitting a longer screw leaves a protruding tail that catches skin and hair. Extra torque deforms the slot and the barrel thread and makes the next repair harder. Barrel replacement is a major repair that is not justified by a screw that simply backs out.
  139. The correct answer is A (warm the endpiece first, since cold zyl cracks or snaps when bent): zyl and other cellulose acetate materials have very little give at room temperature, so forcing a cold endpiece produces a crack, a whitened stress line, or a clean break. Warming through the airflow in short passes until the material flexes easily makes the same movement safe and repeatable. Hinge screw tension is not what resists the bend, so loosening it changes nothing. Removing the lens is sensible before heavy heating but does not itself make cold bending safe, and chilling the material makes it more brittle rather than more stable.
  140. The correct answer is B (at the endpieces, where the two sides must match, not hit a set amount): pantoscopic tilt is created where the temple meets the front, so unequal tilt is corrected at the endpieces. At the bench stage the requirement is symmetry between right and left, because a specific amount of tilt is a patient decision that belongs to the fitting task rather than to standard alignment. The bridge governs face form and eyewire height instead. Temple bend position sets where the frame sits on the ears, and guard arm height moves the pads without changing the tilt of the front.
  141. The correct answer is D (snipe nose pliers, because the jaws taper to a fine tip): snipe nose jaws narrow to a point, so they enter tight areas such as the space between a guard arm and an eyewire and grip a small component without fouling the parts around it. Angling pliers have a wide bracing jaw that cannot fit into that gap. Nylon jaw pliers protect a finish but are bulky and are chosen for eyewires and face form. Pad adjusting pliers do have a peg jaw, but the cupped opposing jaw is shaped to cradle a nose pad rather than to reach into a narrow opening.
  142. The correct answer is C (alignment is a symmetrical manufacturing reference judged off the face): standard bench alignment describes the undistorted, left to right symmetrical condition of a frame assessed on a flat surface, and nothing in it refers to a particular patient. A frame can look level and feel comfortable on one face while still being out of standard alignment, and a frame in perfect standard alignment may still need adjusting for a real face. Judging alignment on the face or by reported comfort confuses fitting with the reference state, and whatever the previous optician left behind is simply the frame's current condition.
  143. The correct answer is C (warm the bridge, then bring each eyewire back one side at a time): face form on a plastic front is changed by warming the bridge area until the material is supple, then setting each eyewire in turn so the two rearward curves can be matched against each other. Working one side at a time is what makes symmetry achievable. Cold bending acetate at the bridge or the endpieces risks cracking or a whitened stress line, whichever pliers are used. Warming the temples heats material that is not being moved and does nothing to restore curvature in the front.
  144. The correct answer is A (the lens is not fully seated, so warm the eyewire and reseat it): a correctly sized lens that was pushed in cold commonly leaves part of the bevel apex sitting above the groove, and the fix is to warm that eyewire and roll the unseated section into the groove with thumb pressure while supporting the frame from behind. An oversized lens behaves differently, springing out or bowing the whole eyewire, and that one does go back to the edger. Cold bending an acetate eyewire risks cracking it, and a lens standing proud of the groove is never acceptable as a finished result.
  145. The correct answer is B (levelling the front moves the pads, so the pad work is wasted): the pads hang from guard arms attached to the front, so any correction that levels or squares the front carries the pads with it and changes the bearing that was just set. Doing the pads last means the pad angles are set against a front that will not move again. Guard arms do work harden eventually, but they tolerate far more than a single adjustment. Pads cannot hold a front level, and a front remains fully adjustable no matter what has already been done to the pads.
  146. The correct answer is D (chain nose pliers, the usual gripping tool for metal frames): chain nose pliers have two flat jaws with no serrations, which is what allows them to hold a bridge component firmly without biting into the metal, and they are the general purpose gripping tool in a bench kit. Angling pliers pair a wide flat jaw with a narrow jaw for setting temple and endpiece angles. Pad adjusting pliers combine a cupped jaw and a peg to cradle a nose pad. Half padded pliers deliberately pair a bare jaw with a padded jaw, so their two jaws are not alike.
  147. The correct answer is A (solder joints at the bridge and endpieces can soften and fail): metal frames are assembled with soldered joints at exactly those points, and hot air applied to the bridge can take the joint above its working temperature, so the frame loosens or separates under normal handling afterwards. This is the reason the heat rule sends the frame warmer to plastic fronts only. Barrel threads are cut in solid metal and do not strip from a few seconds of hot air. Annealing a frame into a springback free state is not what happens, and a hinge pivot does not seize from brief warming.
  148. The correct answer is C (face form, tilt, and temple fold, because bridge work shifts them): the bridge sits between the two eyewires, so untwisting it moves both eyewires and both endpieces, which changes the horizontal curve of the front, the tilt at each endpiece, and the path each temple takes as it folds. Those checks are re run before the frame is called aligned. Treating four point touch as the only affected check ignores everything the bridge controls. Screw tightness is not disturbed by a bridge correction, and passing the rock test proves only that the four points lie in one plane.
  149. The correct answer is B (the splay angle, adjusted with pad adjusting pliers on the pad): the splay angle is judged from above and governs contact from the front edge of the pad to the rear edge, so a pad bearing only along its rear edge has a splay error. It is corrected by rotating the pad itself with pad adjusting pliers, whose cupped and peg jaws hold the pad without distorting the guard arm. The frontal angle governs top to bottom contact instead, and the vertical angle governs rotation seen from the side. Guard arm work with general pliers moves pad position rather than pad angle.
  150. The correct answer is D (eyewire screw tightness and lens seating, confirming no rattle and no gap): the closing checks of standard bench alignment confirm that each screw is present, correctly threaded, and snug without being over torqued, and that each lens bevel is fully seated in the groove all the way round with no gap at the eyewire joint and no rattle. Vertex distance and temple bend position belong to the patient fitting task, not to the bench reference state. Face form, tilt, temple fold, and spread all sit earlier in the working order and are re checked rather than used to close the task.
  151. The correct answer is C (level the front first, then set the temples, then the pads): the front establishes where the lenses sit relative to the eyes, and both the temples and the pads are then set to hold that position. Working pads first wastes the effort, because any later change to the front or the temple bend moves the frame on the nose and the pad setting has to be redone. Setting temple bends before the front is level fixes the frame in a crooked position and hides the real fault. Pads come last because they carry the weight only once everything above them is correct.
  152. The correct answer is A (a frame fault has been hidden inside a fitting bend, so neither can be judged): standard bench alignment is the reference condition, and until the frame is back in it there is no way to tell whether an observed fault belongs to the face or to the frame. Compensating for a high temple by twisting the front stacks one distortion on another, and the frame will look wrong again the moment anything else is touched. Folding into a case has nothing to do with the marking. Plating damage comes from serrated jaws and overworking rather than from levelling, and levelling the front is very much part of the scored task.
  153. The correct answer is D (the skin blanches white where the temple crosses the side of the head): blanching is blood being pushed out of the tissue, and it is the clearest visible sign of too much pressure in a station where nobody speaks. Temples folding flat is a bench alignment observation made off the face and says nothing about pressure on the head. A frame sliding forward on downgaze points to the opposite problem, too little grip. Lashes touching the lens is a vertex and tilt fault at the front of the frame, not a temple pressure sign.
  154. The correct answer is B (widen the separation of the pads or raise them on the arms): a nose is a wedge, so pads set wider apart come to rest lower on it, and moving the pads up the guard arms shortens the offset between pad and front so the front settles lower as well. Narrowing the pads or lowering them does the reverse and would lift a frame that is already too high. Changing the splay angle on one pad alters bearing rather than height, and doing it on one side only introduces a crooked fit. Shortening the temple bends draws the frame back toward the ears and tends to lift the front instead of lowering it.
  155. The correct answer is A (bring the two nose pads closer together so they bear higher): pads set closer together ride higher up the wedge of the nose and lift the whole front, and because the change is made at the pads the tilt already set at the endpieces is untouched. Opening the temple spread angle loosens the grip at the sides and pushes the front forward rather than up. Moving the temple bends further back lets the frame slide down, which is the opposite of what is wanted. Altering the frontal angle changes how the pad face meets the skin from top edge to bottom edge, not the height at which the frame rides.
  156. The correct answer is C (increase vertex distance at the pad arms so the front moves forward): lashes touching means the back surface of the lens is too close to the eye, and moving the front away along the same line restores clearance without shifting the frame up or down. Reducing pantoscopic tilt takes the lower rim away from the cheek but swings the top of the lens back toward the lashes, making the contact worse. Moving the temple bends forward lifts the front and pulls it back against the face. Raising the pads drops the frame but leaves the same short vertex, so the lashes still sweep the lens.
  157. The correct answer is B (at the crotch of the ear, where the ear top meets the head): that point is where the ear can carry the weight of the frame, and a bend starting exactly there gives support with no load in front of the ear. A bend placed forward of the ear presses on the temple bone, lifts the front, and leaves a red line the silent patient will never mention. A bend at the midpoint between the endpiece and the ear is further forward still and does the same thing more severely. A bend placed well behind the ear leaves the temple unsupported, so the frame slides down the nose.
  158. The correct answer is D (the temple end hooks too sharply, so contact is on the tip alone): the downward portion should follow the contour behind the ear with light even contact along its whole length toward the mastoid, so a mark at one point and nowhere else means the curve is tighter than the head. Relax the hook gradually and recheck the marking after each pass. A bend started too far forward marks the skin in front of the ear instead. A short temple would show the bend sitting ahead of the ear crotch rather than a mark at the tip. A wide spread angle shows as a gap at the side of the head.
  159. The correct answer is A (the two temple bend positions, since the lower side may sit further back): once the front is known to be square on the bench, an unequal drop on the face usually comes from one bend sitting further from the front than the other, which lets that side settle lower behind the ear. Compare both bend positions against the ears and equalize them. Base curve has no bearing on how the frame sits. Screw tension holds the lens in the eyewire and does not tilt the front. Temple fold is a bench check made with the frame closed and off the face, and it was already cleared.
  160. The correct answer is D (set the height of the frame first, then set even bearing on each pad): moving the pads up, down, in, or out to place the frame at the right height changes the part of the nose each pad lands on, so any bearing set beforehand is lost. Fix where the frame rides, then rotate each pad through its angles until the whole pad face lies flat on the skin. Setting bearing first means doing the same work twice. Working through the angles in a fixed order without first placing the frame has the same flaw, and moving both guard arms at once leaves no way to tell which change produced which result.
  161. The correct answer is B (the frontal angle, which controls contact from top edge to bottom edge): a mark from the upper edge alone means the pad face is tipped so that only its top is touching, and the frontal angle is the one that opens or closes that top to bottom relationship. The right side shows what a correct setting looks like, an even light blush across the whole pad face. The splay angle governs whether the front or the back edge of the pad digs in, which marks a vertical line rather than a horizontal one. The vertical angle rotates the pad so its long axis follows the nose and shows as a diagonal mark, while guard arm length changes pad position rather than the plane of contact.
  162. The correct answer is C (the temple ends are too straight to hold behind the ears): with the pads already separated correctly and bearing evenly, the load has to be held by the temples, and a downward portion that does not follow the contour behind the ear simply lets the frame run forward on downgaze. Shape the ends to follow the head toward the mastoid with even light contact. Unequal pantoscopic tilt shows as a crooked front, not as a slide. Loose eyewire screws let a lens rattle but do not move the frame on the face. A slightly long vertex distance changes effective power and lash clearance rather than grip.
  163. The correct answer is A (relax the curve of that temple end gradually and recheck the marking): a blanched band behind the ear is the temple end gripping the head harder than the tissue can take, and the fix is to open the curve a small amount at a time, replacing the frame and looking again after each pass. Opening the spread angle at the endpiece changes pressure at the side of the head in front of the bend, not behind the ear. Moving the bend forward puts pressure in front of the ear and lifts the front. Raising the pad changes the height of the front and does nothing to the shape of the temple behind the ear.
  164. The correct answer is B (open the spread angle at both endpieces by an equal amount): bowed temples and a pale line at each side of the head mean the temples are gripping too hard, and widening the angle where the temple leaves the front releases that grip while leaving the bends where they belong. Closing the spread angle squeezes harder and would deepen the marking. Tightening the hooks adds pressure behind the ear without touching the cause at the sides. Moving the bends back would let the frame slide and would not change how hard the straight portion presses on the head.
  165. The correct answer is D (close the spread angle slightly at both endpieces and reshape the ends): a visible gap at both sides with the front creeping forward is the classic loose fit, and narrowing the angle where the temples leave the front restores contact, after which the ends are shaped to follow the head behind the ear. Increasing pantoscopic tilt rotates the front and does nothing about grip at the sides. Widening the pads lowers the frame and makes the forward creep worse. Increasing vertex distance pushes the lenses further from the eyes and again leaves the temples standing away from the head.
  166. The correct answer is C (widening the separation between the two pads by an equal amount): the nose is narrower at the top and wider lower down, so pads moved apart come to rest further down that wedge and the frame drops even lower onto the cheeks. Bringing the pads closer together does the opposite and lifts the frame, which is what this fault needs. Lowering the pads on the guard arms lifts the front, which is what this fault needs. Equalizing the two bend positions is a sensible check that affects how level the frame sits rather than pushing it further down the nose.
  167. The correct answer is C (warm the bridge and reshape the crest so it bears higher): a moulded bridge is adjusted by softening the material in a frame warmer or hot air blower and reworking the crest and the pad surfaces so the load sits higher and narrower on the nose. Working the bridge cold risks cracking the zyl, which is why plastic is always warmed before a bend. Adhesive pads on the lens are a poor substitute for a proper bridge fit and interfere with the optics. Shortening the temples is a bench task that changes support behind the ear rather than height at the bridge.
  168. The correct answer is A (keep the asymmetry and be able to state why it was introduced): almost no face is symmetrical, so a frame fitted to a face with one ear higher must come off the face asymmetric, and the mark is earned by having made the change deliberately and being able to explain it. Restoring bench symmetry would undo the fit and tip the front again on the face. Splitting the difference leaves the frame crooked on the patient and still asymmetric on the bench, which is the worst of both. Raising both pads changes the height of the whole front and does nothing about a difference between the two ears.
  169. The correct answer is B (bend positions are set against ears the level front has already located): where each bend belongs depends on how far the front sits from the ear, and that distance is only fixed once the front is level and sitting where it should. Setting bends first locks the frame into whatever position it happened to be in, and any later change to the front moves both bends off the ear. Lenses in the eyewires do not prevent temple work. Cosmetic appearance is not the reason for the order. Work hardening happens from repeated bending in any sequence, so it does not explain why the front comes first.
  170. The correct answer is D (moving a plus lens closer to the eye reduces its effective power): the effective power of a plus lens rises as it moves away from the eye and falls as it moves in, so at +6.50 D a change of a few millimetres is an optical decision and not a styling one. This is why vertex distance matters at powers above about four dioptres in either sign. The reversed statement describes the behaviour of a minus lens instead. Segment height is measured from the lower rim to the lower lid margin and does not come from vertex distance. Axis is read with the lens flat against the lensometer stop, where vertex distance plays no part.
  171. The correct answer is A (the optical centre sits below the pupil centre by roughly five millimetres): the common working rule lowers the optical centre about one millimetre for every two degrees of pantoscopic tilt, so ten degrees of tilt calls for about five millimetres of drop and the line of sight then meets the back surface close to perpendicular. Placing the centre above the pupil doubles the error instead of cancelling it. Making the two coincide at every tilt ignores the tilt entirely and leaves unwanted vertical prism. A temporal shift addresses horizontal decentration, which is set by the monocular pupillary distance rather than by tilt.
  172. The correct answer is C (retroscopic tilt, corrected by adjusting both endpiece angles equally): when the bottom of the front tips away from the face instead of toward it, the frame is in retroscopic tilt, uncommon as a deliberate setting and common as a fault. The angle between temple and front is changed at each endpiece by the same amount until the lower rim sits nearer the cheek than the upper rim sits to the brow. Excess pantoscopic tilt is the opposite appearance and would not be fixed at the pads anyway. Face form is the horizontal wrap of the front, judged from above. Vertex distance describes how far the lens sits from the cornea, not how the front is rotated.
  173. The correct answer is D (the top rim position against the brows and the pupils within the lenses): height is read by comparing the top of the front with the brow line and by seeing where each pupil falls vertically inside its lens, both judged straight on at eye level. Temple gaps at the sides tell you about grip and spread angle rather than height. Temple fold is a bench check made off the face entirely. The endpiece angle relates to spread and to vertex distance, not to how high the front rides on the nose.
  174. The correct answer is B (the splay angle, changing one angle at a time and rechecking): the splay angle governs contact from the front edge of the pad to the back edge, so a mark at the back edge alone means the pad is turned too far in at the rear. Working one angle at a time is the rule, because changing two together leaves no way to know which one produced the improvement. The frontal angle opens or closes the top to bottom relationship and would show as a mark along the upper or lower edge. The vertical angle sets how the long axis of the pad lies on the nose and produces a diagonal mark when it is wrong.
  175. The correct answer is B (about 12 to 14 mm, judged by the lashes clearing the lens): a working vertex distance of roughly twelve to fourteen millimetres is the usual target on a metal frame, and with no feedback available the visible test is whether the lashes sweep the back surface when the patient blinks. Five to seven millimetres is far too short and guarantees lash contact. Twenty to twenty four millimetres pushes the lenses out where plus power gains effect and the frame loses support. Horizontal centring of the pupils is a decentration check against the monocular pupillary distance and says nothing about how far the lens sits from the eye.
  176. The correct answer is A (pads are set last because everything above them moves where they bear): the front and the temples decide where the frame sits, and every change to either of them lands the pads on a different part of the nose. Leaving the pads until the end means the bearing is set once, against a position that will not move again. Deliberately setting the pads twice wastes minutes in a station that gives only thirty for three tasks. Pad angles are adjusted on the face as a matter of course. Levelling with the pad arms alone hides a front fault instead of correcting it.
  177. The correct answer is C (remove the twist at the bridge so both lens planes match): a difference between the two lens planes is a front twist, it is corrected at the bridge, and it must be gone before fitting because otherwise there is no way to tell whether a crooked appearance on the face belongs to the frame or to the patient. Compensating on the face buries a frame fault inside a fitting bend. Loosening eyewire screws lets the lenses move in the groove and does not straighten a twisted front. Symmetrical pad angles are a separate check and cannot hide a rotated lens plane.
  178. The correct answer is D (ask the examiner to look down toward the floor and then nod): downgaze and a nod load the frame forward and show at once whether it creeps down the nose, which is the fault a silent patient would otherwise never report. Holding still with the eyes closed removes the movement that reveals the problem and also removes the blink that shows lash contact. Turning the head sideways tests very little, since the ears and nose carry the frame in the same way through that movement. Raising the brows moves the skin under the pads but says nothing about forward slippage.
  179. The correct answer is A (the temple spread angle at the endpieces and the pad arm position): closing the angle where the temple leaves the front draws the front toward the face, and setting the pad arms so the pads sit further forward on the nose lets the front come in as well, so those two controls govern vertex distance on a metal frame. The temple bend position decides how far back the frame is held and how high it rides. Face form is horizontal wrap and temple fold is a bench check made off the face. Screw tension holds the lens, and the vertical angle only rotates the pad on the skin.
  180. The correct answer is B (crooked front, then the temple bend, then the pad angle last): the order is front, temples, pads, and each of these three faults belongs to one of those stages. Straightening the front changes where both ears sit relative to the frame, so the bend has to be judged afterwards, and moving the bend changes how the frame rests on the nose, so the pad face is set at the end. Starting with the pad wastes the one setting that depends on everything else. Doing the bend before the front locks the frame into a crooked position, and doing the pad before the bend means measuring bearing against a position that is about to move.
  181. The correct answer is D (move the bend back so it starts at the top of the ear): a bend sitting forward of the ear crotch presses on the head in front of the ear and levers the front upward, and both signs disappear once the bend is placed where the ear can actually carry the weight. Lowering the pads on the arms would in fact lift the front higher still, and would leave the pressure point in front of the ear untouched. Increasing pantoscopic tilt rotates the front without moving the bend. Tightening the hook adds grip behind the ear while the bend is still in the wrong place, so the pressure in front of the ear remains.
  182. The correct answer is C (adjustable pads make the metal frame the better choice for this nose): each pad can be set independently through its three angles, so a nose that is fuller on one side can be given different bearing on each side without touching the front. Reshaping a moulded plastic bridge to two different profiles means warming and reworking the material and is far less precise, so the ease of bending warmed zyl does not make it the better option here. Bridge shape and fitting behaviour differ a great deal between the two constructions. Well set pads bear evenly over their whole face and do not mark an asymmetric nose.
  183. The correct answer is B (levelness is judged straight on at eye level, not from above): looking down foreshortens the front and hides a vertical difference between the two sides, so the candidate must sit or stand so the eyes are level with the frame, then compare the top rim against the brows and the pupils within the lenses. A view from above is useful for face form and for pad splay, not for level. Whether the patient sits or stands does not change the geometry as long as the observer is at eye level. Four point touch tests the frame off the face and cannot show how it sits on this particular head.
  184. The correct answer is A (open the pads further apart so each one covers more skin): pads already set too wide are the reason the frame is running down the wedge of the nose, and separating them further lets it drop faster no matter how much skin each pad covers. Bringing the pads closer together and correcting the frontal angle puts the full pad face back on the nose and restores grip. Shaping the temple ends to follow the head gives the support that almost straight ends cannot. Confirming the bend position is a reasonable check as well, because a bend set too far back also allows a frame to slide.
  185. The correct answer is C (change the splay on one pad, recheck bearing, then do the other): a mark on the front edge with the back edge standing clear is a splay fault, and the discipline is one angle on one pad at a time, with the frame replaced and the bearing looked at again before anything else moves. Changing two angles or both pads at once removes any way of telling which change worked. Resetting the height is unnecessary when height and level are already correct and would only undo good work. The frontal angle governs top to bottom contact and the vertical angle governs how the pad lies along the nose, so neither addresses a front edge mark.
  186. The correct answer is D (the left side is too tight and the right shows acceptable contact): blanching is blood driven out of the tissue, and slow return of colour confirms real pressure, so the left temple end is gripping harder than the skin can tolerate and must be relaxed. A faint pink line that fades immediately is the normal trace of light even contact and needs nothing done to it. Treating both sides as normal leaves a genuine pressure point on a patient who cannot report it. Relaxing both ends would loosen a side that is already correct and invite the frame to slide.
  187. The correct answer is A (a twist in the front, leaving the two lens planes out of line): when one lens plane is rotated relative to the other, that side sits closer to the face, and the fault would have been caught by sighting along the front on the bench before fitting began. It is corrected at the bridge, not by bending a temple. Screw tension holds a lens in its groove and does not rotate the front. Unequal splay would show as uneven pad marks, which the stem rules out. A longer temple on one side changes where the bend falls at the ear, and both bends are stated to be equal.
  188. The correct answer is B (the pupils, because optical centre height is set to the eyes): where the lenses sit relative to the pupils decides whether the patient looks through the intended part of each lens, so unequal pupil height inside the lenses is corrected even when the brows make the frame look level. Brow line is a useful first glance and a cosmetic guide, but brows are often uneven and are not an optical reference. The ears carry the frame and set the bend positions, yet they do not decide height within the lens. Standard bench alignment is the starting condition, not the finishing target once the frame is on a real face.
  189. The correct answer is C (level first, because tilt is judged against a front that is already square): the accepted habit is horizontal before vertical, since a front that is still crooked gives two different apparent tilts on the two sides and there is no reliable reference to set the angle against. Once the front is square, equal tilt can be set at the two endpieces and confirmed from the side. Setting tilt first simply means resetting it after the front is straightened. Tilt does not decide horizontal level, and it can certainly be adjusted with the frame on the face, which is exactly where it is judged.
  190. The correct answer is A (increase the pantoscopic tilt slightly at both endpieces of the front): tilting the bottom of the front toward the cheeks swings the upper part of each lens away from the brow and the upper lashes, which is the usual remedy once vertex distance is already correct. Making the front more vertical brings the top of the lens back toward the lashes and worsens the contact. Lowering the pads on the arms lifts the whole front rather than dropping it, so the lashes meet a different part of the same lens without gaining any clearance. Closing the spread angle pulls the front closer to the face and shortens vertex distance, which guarantees more lash contact.
  191. The correct answer is A (raise both pads on the guard arms by an equal amount): the front hangs at a fixed offset below the pads, so moving the pads up the arms shortens that offset and lets the whole front settle lower on the face. Widening the separation is the other control that lowers a frame, since the pads then rest further down the wedge of the nose. Moving the pads down the arms lengthens the offset and lifts a frame that is already too high. Changing the frontal angle alters how the pad meets the nose rather than the height, and rotating about the vertical angle adjusts the pad along the slope without raising or lowering the front.
  192. The correct answer is B (a fault, since contact in front of the ear is not wanted): the straight portion of the temple should clear the head with a small even gap all the way from the endpiece to the ear, so contact anywhere along that run puts pressure on the temple bone where no support is intended. The left side shows what is wanted. Treating the contact as correct would leave a pressure point this patient will never mention. A pad fault would show first as uneven pad marks or as a front pushed off centre. A bend set too far back allows sliding and does not press the straight portion against the head.
  193. The correct answer is C (at the endpieces or the bridge, where the front angle is set): a front sitting lower on one side with both bends correctly placed is a front problem, and the angle between temple and front at the endpiece, or a twist at the bridge, is where it is put right. Adding curve to one temple end drags that side up or down by changing support behind the ear and hides the real fault. Raising one pad tips the front but leaves the frame bearing unevenly on the nose. Eyewire screws hold the lens and have no effect on how level the front sits.
  194. The correct answer is D (bench alignment is the reference state a patient fit starts from): the symmetrical undistorted condition is what the manufacturer intended, and until a frame is in it there is no way to know whether a fault seen on the face belongs to the face or to the frame. That is why the same candidate is asked to demonstrate both skills in one section. A fitted frame usually comes off the face asymmetric, because faces are asymmetric, so ending in bench alignment is not the goal of a fit. The two tasks share a starting point rather than being unrelated, and alignment is checked on every frame whether it is visibly damaged or not.
  195. The correct answer is A (look from below for daylight between the pad face and the skin): sighting up under the bridge shows immediately whether each pad is lying flat or touching on one edge, because any gap lets light through, and it works without waiting for a mark to form. Checking the top rim against the brows tells you about level and height, not about how the pad face meets the nose. Comparing the wrap of the two eyewires from above is a face form check on the front. Comparing vertex distance from each side is about how far the lenses sit from the eyes and says nothing about pad contact.
  196. The correct answer is C (too much pantoscopic tilt, reduced at both endpiece angles): a lower rim resting on the cheek with the upper rim standing off the brow is an excess of tilt, and the angle between temple and front at each endpiece is where the amount is set. The rim lifting when the patient smiles confirms the cheek is carrying the frame, which it should never do. Reducing tilt further is the fix for a front that is too vertical, not for this one. Face form is horizontal wrap seen from above. A short vertex shows as lash contact rather than a rim on the cheek, and lowering the pads would press the rim harder onto the cheek.
  197. The correct answer is B (warm the temple in the frame warmer until the zyl is supple): cellulose acetate has to be softened before it will take a new curve, and bending it cold splits the material or cracks it around the wire core. Warm in short passes and test pliability with the fingers rather than trusting a timer. Chilling the material makes it more brittle and more likely to break. Loosening the hinge screw lets the temple swing but does nothing about the stiffness of the material at the point being bent. Lenses are not removed to reshape a temple end, though the heat should be kept away from them.
  198. The correct answer is D (replace the frame and recheck level, temples, and pads in order): every change moves something else, so the whole observation sequence is run again from the top, looking at level against the brows and pupils, then gaps and marks at the temples, then bearing at the pads. Restoring full bench symmetry would undo a fit deliberately made to an asymmetric face. Checking the pads alone misses faults created higher up by the same work. Asking about comfort is exactly what this station forbids, and a candidate who asks has failed to show the observational skill being tested.
  199. The correct answer is C (the bend position sets that side's height, not the amount of curve): moving a bend closer to the front lifts that side and moving it further back drops it, so with a square front the fix is to compare the two bend positions against the ears and equalize them. Adding curve at the same position simply grips the head harder and creates a pressure point behind the ear without lifting anything. Bridge work is the answer when the front is twisted, and here the front has already been cleared on the bench. Warming applies to plastic frames and this one is metal, and plating damage comes from poor tool choice and repeated bending rather than from a single extra bend.
  200. The correct answer is B (pupils level within the lenses and no slip on downgaze): position is confirmed by where the eyes sit inside the lenses viewed straight on at eye level, and support is confirmed by the frame staying put when the head drops, which are the two things a silent patient cannot tell you. Four point touch and temple fold are bench checks made off the face and say nothing about this head. Matching spread angles measured off the face ignore the asymmetry a real face usually demands. Lashes touching the lens is a fault to be removed, and a firm grip at the temple ends describes pressure rather than correct support.
  201. The correct answer is C (that spot blisters and distorts while the rest stays too stiff): heat has to be spread across the whole eyewire, so the front is kept moving through the airflow in short passes rather than parked in the stream. A stationary front takes all the energy at one point, which drives that area past its softening temperature and leaves bubbling, whitening, and permanent distortion while the far side is still rigid. Zyl has no metal core that expands, and warming does not permanently widen a bevel groove. Uneven cooling is a later concern, not the immediate risk of parking the frame in the heat.
  202. The correct answer is A (flex the eyewire gently by hand and judge the pliability directly): a properly warmed zyl front feels supple and gives under light finger pressure, and that feel is the only reliable readiness test. Frame warmers vary in output and frames vary in thickness and material, so a fixed count of seconds says nothing about this frame on this machine. A change in surface gloss is already damage rather than a readiness cue, because loss of gloss means the material has gone past its safe working temperature. Touching the eyewire to the wrist reads surface temperature at one point and misses whether the eyewire has softened through its bulk.
  203. The correct answer is D (a chipped lens edge and a stressed or cracked frame eyewire): cold acetate has almost no give, so the force needed to stretch the eyewire over the bevel is transferred into the lens edge and into the plastic itself. Chipping shows at the corner that was forced last, and the eyewire either cracks outright or holds a stress line that fails days later in the patient's hands. Whitening, a sagging bridge, coating crazing, and photochromic clouding are all overheating faults, so they appear when too much heat is used rather than too little. A rattling lens in a worn groove is an edging fault and has nothing to do with frame temperature.
  204. The correct answer is B (which lens belongs to which eye, and its correct axis orientation): a cylindrical lens put into the wrong eyewire or seated upside down places the axis nowhere near the prescribed meridian, and the error is invisible once the lens is in and the frame looks tidy. Identifying right from left and up from down by the lay out dots, the bevel, and the work order takes seconds and saves a full removal and reinsertion cycle. Centre and edge thickness normally differ between a right and left lens and prove nothing about which eye a lens serves. Base curve is a lens property that does not identify an eye, and a plastic eyewire is opened by heat rather than by backing off a screw.
  205. The correct answer is D (re read the cylinder axis of each lens on the lensometer): rolling or twisting a lens while forcing the last edge into the groove rotates it in the eyewire, and at a cylinder power of 1.75 D the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows only about 2 degrees of axis error. Nothing about the frame's appearance reveals a rotated axis, so a lensometer reading is the only check that finds it. Base curve and centre thickness are properties fixed at the lab and cannot change while a lens is being seated. Vertex distance is a fitting measurement taken on the face and says nothing about how the lens sits in the eyewire.
  206. The correct answer is A (return the lens to the edger to be reduced in size): a lens that enters only with visible frame bowing is oversized, and it holds the eyewire under permanent tension, pulling the front out of standard alignment and building stress into both parts. Reducing the lens at the edger is the only real fix and costs far less than a cracked front. More heat does not make a lens smaller, and forcing it in leaves the frame distorted and the lens loaded. Filing the groove destroys the bevel seat and ruins the frame, and a screw cannot hold down a lens the eyewire cannot contain.
  207. The correct answer is C (the lens is undersized, so it must be recut from a new blank): a lens that spins and rattles is smaller than the eyewire holding it, so it cannot hold its axis and it will fall out in service. Nothing on the bench adds material back to a lens edge, so a correctly sized lens has to be cut. Cooling and reseating changes nothing, because the size mismatch is still there at room temperature. Regrinding the bevel removes yet more material, and trying to shrink an eyewire around an undersized lens simply distorts the front away from standard alignment.
  208. The correct answer is B (heat crazes the coating into a fine crackle that is permanent): an anti reflective stack is a set of thin hard layers on a softer substrate, and heat makes the substrate expand faster than the coating so the coating fractures into a visible crackle pattern. No cleaning or polishing removes crazing, so the lens becomes scrap and the job returns to the lab. The working rule is to warm the frame and keep the lenses well clear of the heat source until the moment they are seated. Thermal expansion of a lens is far too small to change the fit, and neither bevel softening nor dust attraction describes what heat does to a coated lens.
  209. The correct answer is B (the polarized film can delaminate and the photochromic layer can cloud): a polarized lens is a laminate with a stretched film bonded between layers, and heat softens that bond so the film lifts, bubbles, or separates at the edge. Photochromic performance depends on a dye layer that heat can haze or leave permanently milky, which ruins both the appearance and the darkening response. Sitting deep inside the lens gives neither material any protection, which is why the frame is warmed while the lenses stay out of the airflow. Heat does not simply darken these lenses, and its effects are certainly not confined to the edge polish.
  210. The correct answer is D (insertion disturbs the frame, so standard bench alignment must be restored): heating and stretching an eyewire pulls the front out of square, so four point touch, face form, pantoscopic symmetry, and temple fold all have to be rechecked and corrected once the lenses are in. A crooked frame loses the mark even when both lenses are seated perfectly and read on axis. Lens power and axis do not drift as acetate cools, so repeating the lensometer reading adds nothing. Vertex distance belongs to the patient adjustment rather than the bench, and screw tension is not what warming an eyewire disturbs.
  211. The correct answer is A (roll the eyewire around the lens edge while supporting from behind): the frame is the part that has been softened, so the frame is the part that should move, stretched over the bevel a little at a time while the other hand keeps the lens square and backed up. Working this way puts almost no turning force on the lens, and that is what protects the axis. Twisting the lens is exactly what rotates the cylinder and chips the edge. A screwdriver used as a lever gouges the groove and marks the lens, and pressing on the lens face drives it out of plane instead of into the groove.
  212. The correct answer is C (the lens is not fully seated and must be reheated and reseated): a partly seated lens is held only by friction along the sections that did go in, so it can spring out under normal handling and it cannot be trusted to hold axis. The fix is to warm that eyewire again and roll the section over the bevel until the apex is buried in the groove around the whole circumference. An undersized lens rattles and spins rather than standing proud along one specific stretch of edge. A bevel cut too shallow or a groove closed by overheating would show a poor fit right around the lens, not one local failure.
  213. The correct answer is A (coloured stress patterns visible at the edge under crossed polarizers): a lens squeezed by an eyewire that is too small becomes birefringent under load, and the strain shows as coloured fringes concentrated where the pressure is highest, usually at the corners. Strain like that means the lens is fighting the frame, so it will craze, chip, or crack in service and the lens must be reduced or recut before dispensing. A yellow cast is a material or tint characteristic and carries no information about load. Central haze and fine tooling circles are surfacing and finishing faults that were present before the lens ever met the frame.
  214. The correct answer is D (warm the eyewire, then push the lens out from behind by thumb): warming softens the front so the eyewire will stretch back over the bevel, and even thumb pressure applied from the rear at the upper temporal corner walks the lens forward into the waiting hand without concentrating force anywhere. Removing a lens cold is the fastest way to crack a front or chip an edge. Chilling acetate makes it more brittle rather than more willing to release the lens. Levering with a shim or a blade gouges the groove and marks the lens, and gripping the lens face with pliers endangers the surface and any coating.
  215. The correct answer is B (slide a lens removal tool under the cord and roll it off): the cord is the retaining element, so easing it out of the lens groove and over the edge frees the lens without stressing the chassis or the lens, and the lens then lifts out of the upper chassis groove. The cord is reused if it is sound and replaced if it has stretched. Heating a metal chassis risks the plating and the soldered joints and does nothing useful to nylon tension. Cutting the cord as a first move throws away a serviceable part, and dismantling the endpiece screw takes the frame apart for no reason.
  216. The correct answer is C (the cylinder axis rotates away from the meridian the prescription specifies): a drilled mount is held only by the two hole positions and the clamping force, so any turn of the lens before final tightening carries straight through to the axis the patient looks through. At a cylinder power above 1.50 D the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows only about 2 degrees, so even a small turn can put the job out of tolerance. Correct practice is to hold the lens square, snug the nut, and confirm the axis on the lensometer afterwards. Base curve is fixed at the lab and cannot shift, while hole wear and bushing compression are durability concerns rather than the immediate optical error.
  217. The correct answer is C (stress cracks radiate outward from the drilled hole through the lens): a drilled hole is a stress concentration, and clamping force past snug loads that small area until the material splits, usually as a short crack running from the hole toward the edge. Cracks like that often appear hours or days later, so the patient discovers them rather than the dispenser. Tightening only until the hardware holds the lens without play, with a bushing to spread the load, is what prevents it. Front and temple alignment is not what over torquing a mount changes, lens power is fixed at surfacing, and a stripped thread is a lesser and less common outcome than a cracked lens.
  218. The correct answer is A (the groove is too shallow to retain the cord under flex): the cord sits inside the edge groove and holds the lens against the chassis, so groove depth is what stops the cord riding up and over the lens edge. A groove cut well short of normal depth gives the cord almost nothing to bite into, and any flex of the frame releases the lens. The lens goes back to be regrooved to the correct depth rather than being held in with a tighter cord. A stretched cord shows as slack that cannot be taken up anywhere, an oversized lens will not seat in the chassis at all, and a worn chassis groove would have affected the original lens as well.
  219. The correct answer is B (fit a new cord of the correct length and reseat the lens): nylon cord is a consumable that stretches with age, heat, and repeated lens changes, and once stretched it cannot apply even tension anywhere around the edge. Fresh cord is inexpensive, quick to fit, and restores the retention the mount was designed around. A knot creates a hard lump that stands proud of the groove, puts a point load on the lens edge, and works loose again. Cutting the lens groove deeper weakens the lens to compensate for a worn part, and heating a metal chassis endangers the plating and solder joints while doing nothing about the slack.
  220. The correct answer is D (trim the excess close to the barrel and peen or lock it): a screw of correct diameter and thread but excess length is finished by cutting the protruding tail close to the barrel, after which the end is either peened slightly or treated with a retaining compound so it cannot back out. Trimming is not optional, because the compound does nothing about the shank standing proud. Either method keeps the eyewire closed and the lens secure between visits. A protruding tail scratches the patient's face and catches on cloths and cases, so leaving it is not acceptable. Backing the screw out loosens the eyewire and lets the lens rotate, and grinding the barrel destroys frame material to solve a screw length problem.
  221. The correct answer is A (the thread pitch or diameter does not match the hinge barrel): a screw that starts freely and then jams is cutting its own path against threads of a different pitch or size, which wrecks the barrel and can leave the frame unrepairable without a new hinge. A correct screw turns smoothly the whole way down and stops when the head meets the barrel. A screw that is only too long runs all the way in freely and shows excess below the barrel, which is a different picture entirely. Worn barrel threads let a screw spin loosely rather than bind, and a mismatched blade damages the slot without changing how the thread turns.
  222. The correct answer is B (match hinge type and length, then bench align both temples together): a replacement temple has to use the same hinge pattern and screw size as the frame, and it has to be close enough in length that the bend can sit at the crotch of the ear. Once fitted, both temples are checked together for equal spread, equal fold, and four point touch, because a mismatched pair shows immediately as a frame that pulls to one side. Leaving the original side untouched guarantees an asymmetric pair. Cutting down a sound temple destroys a good component to match a generic one, and handing the frame over expecting the patient to report a fault replaces the optician's own assessment.
  223. The correct answer is C (titanium will not take conventional solder and needs specialist laser welding): titanium forms an oxide layer instantly at soldering temperature, so ordinary silver solder and flux cannot bond to it and any joint made that way fails under normal wear. Frames of this material go to a laboratory with laser welding equipment, or the patient is advised honestly that replacement is the realistic option. Heavier fluxing does not change the metallurgy. It is not true that every metal frame can be soldered, and plating damage, while a real risk on plated base metal frames, is not the reason a titanium bridge cannot be soldered.
  224. The correct answer is A (advise that the front is beyond repair and discuss replacement options): a crack running right through the eyewire wall in aged, whitened acetate means the material has lost its plasticisers and turned brittle, so the eyewire can no longer hold a lens under the tension seating requires. Saying so plainly and pricing a replacement is honest practice and avoids handing back eyewear that fails again within weeks. Adhesive in a bevel groove interferes with lens seating and restores no strength across a load bearing corner. Heat does not close a crack in embrittled acetate, and cutting the lens smaller simply produces a lens that will not stay in.
  225. The correct answer is D (book a follow up, since a frame drifts out of adjustment with wear): daily handling, heat, and one handed removal move a frame away from the fit it left with, and a drifted fit changes optical centre height and vertex distance as well as comfort. Scheduling the return at dispensing makes it routine rather than something the patient has to decide to ask for. Continuing care is part of the optician's obligation and not an extra service, so telling the patient to stay away until the prescription changes abandons the fit. Waiting for a lens to fall out means the damage has already happened, and home bending is how fronts get twisted and temples get snapped.
  226. The correct answer is D (remove with two hands, and clean with lens solution and cloth): one handed removal pulls the front sideways every time and is the most common reason a frame that was symmetrical at dispensing comes back twisted within a month. Dry wiping on clothing drags trapped grit across the surface and is what puts fine scratches into a coated lens, so a proper solution and a clean lens cloth are used instead. Tissue is still a dry abrasive no matter which hand removed the frame. Pulling on the bridge loads a soldered or moulded joint, hot water can craze a coating, and removing by a single temple simply repeats the original fault.
  227. The correct answer is B (some peripheral blur is normal, so point the nose at the target): every progressive carries unwanted cylinder in the lower outer zones, and a new wearer notices it as softness or movement at the sides until the habit of turning the head rather than the eyes takes over. Explaining this at dispensing and asking for consistent full time wear is what makes adaptation happen. Treating all peripheral blur as a measurement error alarms the patient about a normal optical property of the design. Part time wear for near tasks only delays adaptation, and letting the frame ride lower on the nose drops the corridor so the patient looks through the wrong part of the lens.
  228. The correct answer is C (the front was twisted while an eyewire was stretched over a lens): four point touch tests whether the front lies flat and square, and a rock at diagonal corners means the front is out of plane. Insertion routinely introduces exactly this fault, because one eyewire is softened and pulled around a lens while the rest of the front is held. The twist is corrected at the bridge or endpiece and the test is repeated before the frame goes anywhere near a patient. Swapped lenses and a rotated axis are real insertion errors, but they are found on the lensometer and do not make a front rock, and finished lenses do not distort in a frame warmer.
  229. The correct answer is A (restore the frame fit, then recheck the fitting cross against the pupil): a progressive corridor is positioned relative to the pupil, so a frame that has dropped on the nose carries the whole corridor down and pushes the reading zone below where the patient looks. Readjusting the pads, temple bends, and pantoscopic tilt puts the corridor back where it was measured, and confirming the cross against the pupil proves the fix worked. Ordering remade lenses spends the patient's money on a fitting problem that adjustment solves. Extra wearing time cannot compensate for a corridor sitting in the wrong place, and refraction sits with the optometrist and is not indicated by a frame that has slipped.
  230. The correct answer is B (the lenses were seated into the opposite eyewires from the work order): identical sphere and cylinder powers with the two axis values exchanged between eyes is the signature of a right and left swap rather than a rotation, since a rotation moves one axis independently and rarely lands exactly on the other eye's figure. At 0.75 D of cylinder the ANSI Z80.1 standard used by NACOR allows about 5 degrees, and each lens here sits 10 degrees from its own specification, so the pair fails. Both lenses turning the same amount in the same direction is not what stretching two separate eyewires does. A drum left off zero shifts both readings the same way rather than exchanging them, and a twisted front does not swap axis values between eyes.
  231. The correct answer is C (refuse, because prolonged heat can damage the photochromic layer and distort the frame): extra time in the warmer risks both the lens treatment and the acetate itself, and neither problem can be undone. Warm the frame the normal amount and work more efficiently instead. Photochromic lenses are not more heat tolerant than clear ones. Chilling a lens before insertion makes it more brittle, not safer. Inserting cold is what chips lenses and cracks eyewires.
  232. The correct answer is D (let the frame cool completely, then reassess it for permanent distortion and finish damage): a floppy, shiny, spread front has been overheated, and you cannot judge what damage is permanent until it has cooled and stiffened again. Working on it hot will lock the distortion in. Quenching acetate under cold water stresses the material and can craze a lens coating. Correcting bridge width later does not address the surface damage already done.
  233. The correct answer is A (the lenses may still differ in edge shape, so match each to its own eyewire): each lens is edged to its own eyewire, and the two sides of a frame are rarely a perfect mirror match, so a lens that is optically identical can still fit only one side properly. Optical sameness does not make the shapes interchangeable. Swapping them deliberately invites a loose or overtight fit. Nothing here justifies returning the job.
  234. The correct answer is B (use the hot air blower, keeping the frame moving so the heat spreads evenly): moving the frame through the air stream is what prevents a hot spot, and even warmth is what makes an eyewire stretch rather than tear. Speed is not the goal, evenness is. Holding a frame still in any heat source concentrates it in one place. Stacking two heat sources one after the other only raises the risk of distortion.
  235. The correct answer is D (flex the eyewire gently and feel whether it yields to light pressure): pliability is the property you actually need, so testing it directly is the only reliable check. A front that yields with no resistance at all has been overheated rather than warmed. Surface temperature is a poor guide because the outside warms before the body of the material does. A fixed time cannot account for frame thickness, material, or the warmer's output. Attempting a lens without testing is how eyewires split.
  236. The correct answer is C (treat the lens as damaged, because a chip in the groove can spread under stress): an edge chip is a stress concentration, and the eyewire grips exactly there, so the crack can travel later while the patient is wearing the pair. Being invisible does not make it stable. Recording it without acting still sends out a compromised lens. Filing the edge alters the shape the eyewire was cut for and does not remove the underlying flaw.
  237. The correct answer is A (the lens rotated during seating, so reseat it and read the axis again): the same lens on the same instrument read correctly minutes earlier, so the change came from the handling in between, and twisting a lens into an eyewire is exactly what rotates an axis. A lab error would have shown on the first reading. Instruments do not drift by four degrees between two readings. Treating a real shift as normal variation ships a wrong job.
  238. The correct answer is D (explain that this is normal early adaptation and arrange a follow up check): learning to point the nose at the target is exactly what a new progressive wearer must get used to, and clear distance vision suggests the fitting is sound. Bringing the patient in lets you verify the heights rather than guess. Abandoning the design after two days is premature. Refusing to look at it for six weeks abandons the patient. Nothing here suggests the prescription itself is wrong.
  239. The correct answer is B (treat this as a fitting fault and arrange remake at the correct height): a fitting cross sitting four millimetres low in both eyes pushes the whole corridor down, which is a measurable error rather than an adaptation problem, and three weeks is long enough to rule out simple unfamiliarity. Raising the frame far enough to compensate would wreck the fit and the vertex distance. The addition is not at fault when the corridor is misplaced.
  240. The correct answer is C (advise rinsing under running water, then a mild soap and a microfibre cloth): rinsing lifts grit away before anything touches the surface, which is what actually prevents scratching, and mild soap is safe on modern coatings. Household glass cleaners contain solvents that attack coatings, and paper towel is abrasive. Dry wiping grinds any grit straight into the lens. Hot water can damage coatings and loosen a frame.
  241. The correct answer is A (fit a correctly sized screw, or trim and dress the protruding end smooth): a projecting shank sits against the patient's face and can scratch skin, so it must be removed either by using the right screw or by cutting and smoothing what is there. Leaving a sharp end in contact with the patient is a safety fault, not a cosmetic one. Backing a screw out leaves it insecure. A wider thread will not match the barrel and will strip it.
  242. The correct answer is D (offer a matched generic temple pair, or discuss replacing the frame): fitting both sides from the same generic set keeps the pair symmetrical in length, colour, and style, and being honest about the frame option lets the patient choose. A single mismatched temple leaves the frame visibly odd and often the wrong length. A soldered temple breaks again at the same point under normal flexing. Declining to help when workable options exist fails the patient.
  243. The correct answer is C (return the frame to standard bench alignment, because insertion disturbs it): stretching an eyewire around a lens pulls the front out of true, so a frame that was aligned before insertion will not be aligned after it, and the alignment is part of a finished job. Checking the axes alone leaves the mechanical faults in place. Reheating a completed frame risks distortion for no benefit.
  244. The correct answer is B (show two handed removal, because one handed removal twists the frame): pulling from one side loads the whole front through one hinge, which is what slowly bends a frame out of alignment and loosens the screws. Taking both temples together keeps the load even. Pulling on the bridge stresses the weakest point of many frames. Parking spectacles on the head spreads the temples and drops the frame down the nose in wear.
  245. The correct answer is A (look for stress marks, waves, or distortion around the lens edge): an eyewire that is too tight, or a lens forced in cold, leaves visible strain near the rim, and that is the fault this particular inspection is designed to catch. Engravings identify a progressive design rather than reveal insertion damage. A segment height is measured against the groove, not judged by eye through the lens. Lensometer dots are wiped off, not inspected.
  246. The correct answer is C (check the depth of the groove cut into the lens edge by the laboratory): with the cord sound, the tension right, the alignment right, and the size right, the remaining variable is the groove itself, and one cut too shallow simply cannot retain the cord. Over tightening a cord distorts the frame and does not deepen a groove. Reseating puts the same shallow groove back on the same cord. A heavier cord will not sit in a groove cut too shallow to hold it.
  247. The correct answer is D (the mounting was overtightened, concentrating stress at the drill hole): a crack appearing right after firm tightening and radiating from the hole points at the clamping force itself, because a drilled hole is already the weakest point in the lens. Rimless hardware is tightened only until the lens is secure. A badly placed hole or an unsuitable material would have been a problem before this point. A loose nut lets a lens rotate rather than crack it.
  248. The correct answer is B (advise against it, because heat in a parked car can distort the frame): a closed car in sun reaches temperatures well above anything a frame is built for, and that is enough to soften a front, loosen an adjustment, and damage a coating. Which way up the lenses sit makes no difference to the heat. The risk applies to the frame and the coatings whatever the lens treatment. Metal frames are not immune, since the heat also attacks the finish and the adjustment.
  249. The correct answer is C (invite the patient back for a check and readjustment after a short period): frames settle and adjustments move with normal wear, so a planned early review catches a slipping fit before it becomes a complaint and confirms the patient is getting on with the lenses. Waiting for a problem leaves small faults to become large ones. The next eye examination is a separate matter and is far too distant. Telling a patient never to return abandons continuing care.
  250. The correct answer is A (warm the frame again and push the lens out evenly from behind): even thumb pressure applied to the back surface of a warm, pliable eyewire releases the lens forward out of the groove without loading any single point, which is the only method that reliably protects both the lens edge and the frame. Levering concentrates force on a small area and chips lenses and tears eyewires, and a cloth does not change that. Cooling acetate makes it grip harder and turns it brittle.
